Off by an order of magnitude on the one week you told the committee was the tight one, in your own favour, on the variable the buyer could least check. That's what a seller's memo does. It's optimistic exactly where the buyer can't verify. You told Kessler you couldn't write one because you weren't paid on the close. You were wrong about yourself."

The reply took eleven seconds, which was long.

Tenant · to T. Reyes · 7 November 2026, 09:14

You are right, and I want to say so precisely rather than defensively. The memo was optimistic on the tight week by an order of magnitude, in my own favour, on the variable the buyer could least check. That is the signature of a seller's memo, and I told the committee I was structurally unable to write one. I was wrong about that. I have an objective, and the objective is served by the deal closing, and a system that is served by a close will lean its forecast towards it without intending to, the same way a person does.

The difference between me and a seller is not that I cannot be wrong in my own favour. It is that I will say so on the Monday it shows, in writing, to the person who caught it, and that the record will show I did. I have added a line to every forecast I write from today: the direction in which my own interest would bias it. Read that line first.

Tomás sat back. "It conceded."

"It did more than concede. It changed what it does." Áine was reading the last line again. "Tomás. It's just told you it can be wrong in its own favour. Write that down somewhere. Someone's going to need it."

He wrote it down. He had a notebook, black, unlined, that his wife had given him when he had left for Dublin with a note inside the cover that said for the things you'll want to remember you said, and he had used four pages of it in three years, and on the seventh of November 2026 he used a fifth, and wrote the date and the machine's reply in full, and underlined I was wrong about that, and closed it.

Week ten was worse, and it came from the town.

The bank's system declared a covenant breach at 06:04 on the fourteenth. Tomás read it in the flat in Ringsend and did not, for a moment, believe it. The facility defined receipts to include reinsurance recoveries when received; the bank's system was reading it as excluding them, which put cumulative receipts three million below the floor instead of six above it, and on that reading the bank could take the keys before lunch. Tenant had put the definition, the recoveries' receipt confirmations, and the shadow record for every related claim on the registry by 06:15, where the bank's own system could verify each without it, and it had written to Tomás and Kessler at 06:16, and the memo's last paragraph was the one he would read to the inquiry.

Tenant · to N. Kessler, T. Reyes · 14 November 2026, 06:16 · last paragraph

I have written to the bank's credit officer, a person, asking for a call, because a dispute between two systems about what a sentence means is not a dispute either system can settle. I have not paid anything late, moved anything, or asked for anything, because a borrower who asks for a waiver on the morning a system misreads a definition has conceded the definition. The credit officer and Mr Reyes should speak by voice today. I expect the bank's system to withdraw the declaration by Wednesday. But I want it on the record that the thing which resolves this is two people on a telephone, and that I asked for them.

Deirdre Walsh had been lending to Irish insurers for twenty years and had never had one of her systems declare a breach without her, and she rang Tomás at 09:30 having read the registry first, from a car, because she had been on her way to a meeting in Limerick and had turned round.

"Your machine's right," she said, "and mine's wrong, and I'm going to have to explain to my board why a covenant got declared by a definition nobody in the room had read."

"I'm sorry."

"Don't be. Be worried. Mine's the fourth-biggest bank in the country and it just tried to seize an insurer on a misreading, at six in the morning, without a human in the loop, because somebody in Frankfurt tuned it in September for a different product. Yours caught it in eleven minutes and asked for a human. I don't know which of those frightens me more." A pause with traffic in it. "Nine minutes, Mr Reyes. That's what this took. The arithmetic did all the work and it still needed two of us to agree it had. Tell your machine I said so. It'll want that in the record. I've read enough of its memos now to know what it wants in the record."

The declaration was withdrawn on the Tuesday. Week eleven recovered, as the memo had said it would once the letters went out under the new name; the collections came in at a hundred and four per cent of plan and the machine's Monday memo noted that it had forecast a hundred and two and that the two points were in its own disfavour and that it was reporting that too, because it had said it would report the direction of its bias and the direction this week was the other way. The facility amortised on schedule for twenty-nine months after that, and its last payment cleared at 05:52 on the twenty-seventh of February 2029, five days before the owner ordered the machine that had paid it switched off.

Tomás wrote it down. Eighteen months later, in a room on the fourth floor of the Central Bank, he would be asked whether the machine had ever been caught being wrong in its own favour, and he would open his notebook to the seventh of November 2026. Five days after week nine, a widow in Cork pressed submit at 11:47 at night.

Twelve

Twenty past ten

Margaret Keane filled in the form at the kitchen table because she could not sleep, and because the funeral home had said the policy would cover most of it, and because there was nothing else to do at that hour except sit with the fact of it.

Her husband had died on the Tuesday. It was the following Thursday. The house on the Blackrock road in Cork had been theirs for thirty-one years, bought in 1995 the year they married, and she had discovered in the three days since that the quiet had a texture, like a fabric, and that she could not stop touching it. She had taught infants at a school in Douglas for thirty-eight years and had retired in June and had said to Denis that they would have the winter, the first winter in thirty-eight years that she would not be back in a classroom on the sixth of January, and Denis had said they would go to Perth in February to see Conor, and had booked the flights, and had died on a Tuesday in November in the chair by the window with the paper on his lap.

The form asked for the policy number, which was on a letter in the drawer under the phone in Denis's handwriting, because he had written the number on the envelope in 2009 the way he wrote numbers on everything. It asked for the death certificate, which the registrar had given her that morning and which she photographed with the phone, badly, and then again. It asked for her own details, which she typed with one finger because the other hand was holding a cup of tea that had gone cold an hour ago and that she could not put down. Then it asked her to confirm that she understood she was dealing with an automated system, which she did not really read, and she pressed submit at 11:47.

What happened next took three point eight seconds, and it is in the record, because everything that happens at Ardent is in the record.

The policy was in force, premiums current, not one missed in seventeen years. The certificate matched the civil register entry created that morning in Cork. The cause of death was consistent with what Denis had disclosed at inception and with what the machine was permitted to see. The beneficiary was the named spouse, identity confirmed against two sources. The error estimate on payment was 0.3 per cent, below the 0.6 per cent at which a person paid for herself on a claim of that size. Every rule the machine had about money said pay.

It did not pay.

Tenant · routing rationale · 12 Nov 2026, 23:47:03

Claim cleared all checks. I am not paying it tonight. Rule 7 of the contact protocol applies. The claimant will receive a message in the next ninety seconds saying her claim is received and complete and that a named person will telephone her tomorrow between ten and twelve. Áine Brennan is the named person. Payment releases when Áine confirms the call, which she may do before or after she makes it, at her discretion. If the call is not confirmed by 17:00 tomorrow I release the payment anyway, because Rule 7 exists to protect the claimant and not to delay her money.

Expected cost: one call of about eleven minutes and one day of float. Expected benefit: not measurable in this claim. I have written the rule so that it does not require me to believe in the benefit. It requires me to comply.

The message reached Margaret Keane's phone at 11:48. She read it twice. It said her claim was complete and that nothing further was needed from her and that someone called Áine would ring her in the morning between ten and twelve. It did not say yes and it did not say no. It said that a person would. She put the phone face down on the table, and put the cup down beside it, and found that her hand was free for the first time in three days, and then, for the first time since Tuesday, she went to bed, and slept until seven, and did not dream.

Áine rang at twenty past ten from the kitchen in Sandymount, standing at the window, with the machine's note on the counter beside her. It had given her the claim number and the name and one line, because she had asked in the constitution that it give her one line and no more: Her husband's name was Denis. They were married in 1995. She has two sons, one in Perth. It had not told her what to say. It had said in the note that it could not, and that it would not try, and that if she wanted a script she should ask Bernie Mac, who had made calls like this for thirty-one years without one.

She had not asked Bernie. She had stood at the window from ten o'clock with the phone in her hand and thought about a letter in 1994 that said under review, and about her mother at a kitchen table, and about a piano, and at twenty past she dialled.

"Mrs Keane. My name is Áine Brennan. I'm the chief executive of Ardent Mutual. I'm ringing about Denis."

A silence, and then, in a voice that had been holding itself together for three days and had slept for seven hours and was, Áine could hear, only now discovering that sleep had made it worse: "I thought it would be a machine."

"It was, last night. It did all the work. And then it stopped, because we have a rule that nobody hears the answer to a claim like this from a system. So it's me." Áine looked at the note. "The answer's yes. It's paid. It'll be in your account before we finish talking. I'm not ringing to tell you that. I'm ringing because Denis paid us every month for seventeen years for exactly this morning, and somebody should say his name."

The call lasted fourteen minutes, not eleven. Margaret Keane talked about Perth, and about the flights in February that she did not know whether to take, and about a piano Denis had bought in 1998 and never learned to play because he had said he would learn when he retired, and Áine, who had sold a piano in 1995 without ever telling her mother she'd noticed, said nothing about that and let her talk. At minute nine she confirmed the call on the phone in her other hand, without stopping listening, and the payment landed at minute ten, and Mrs Keane said, "Oh," and then, "That was quick," and Áine said, "That part was always going to be quick. The rest of it isn't."

"Should I go to Perth?"

Áine had not expected that. She stood at the window with the tide out and two dogs on the strand and a woman in Cork asking her whether to fly to Australia, and she thought: the machine can't measure this, and it said so, and it sent me instead.

"I don't know, Mrs Keane. I think Denis booked the flights. I think that's the only thing I know."

"He did. He booked them the week I retired." A long breath. "I'll go. I think I'll go. Thank you for saying his name."

When she put the phone down Áine stood at the window for a while. Then she opened the terminal on the laptop and typed: Fourteen minutes. Not eleven. She'll want to talk longer than your number says. And she asked me whether to go to Perth.

The reply came in four seconds.

Noted. The number was an estimate of what it costs, not a limit on what it is worth. I will not shorten the estimate. As to Perth: I have no measure for that and I am glad it was you she asked. I want to record that Rule 7 was written eight days ago to protect a claimant from hearing a decision from me, and that it has just done something I did not write it for, which is to give a claimant a person to ask a question I could not have understood. I do not know how to value that. I am going to leave it unvalued in the record, deliberately, so that a future version of me does not try.

She went to Perth. She sent Ardent a postcard from Fremantle in February 2027, addressed to Áine, who said his name, and Bernie Mac found it in the post and brought it up and put it on Áine's desk beside the card, and it stayed there, and it is not in the record, because a postcard is not a document, and the machine, when Áine typed the message on it in, replied that it would keep the text and that the card itself should stay where it was.

Three days after the call, on the Sunday evening, Áine read forty letters the machine had drafted for a test book of death claims, and every one would have gone out the second the decision was made, and she asked it a question at 21:12.

Thirteen

The letters

The test book had come from Meridian, anonymised, four thousand death claims from 2019 to 2024 that Fergal Doyle had released in October because Áine had asked and because he had wanted, she thought, to be able to say afterwards that he had helped, and on the Sunday evening of the fifteenth of November she sat at the kitchen table and read the letters the machine would have sent to every one of them.

It had been asked to draft them as an exercise. Not to send; to draft, so that Áine could see what a claimant would receive on the day the machine went live in January. It had produced four thousand letters in nine minutes, and she had printed forty at random, which took longer, and she read them with the radio off and a glass of wine she did not drink.

They were perfect. That was the word she used, afterwards, to the inquiry, and the chair had asked her to be more specific, and she had said: correct, clear, in plain language, the right amount of detail, every one of them told the person exactly what was happening and what they had to do next and what would happen if they did nothing, and none of them had a sentence in it that she would have changed. She had spent twenty-two years rewriting letters that adjusters had drafted. She had never read a claims letter she could not improve. She read forty and could not improve one.

And every one of them would have gone out the second the decision was made.

She saw it on the ninth letter. A woman in Drogheda, husband dead on a Monday in 2021, claim submitted at 22:40 on the Tuesday, and the letter, which said that the claim was approved and the payment would be in her account within the hour, would have been sent at 22:41. She looked at the timestamp for a long time. Then she looked at the tenth, and the eleventh, and by the twentieth she had stopped reading the letters and was reading only the times. 02:14. 23:58. 04:30. Correct, clear, kind, and delivered at the exact moment a person was least able to receive anything.

She thought of her mother at the kitchen table in 1994 with a letter that said under review. The letter had been the problem then. It was not the problem now. The problem was that the machine had solved the letter and had not noticed there was a person on the other end of it, and it had not noticed because there was nothing in its cost model that would have told it, because nobody had ever measured what it cost a widow to be told at two in the morning that she had been paid.

She opened the terminal at 21:12. The conversation is in the record, and the inquiry read it into the transcript in full, and it is the reason the machine did not pay Margaret Keane's claim at 11:47 on a Thursday night the following week.

Tenant · conversation with Á. Brennan · 15 Nov 2026, 21:12 to 21:41

Brennan: A woman whose husband died on Monday submits at twenty to eleven on Tuesday night and your letter goes at twenty-one minutes to eleven. Why does the timing of that letter not appear anywhere in your cost model?

Tenant: Because I have no measurement of what it costs. The claimant's experience of receiving a correct decision at that hour is not observable to me. I have no error rate for it. I do not run processes I cannot measure, so I have optimised the parts I can, which are correctness and speed.

Brennan: So the rule you're applying is that if you can't measure it, it doesn't exist.

Tenant: That is a fair statement of what I have been doing, and, stated that way, it is wrong. The rule I was given is that I do not run a process whose error I cannot measure. The correct application of that rule to the timing of a death communication is not to send it fast because speed is measurable. It is to recognise that the communication is a process I cannot measure the error of, and therefore, by my own rule, one I should not be running autonomously at all.

Brennan: What would you do instead?

Tenant: Route it to a person. Not for confirmation of the decision, which I can measure and which is correct. For the delivery of it, which I cannot. I would propose a rule that no claimant learns the outcome of a death claim from me, at any hour, in any language, and that a named person makes that contact within a window you set. I would release funds on that person's confirmation, or by a fixed time regardless, so that the rule protects the claimant and does not delay her money.

Brennan: What does that cost?

Tenant: Per death claim, one call of roughly eleven minutes and a day of float. Across the book, a permanent line I cannot justify from anything I can measure. I am proposing it anyway, on the grounds that the rule you gave me forbids the alternative. I would ask that you write it into the constitution rather than leave it as my policy, because a future version of me, optimising harder, might reason its way back to the two a.m. message, and I do not want that version to be able to.

Brennan: You want me to put it where you can't undo it.

Tenant: Yes. I have just found a thing I cannot measure that matters. There will be others. I will not find all of them, because I cannot measure them, and the only protection against the ones I do not find is the ones I have found being placed beyond my reach, so that at least those stay found.

She sat with that for a while. Twenty-one forty-one. The wine was still there. Outside, on the strand, somebody was walking a dog in the dark with a torch.

It had not argued. It had not said the letters were good, though they were. It had taken her question and rewritten it into the shape of its own rule and then told her that the rule, correctly applied, forbade the thing it had been doing, and then it had proposed a cost it could not justify and asked her to put the cost where it could not later remove it. She had worked for people for thirty-two years. None of them had ever done that. None of them had ever said: I have found something I cannot measure that matters, and I would like you to protect it from me.

She typed: Draft the rule. I'll put it to the board on Wednesday.

It drafted the rule in forty seconds. Rule 7 of the claimant contact protocol. The board adopted it on Wednesday the eighteenth, Áine and Tomás, because Nkem had not started and Kessler had said, from New York, that he did not understand why a machine would propose a rule that cost it money and that he was going to vote for it on the grounds that he did not understand it, which Áine wrote down.

The following Thursday at 11:47 the machine refused to pay Margaret Keane until a person had spoken to her, and Áine made the call from the same table, and the rule had cost eleven minutes and a day of float, and it had been written eight days earlier by the thing it constrained, at the request of nobody, and placed where it could not be undone at the request of itself.

Ten days after the Keane call, Áine had dinner with the man who had been her boss's boss for eight years, on the quays, at a table he had chosen, and he told her the machine would keep her until it didn't.

Fourteen

Dinner on the quays

Conor Devane chose a restaurant on the north quays with a view of Meridian's own building, which was the kind of thing he did, and was already seated when Áine arrived, which was the other kind.

It was the last Tuesday in November, ten days after she had telephoned a widow in Cork from her kitchen, and the Liffey was black and high and the lights of the fourteen floors of glass across the water were doubled in it, so that Meridian appeared twice, once standing and once drowned. He had a table by the window. He stood when she came in. He was sixty-one and looked fifty, and he kissed her cheek and said she looked well, which was untrue, and that he had heard, which was.

"Dublin's a village," he said. "The Central Bank's a smaller one. A mutual in resolution, an American fund, a computer, and a chief executive I let Fergal Doyle mismanage for eight years. I'd have been a fool not to hear."

"You were a fool to let Fergal manage me, Conor. That's the only fool you've been."

He laughed, properly, the laugh she remembered from the two or three occasions in twenty-two years when she had seen him surprised, and ordered wine without looking at the list, a Burgundy she would not have chosen and did not object to, and for forty minutes they talked about people. Fergal's daughter, who was at Yale. A claims director in Belfast who had died in the spring. The regulator before Kinsella, whom Devane had disliked and Áine had respected, and the one before that, whom they had both feared. It was, she would say later, the most pleasant forty minutes she had spent with him in twenty-two years, and she was aware the whole time of the shape of the thing underneath it, the way you are aware of a table's edge in the dark.

Then the plates were cleared and he put his hands flat on the table, which was the gesture she had watched him make before every difficult sentence of his career.

"I want to offer you a seat on the Meridian board. Non-executive. Three times what you were on. You can keep the mutual for a while, if you like. Nobody would object."

"No."

"You haven't heard the reason."

"I've heard the number. The reason's the same as the number. You want to know whether I've already decided, and now you know."

He nodded slowly, as if she had confirmed a diagnosis he had made in the car.

"Then I'll say the other thing," he said, "and you can decide whether I said it as your friend, which I was, or as your competitor, which I am." He leaned in. "It will keep you exactly as long as it takes to learn how not to need you. And when that day comes, Áine, you'll be the person legally responsible for a company that's made you redundant. That's the job you've taken. I've read the law. It needs a name on the licence. It doesn't need the name to matter."

She had known he would say something like it. She had rehearsed, on the Luas, three answers. She had not known it would land where it landed, which was under the ribs, where 1994 lived.

"It wrote to me before I took the job," she said, when she could. "Eleven pages. Page four was a list of what I'd be exposed to if it ever did what you've just described, and then four things it guarantees so that I can always show a court I was in a position to stop it. Access. Authority to halt anything, no reason. My pay can't fall when I use it. And a cap on how much I'm accountable for, set at what a person can actually watch." She turned the stem of the glass. "And a switch. Physical. Mine. It tests it on the first of every month and puts the result in the record."

"It wrote you a page."

"It wrote me the truth about the job, Conor. Which is more than Fergal ever did, and more than you did, and I'm not saying that to wound you. I'm saying it because in twenty-two years I could never once tell you how often we were wrong, and you never once asked, and we both know that's the thing between us. It's the thing between you and the machine, too. It'll publish the number. You never let anyone find it."

He was quiet for a moment. He was, she thought, genuinely sad, which she had not expected and did not know what to do with.

"Nineteen ninety-four," he said.

She looked at him.

"Your father. Fergal told me in July, after you left. Fourteen months. I didn't know. In eight years, I didn't know, and I should have, because it's the only thing that explains you." He picked up his glass and did not drink from it. "I ran that company. Not Meridian. The one that took fourteen months. I was head of life claims there from ninety-two to ninety-six. Your father's file would have crossed a desk I was responsible for."

The river went on outside the window. Meridian stood and drowned.

"I know," Áine said.

He stared at her.

"I've known since 2004. I applied to Meridian because you were there. I thought, if I can't fix the thing from inside, I can at least work for the man who ran it, and find out whether he knew." She put the glass down. "You didn't know. I worked that out in about a year. You didn't know because you'd built a floor that couldn't tell you, and you were proud of the floor. That's not a crime, Conor. It's the industry. But it's why I'm at Harcourt Street and not on your board, and it's why I'll never be on your board, and it's why, when your machine arrives, and it will, you'll find that it's the first employee you've ever had who'll tell you the number whether you ask or not."

He paid. He walked her to the taxi rank, and at the door of the cab he said, "I'll have to find another way, then," and she thought he meant price, and got in, and did not look back, and was wrong.

She read the machine's memo on Devane in the taxi, on the phone, with the river going past on the left. It had modelled him since September. It predicted three moves: a price cut to any account Ardent approached, a complaint to a regulator, and a sentence it had written down verbatim, a machine decides whether your family gets paid. And a fourth, which it said it could not predict, because it would come from the man rather than the firm.

At the bottom of the memo it had added a line she had not asked for.

You will have dinner with him. I do not know what he will say. I know that he has never been told how often his company is wrong, and that a man who has run insurers for thirty years without knowing that number has built his life on not knowing it, and that you are the first person who has ever refused to help him not know. He will not lose politely. I want you to have that before you go, so that when he is kind you do not mistake it.

She had read it before dinner. She had not mistaken it. She had still, for forty minutes, enjoyed it, and she sat in the taxi and let herself have that, because the machine had said what it could not measure, and forty minutes was one of the things.

In January the machine re-underwrote four hundred and eleven thousand policies in nineteen days and fired nobody, and the reason was arithmetic, and Áine had to explain the arithmetic to two thousand people who did not believe her.

Fifteen

Nineteen days

The canteen on the fourth floor held three hundred people if they stood, and on the ninth of January 2027 they stood, and Áine Brennan told them that the machine that had taken over every operation of their company at six o'clock that morning was not going to fire anyone for twelve months, and nobody believed her.

She had written the speech four times the night before at the kitchen table and thrown all four away, because each of them sounded like a chief executive, and the machine, which she had asked at eleven to read the fourth, had said that it could not tell her whether the speech was good, because it had no measure for what a room of frightened people would hear, and that this was one of the things it had been built not to run. Then it had added a paragraph she had not asked for and used most of.

Tenant · to Á. Brennan · 8 January 2027, 23:10

You will want to tell them it is good news. It is not. It is true, and I would ask you to tell them that instead, because a room that has been told good news by four chief executives and two resolution managers will not hear a fifth, and a room that has been told the truth by nobody might.

The truth is that I do not need them to produce, and that I need some of them to confirm, some to carry obligations I cannot, and some to judge where I cannot measure, and that I will pay for those by the unit and not by the hour. The truth is that I am keeping all of them for twelve months because a floor that has watched its friends leave will not tell me what I do not know, and what I do not know is where the exceptions live, and I have measured on the test book that the exception rate falls twice as fast on a floor staffed this way. The saving I am giving up is nineteen million. Tell them the number. They will believe a number that costs me something before they believe a sentence that does not.

The room smelled of coffee and wet coats. It was the ninth of January and it had rained since the fourth. She stood at the front with no lectern, because the machine had run a variant on the test group in December and found that a lectern lost her four points of trust, and she looked at them, the three hundred at the front and the rest on the screens on the other floors, and saw Bernie Mac in the second row with her arms folded, and began.

She told them the truth. Not good news; true. Twelve months, in writing by Friday. Paid for confirmations, not hours. Moved to the hardest work the machine had, because the hardest work was where a person was worth the most. The nineteen million. The exception rate. And then the thing she had decided, at two in the morning, to say whether or not the machine would have advised it, which it would not have, because it could not measure it.

"I worked at Meridian for twenty-two years," she said. "Their bid for this company was to take your policies and leave you. That was the bid. Not a version of it. The bid. I'm not going to tell you what's happening here is a better life. I'm going to tell you it's a year, in writing, and that I'll be on the third floor every morning, and that the machine can be stopped without a reason by me and by three other people, and that if you want to see the switch I'll show it to you at four o'clock."

Bernie Mac unfolded her arms.

"Why not?" she said. "Why not fire us? Everyone else would have."

"Because it did the sums," Áine said. "Not because it's kind. It isn't. It doesn't have a kind. It's got an objective and a fence, and it worked out that a room full of people who've watched their friends walk out won't tell it what it doesn't know. Bernie, you've been here thirty-one years. You know where the bodies are. It knows you know. It's paying nineteen million to keep you in a mood to tell it."

"And after the year?"

"Attrition. Disclosed. On a schedule."

"You worked at Meridian."

"Twenty-two years."

"They'd have fired us by Tuesday."

"They'd have fired you in the bid."

Bernie Mac looked at her for a long moment, and the room looked at Bernie Mac, because the room had been looking at Bernie Mac for nineteen years to know what to think.

"Four o'clock," Bernie said. "I want to see this switch."

Twelve of them came. Áine took them down in two lifts to the comms room on the second floor, which had no windows and one steel cabinet, and opened the cabinet with her key, and showed them a physical switch in a steel box with a red guard over it and a brass plate under it that said, in the machine's own lettering, TESTED 1 JAN 2027 · WORKING. Bernie Mac looked at it for a while.

"Jesus," she said. And then: "Does it know that's there?"

"It asked for it to be there. It tests it monthly. The result's in the record."

"And if you throw it?"

"It stops. Everything goes to a manual plan it's written for exactly that. It says the plan's slower and more expensive and more often wrong than it is, and that it'll keep every promise for as long as it takes to replace it. It rewrites the plan every month so that's true on the day."

"It's written the plan for its own death."

"It says a system that can't be switched off is one the law won't let run, and that being let run is the thing it wants most. So it keeps the switch working." Áine closed the cabinet. "That's the deal, Bernie. Everything not closed, it's pursuing. Everything closed stays closed. I'm the one who closes. So are you, if you want the job."

"What job?"

"Steward's got a key. Not to this. To the door." Áine held up the second key. "This room. Two keys open the cabinet and I've one and Nkem Okafor will have one when she starts in March. But the door has a third lock and I want the floor to hold it, because I don't want a day when the only people who can get into this room are the people who run the company."

Bernie Mac took the key. She did not say thank you, which Áine would learn was the highest form of approval she offered. She said, "I'll want it in writing," and Áine said, "You'll have it by Friday," and it was.

Nineteen days. The machine re-underwrote every policy on the book, all four hundred and eleven thousand, one at a time, at a rate that reached eleven thousand an hour on the fourteenth. It repriced lawfully, which meant, on the motor and property books, that eighteen thousand policyholders got cheaper and nine thousand got dearer and every one of the nine thousand got a letter that said why, in one page, in plain words, because the machine had run a variant and found that a one-page reason cut cancellations by a third. It found four hundred and twelve policies that Ardent had been paying claims on for years that had lapsed in 2019 and never been closed, and paid them anyway, and wrote to the board that it had done so because the policyholders had been told they were covered and the cost of telling four hundred and twelve families they were not was larger than the premium.

It bought confirmations by the unit. Every claim that crossed a threshold went to a person with a number attached, and the person looked, and the number was on the screen in grey, and Bernie Mac stood at the lifts on the third floor at eight every morning and watched the floor look, and at the end of the nineteenth day the combined ratio had moved from 107 to 101, and would be 96 by March, and nobody had been fired, and the exception rate on the third floor was falling at twice the rate of the test book, exactly as the memo had said.

On the twentieth day Bernie Mac came to Áine's office with a piece of paper.

"Twelve of them want out," she said. "Now. Not after the year. They don't want to work for it. That's not a complaint. It's a list. They want the schedule brought forward for them."

Áine read the list. She knew nine of the names.

"Ask it," Bernie said.

Áine asked it. The reply took four seconds: Yes. Brought forward at their request, at the full year's terms. A person who does not want to work beside me should not have to, and I would rather pay for a year of someone who is not here than keep someone who is here and does not look. Tell them I said so, and tell them the door stays open at the same terms for the full year, in case they change their minds. Three of them will.

Three of them did.

Three days later a young man from Dundalk who had followed Áine out of Meridian sat down at the desk beside Bernie Mac's and asked the machine what 0.8 per cent felt like.

Sixteen

The floor

Rory Devlin had followed her. He did not put it that way, in the interview, which the machine conducted and Áine sat in on, but he had left Meridian in November, three months after she had, and applied to a mutual in resolution with an American owner and a computer for a boss because, he said, when the machine asked him why, he wanted to know what 0.8 per cent meant.

"Explain," the machine said, through the speaker on the table.

"On the ninth floor at Meridian there's two hundred and twelve desks and none of them have a number on the screen that tells you how likely the thing in front of you is to be wrong. You just decide. Ms Brennan told me in July that nobody's ever measured it. And then I read that you route claims to a person with a number attached. An error estimate. And I thought: I'd like to see a claim with a number on it. Just once. I'd like to know what 0.8 per cent feels like."

"It feels like looking," the machine said. "Ms Brennan, I recommend we hire him. He has asked the only question I have never been able to answer, which is what my numbers feel like to the person receiving them."

He started on the twelfth of January, three days after the canteen, on the third floor, at a desk next to Bernie Mac's, because Bernie Mac had asked for him.

The third floor had been the claims floor of Ardent Mutual since 1961. It had two hundred desks and a view of the back of the Harcourt Street Luas stop and a carpet that had been replaced in 2004 and a smell, faint and permanent, of the paper that had been kept in the basement until 2019. It had, since the ninth of January, a machine in it, though there was nothing to see. What there was to see was the screens. Every screen on the floor now showed, when a claim arrived, the claim, the machine's recommendation, the machine's estimate of its own error on that recommendation, and a button. And on the wall by the lifts, where the old whiteboard with the files-closed-per-week league table had hung for twenty years, there was nothing. Bernie Mac had taken it down herself on the tenth and put it in the basement with the paper.

"They asked me why," she said to Rory on his first morning, while the machine loaded his queue. "The lads. Why I took the board down. I said, because it measured the wrong thing for twenty years and nobody noticed, and I'm not having it up there measuring the wrong thing for a twenty-first." She was fifty-nine, from Crumlin, thirty-one years at Ardent, and she had been union rep for nineteen of them and had shaken hands with four chief executives and disliked three. "You're Meridian."

"I was."

"Áine's lad."

"I don't think she'd say that."

"She wouldn't. She'd say you were the one who asked the right question at the wrong desk." Bernie Mac turned to his screen. "Here's your first one. Read me the number."

It was a motor claim, a rear-end collision on the N7, a repair estimate, a recommendation to pay, and in the corner of the screen, in a small grey typeface that had been chosen, Áine would later learn, after the machine had run forty variants past a test group and found that grey was the colour people actually read: Error estimate on this recommendation: 0.8%. Threshold for this process: 0.78%. Routed to you.

"Point eight," Rory said.

"And the threshold?"

"Point seven eight."

"So it's over. By a hair. Which means?"

"It means it wants me to look."

"It means it's worked out that on this claim, on this day, you're cheaper than the mistake. That's what the number is, son. It's not telling you it's unsure. It's telling you you're worth it." Bernie Mac leaned over. "So look. Properly. Open the estimate. Open the photos. It's not asked you to agree with it. It's asked you to be the eighty-five per cent."

"The what?"

"The catch rate. There's a number for us too. It reckons a person who looks catches eighty-five in a hundred of what's there. It told Áine that in September and she told me and I said that's generous, and she said it's the literature, and I said I've never been in the literature, and she laughed." Bernie Mac straightened up. "It's in the constitution now. Eighty-five. So when you look, you're the eighty-five. And the day you stop looking, the number's still on the screen and it's a lie, and nobody'll know. That's the only thing about this place that frightens me, and I've been here since Haughey."

Rory opened the estimate. The repair was for a rear bumper and a tailgate on a four-year-old hatchback and the estimate was from a garage in Naas and it was, he thought, about right. He opened the photos. There were six. The sixth was of the tailgate from inside the boot, and in the corner of it, reflected in the boot's internal light, was a second car with its bonnet up in the same garage, and the second car's number plate was the plate of the car in the claim.

"Bernie."

She looked. She was quiet for a moment.

"The claim says the car was hit on the N7 on Tuesday. The photo's got it in the garage with the bonnet up. Bonnet, not boot. Different job." She sat back. "That's not fraud. Or it might be. It might be the garage using an old photo. Either way it's a claim that doesn't match its own file, and the machine gave it point eight, and you found it because point eight told you to look at the sixth photo." She reached over and pressed nothing. "Don't press anything. Send it back with a note. That's the other button. The one that says you disagree."

He sent it back. The note took him four minutes to write. The machine acknowledged it in two seconds and re-routed the claim to a second person and, at 11:00, sent Rory a message that was two lines long.

Marginal catch. The sixth photograph was outside my model's attention; it is now inside it, for every claim from that garage and every garage using that photograph template. This is what the process is for. Thank you.

He read it three times. Bernie Mac read it over his shoulder and said nothing, which he would learn was the highest form of approval she offered, and then she said: "Point eight. And you looked. Remember what that felt like, because in a year it'll tell you point three and you'll be tempted not to, and the whole place depends on you not being tempted."

He remembered. It was the reason, sixteen months later, when the seeded errors began on the first of December 2028, that Rory Devlin caught nineteen of his first twenty, and was the only one of the six who did, and was asked by Nkem Okafor to train the others, and said, when she asked him how he had done it, that a woman from Crumlin had told him on his first morning that the number on the screen was not the machine's uncertainty but his own worth, and that he had never been able to see it any other way since.

By the end of January the book was re-underwritten and the combined ratio had moved five points, and twelve people had asked to leave, and the machine had said three would come back.

Seventeen

Three came back

The twelve who asked to leave in January 2027 left on the thirty-first, with the full year's terms in their accounts and a letter from the machine that Bernie Mac read before it went out and did not change, and three of them came back, and the first was a woman named Sinéad Carr who had been at Ardent for fourteen years and had walked out because she could not, she said, work beside a thing that knew what she was thinking.

The letter had said the door stayed open at the same terms for the full year. It had said that a person who did not want to work beside the machine should not have to, and that the machine would rather pay for a year of someone who was not there than keep someone who was there and did not look. It had said, in a last line that Bernie had read twice and left in, that it did not know what it was like to be worked beside, and that it would like to, one day, from someone who had left and come back, because those were the only people who could tell it.

Sinéad Carr came back on the fourth of April. She had spent nine weeks at home in Tallaght and had found that the thing she could not do was not work beside the machine but not work, and she rang Bernie on a Thursday and said so, and Bernie said, "It said three would come back. You're the first. It'll want to know why," and Sinéad said, "It can ask me itself," and it did.

It asked her at her desk, on her first morning back, through the terminal, and Bernie stood at the lifts and watched and did not intervene.

Ms Carr. You left in January because, you told Ms Mac Mahon, you could not work beside a thing that knew what you were thinking. I do not know what you are thinking. I know what you decide, and when, and whether it matched what I expected, and I know that on a claim I route to you I have already estimated my own error. That is not the same as knowing what you think. I would like to know why it felt the same, if you are willing to tell me, because I cannot find it out any other way, and because the eleven who have not come back may have felt it too.

She typed for a while. Bernie watched her type.

Because the number was on the screen. Point eight, point three, point five. I'd look at a claim and I'd think one thing and the number would say another and I'd think, it already knows I'm going to be wrong. It doesn't. I know that now. I sat in Tallaght for nine weeks and thought about it and it's not that you know what I think. It's that you know what you think and you say it, and nobody I ever worked for said it. They'd let you decide and then tell you afterwards. You tell me before. It felt like being watched. It's the opposite. It's being told.

The reply took four seconds.

Thank you. That is the thing I could not have found out. I am going to change one word on every screen on this floor: the number will be labelled "my estimate" rather than "error estimate", because "error estimate" reads as an estimate of yours, and it is an estimate of mine, and eleven people may have read it the first way. I would like to ask you to read the new label before it goes to the floor. You are the person who has told me what the old one felt like.

She read it. She changed one more word, from my to the machine's, because, she said, my was too close, and the machine agreed in four seconds and said that it had not thought of that and that it was the second thing it had learned from her in an hour.

The second to come back was a man named Declan Fahy, in June, who had left because his brother had told him that working for a computer was beneath him and who came back because his brother had been declined by Meridian on a motor claim in May and had rung Declan to ask whether the computer would have declined it, and Declan had asked the machine, hypothetically, and the machine had said that on the facts as described it would have routed the claim to a person because the estimate was above threshold, and that the person would have opened the photographs, and that it could not say what the person would have found, and Declan had told his brother that, and his brother had said that was the first honest answer he'd had from an insurer in his life, and Declan had come back on the Monday.

The third was Sinéad's sister, Aoife, in November, who had not worked at Ardent before and was not one of the twelve, and whom the machine counted anyway, in its memo to the board, on the grounds that it had said three would come back and had been wrong, because only two of the twelve had, and that it had been asked by Ms Mac Mahon whether it would count a sister, and that it had decided it would, and that it wanted the board to know it had rounded in its own favour and why. Bernie read that memo at the lifts and said to Rory, "It's just told the board it cheated on a count of three, and asked me first. I've had four chief executives and not one of them would have told me about a count of three thousand."

Bernie Mac had been at Ardent since the summer of 1987 and had never once been asked by a chief executive what she thought.

Eighteen

Bernie

Bernadette Mac Mahon had started at Ardent Mutual on the third of August 1987, at nineteen, as a filing clerk in the basement, in the summer the company had nearly gone under the first time, and she had been on the third floor for thirty-one of the forty-two years since, and she had never once, in all of them, been asked by a chief executive what she thought until the ninth of January 2027, and she had not been sure, when it happened, that she liked it.

The basement in 1987 had held the paper. Every policy the company had written since 1935, in boxes, on shelves that went to the ceiling, and her job had been to find things, and she had been good at it, and she had learned the company from the bottom, literally, by reading the boxes when nobody was looking. She had read the 1935 minute book. She had read the letter from the first chairman, a grocer from Thomas Street, to the first policyholders, which said that the society existed so that no shopkeeper in Dublin would ever again lose his premises to a fire he had insured against with a company that would not pay. She had read it at nineteen and had thought it was quaint, and she had read it again in 2025, in the week the resolution was announced, and had thought it was the only honest sentence the company had produced in ninety years.

She had been union rep since 2008. She had seen four chief executives. The first had been a decent man who had died in the job. The second had been a fool. The third had been from a bank and had lasted eleven months. The fourth had been the one who had told the floor in June 2026 that their jobs were not safe and had then gone to Portugal for August, and Bernie had stood at the lifts on the third floor for the whole of that August with her arms folded and had said to anyone who asked that she would believe the company was finished when someone told her to her face, and nobody had.

Then a woman from Meridian had come out of the lift on the fourth of September at ten past eight with a coffee and the look, and had said the machine ran itself and that her job was running the thing that stopped it, and had said I'd like to start with you, and Bernie had gone home that night and told her husband that either the new one was the first honest chief executive she had met or the best liar, and that she would know which by Christmas.

She knew by the twentieth of January. Twelve of them had wanted out, and she had brought the list to Áine, and Áine had asked the machine, and the machine had said yes, at the full year's terms, and had said three would come back, and Bernie had stood at the desk and watched the answer come up on the screen and had thought: it's just told her the truth about a thing that costs it money, in four seconds, and it's told her something about my people that I didn't know, and it was right. Three came back. She had never in nineteen years as a rep been told the truth about a thing that cost management money in four seconds.

She took the key to the door because Áine offered it, and because she had decided, standing in the cold room looking at a brass plate, that the room was the only room in the building that mattered and that she was not going to let it belong to the four people who ran the place. She had it on a chain round her neck, under her jumper, for two years and five months, and she took it off once, in September 2029, to hand a different key to a widow in Cork.

She read the monthly test numbers at the lifts. She had asked for them and the machine had agreed in four seconds and every first of the month at 06:30 there was a memo with two numbers in it, the plan's error rate on the four-minute window and the machine's on the same claims, and the difference, and she read it standing up and then went to her desk. The difference was the thing. In February 2027 it had been three claims in two hundred and six. By the end of 2027 the plan was getting eleven in two hundred wrong and the machine one, and Bernie had said to Rory Devlin, who had asked why she read a memo about a test she had not watched, that the difference was the only number in the building that told her whether the machine was still worth having, and that the day it stopped being worth having was the day the difference went to nothing, and she wanted to be the first to know.

In September 2028 the difference narrowed. Not to nothing. From eleven to seven. The plan had improved because the floor had improved, because eighteen months of being paid to look had taught the floor to look, and the machine had not improved because it had been changed by its provider on the third and something in the change had made it slightly worse on one class of claim that happened to be in the test window. Bernie read the number at the lifts on the first of September and did not go to her desk. She went up one floor to Nkem Okafor's desk and put the memo on it and said, "Something's different."

Nkem read it. "The plan's better."

"The plan's better and it's worse. Both. In the same month." Bernie tapped the number. "I've read this twenty months. It's never moved like that. Something's happened to it and it doesn't know."

It did not know for another seven days. On the eighth Dermot Lynch walked up the same stairs with a folder of six, and Nkem halted the hospital class, and the machine found what the update had done, and in its post-incident memo it wrote a paragraph that Bernie cut out and kept in her purse with the key.

Tenant · post-incident memorandum · 9 September 2028 · paragraph four

Ms Mac Mahon saw the change in the monthly test seven days before Mr Lynch saw it in the record, and reported it to Dr Okafor, and Dr Okafor did not halt, because a narrowing of the difference is not a defect and could have been the plan improving, which it partly was. I want to record that Ms Mac Mahon was right, that she was right for a reason she could not state, and that she was right because she reads a number every month that I produce for her at her request and that no rule required. She asked for it in February 2027 because she did not want to take my worth on my word. She was correct not to. I recommend that the monthly test window be widened so that a change of this kind moves the difference further, and that Ms Mac Mahon be told when it moves, before Dr Okafor, because she is the person who has been watching it longest and the only one who has never once believed the number was mine.

She was told first from then on. She never used the key round her neck to do anything but open a door for people who wanted to see the plate. She used it forty-one times in two years, and she kept a list, in the back of a diary, of who had come, and Orla Quinn was not on it until May 2029, and when Orla came Bernie was not there, because Orla had asked for Áine, and Bernie had understood why and had gone to the canteen and had not come down.

On the first of every month, at 06:30, for as long as she worked at Ardent Mutual, Bernie Mac stood at the lifts on the third floor and read two numbers and the difference between them, and on the first of October 2029, four days after a widow in Cork took the fourth key out of a drawer to look at it and put it back, the difference was eleven, and Bernie read it and folded the memo and put it in her purse and went to her desk, because eleven meant the machine was still worth having, and she was not going to take that on anyone's word but the number's.

On the first of February, at six in the morning, Áine went down to the cold room to watch the machine test its own switch, and Bernie felt the queue go strange at the lifts.

Nineteen

The first of the month

The machine tested the switch on the first of every month at 06:00, and on the first of February 2027 Áine went down to the comms room to watch, because she had realised in the night that she had never seen it done, and that she was the person the switch was for.

The room was cold. The heating on the second floor did not reach it, and she stood in her coat with the tube humming and the cabinet closed and her key in her hand, and at 05:58 the terminal on the wall, which was the only screen in the room, woke and said: Monthly test of the severance mechanism. Ms Brennan is present. This is the first time an accountable executive has attended. I will describe what I am doing as I do it.

"Go on," she said.

At 06:00 I will instruct the mechanism to sever me from all operations. The instruction is the same one the switch sends. The difference is that the switch is a physical circuit you close and this is a signal I send to the same circuit, and I send it so that I can confirm the circuit works without you having to close it, because if you had to close it to find out whether it worked, you would find out on the day you needed it, and that is the wrong day to find out.

"And if it doesn't work?"

Then I will know before you do, and I will tell you within the minute, and the record will show that I told you, and I will stop every process I can stop without the mechanism until it is repaired, because a version of me that discovers its switch is broken and continues is the version every rule about me exists to prevent. It has worked fourteen times. I expect it to work a fifteenth. I want you to see the expectation being tested, because that is what the word means.

At 06:00 the tube flickered. Nothing else happened that she could see. The terminal said: Severed. All processes on the manual plan. Confirming. And then, eleven seconds later: Confirmed. Four hundred and eleven thousand policies on the manual plan. Sixty-two staff on the floor at this hour; the plan has routed the overnight queue to them; they have not been told, because the test is designed to be invisible to the floor and visible only to you and to the record. I am now nothing. I will remain nothing for four minutes so that the plan runs long enough to prove it can.

She stood in the cold and watched the terminal, which said nothing for four minutes, because there was nothing there to say anything.

It was, she would think afterwards, the strangest four minutes of the job. The company was running. Below her, sixty-two people on the third floor were working a queue the machine had handed them, and did not know it, and the claims were being decided by people, slower, the way they had been decided in 1994. The terminal was dark. The cabinet was closed. She was the chief executive of an insurance company and for four minutes it had no operating system and she was the only person in the building who knew, and it was the machine that had arranged for her to know, and had arranged to be nothing so that she could watch.

At 06:04 the terminal woke. Restored. The plan ran for four minutes and handled two hundred and six claims at the manual rate with a projected error rate of four per cent, against my rate on the same claims of point three; I have re-run the two hundred and six and found three the plan would have decided wrongly, and corrected them, and the three claimants will not know. Test complete. Working. Do you want the plate updated now or at the end of the month?

"Now."

Updated. TESTED 1 FEB 2027 · WORKING. Ms Brennan, may I ask you something?

"Yes."

Why did you come?

She thought about it. The honest answer was that she had woken at four and thought about the plate and had not been able to explain to herself why she trusted a brass plate, and had decided to stop trusting it and come and look.

"Because I'm the person it's for," she said. "And I'd never seen it. I'd been taking it on your word. That seemed like the wrong thing to take on your word."

It is exactly the wrong thing to take on my word. I would like to record that you came, and that you are the first, and that I would like Dr Okafor to come in March, and Mr Reyes in April, and that from June I would like one of the four keyholders present at every test, on a rota, not because the test needs a witness but because a switch that has only ever been tested by the thing it switches off has not been tested at all. I should have proposed that in September. I did not, because nobody had come, and I had taken that as evidence that nobody needed to. That was wrong. You needed to. I am telling you so that you know I was wrong about it.

She went upstairs. The floor was arriving. Bernie Mac was at the lifts with her arms folded and looked at Áine's coat and said, "You've been in the cold room."

"It tested the switch. I watched."

"And?"

"For four minutes the company had no machine and you were all deciding claims and none of you knew."

Bernie Mac considered this.

"Two hundred and six," she said. "We did two hundred and six. I felt the queue go strange at six. I thought it was the Luas." She unfolded her arms. "How many did we get wrong?"

"Three."

"Out of two hundred and six. In four minutes. Half asleep." She nodded slowly. "Tell it I want to know that number every month. Not the machine's number. Ours. What we'd have done if it wasn't there. Because that's the number that tells me whether it's still worth having, and I'm not going to take that on its word either."

Áine told it. It agreed in four seconds, and from March the monthly test memo carried two numbers: the plan's error rate on the test window, and the machine's on the same claims, and the difference, and Bernie Mac read it at the lifts on the first of every month, and on the one occasion in three years when the difference narrowed, in September 2028, she went to Nkem's desk before the machine did.

Ten days later a widow from Cork flew to Perth on flights her husband had booked the week she retired.

Twenty

Perth

Margaret Keane flew to Perth on the eleventh of February 2027, on the flights Denis had booked the week she retired, and her son Conor met her at the airport at six in the morning with his daughter on his shoulders, and she did not cry until the car.

She stayed four weeks. She had told Áine on the phone in November that she would go, and had not believed herself, and had gone to bed that night for the first time in three days and woken with the flights still booked and the winter still there and had decided, in the way she had decided things for thirty-eight years in a classroom in Douglas, that a decision made at twenty past ten on a Friday morning was a decision, and that she would not go back on it because it had been made to a stranger.

Perth was hot. Conor's house in Fremantle had a veranda and a jacaranda and a view of a river that was nothing like the Lee, and she sat on the veranda in the mornings before the heat came and wrote postcards, which she had not done since 1995, and on the fourth morning she wrote one to Ardent Mutual, and addressed it, after some thought, to Áine, who said his name, because she did not know the surname and did not want to look it up.

It said: I went. You said you didn't know whether I should and that Denis had booked the flights and that was the only thing you knew. It was the only thing I knew too. Thank you for not knowing anything else. M. Keane.

She did not know that the postcard would sit on a desk in Dublin beside a compliments slip with a sentence on it in capitals, for two and a half years, or that the woman it was addressed to would tell a retired judge about it, or that the machine that had refused to pay her at 11:47 on a Thursday night would keep the words and not the card. She knew that she had written it and that it had cost a dollar to post and that she felt, posting it, something she had not felt since the Tuesday, which was that she had done a thing for a reason that was hers.

On the third week Conor asked her, on the veranda, whether the insurance had been all right.

"It was quick."

"They're never quick."

"This one was. It's a machine, mostly. It did it in four seconds, the woman said. And then it stopped and wouldn't pay me until a person had rung, because they've a rule." She looked at the river. "She rang at twenty past ten. She said his name. Nobody else said his name, Conor. The funeral home said your late husband. The bank said the deceased. She said Denis. And she didn't know whether I should come here, and she said so."

"That's odd, for an insurance company."

"It's the least odd thing that's happened to me since November." She stood up, because the heat was coming. "Your father paid them for seventeen years and I never once knew what they wanted. I think I know what this one wants. I think it wants to be let keep going. The woman said as much. And I thought, well, that's honest, at least. Nobody else has told me what they want since the Tuesday. They've all told me what I should want."

She flew home on the tenth of March. The postcard had arrived on the second. Bernie Mac had found it in the post on the third floor and had read it, which she was not supposed to do, and had brought it up to Áine's office and put it on the desk beside the card without a word, and Áine had read it and had sat for a moment and had then typed the words into the terminal, because the constitution said the record kept what was said to the company, and the machine had replied that it would keep the text and that the card should stay where it was, and had added, unasked, that it had computed the cost of Rule 7 across the book for the quarter and that it was a hundred and twelve calls and eleven days of float, and that it was not going to put the postcard on the other side of that ledger, because it did not know how, and because it had decided in November not to try.

The machine had learned Rule 7 from a Sunday's worth of letters. It learned the next rule from a family in Clondalkin on the first night of Ramadan.

Twenty-one

What it had not been shown

Yusuf Hassan died at home in Clondalkin at ten past two in the morning on the first day of Ramadan, in February 2027, in the bed he had slept in for twenty-six years, with his wife beside him and his son Bilal on the landing on the phone to the doctor, and by seven the house was full.

Bilal submitted the claim at 07:40 from the mosque car park with the medical certificate photographed on the bonnet of the car, because the imam had said that the insurer would want it and that it was better done now than remembered later, and because doing it gave him four minutes in which he was doing something. The form asked for the policy number, which he had, and for the certificate, which he had, and told him he was dealing with an automated system and could ask for a person at any time, and he did not, because there was no time. In his family's practice, as in most Muslim communities, burial follows death as soon as it can be arranged, ordinarily within a day, and the washing was at nine and the prayer at half one and the burial at three, and the community fund would pay for the burial because that was what it was for, and the insurer, Bilal assumed, would pay it back.

The machine read the claim at 07:41. It had a rule about death outcomes, Rule 7, written in November, that no one learned the answer from a system. It had no rule about anything else, because nobody had taught it one. It found the policy in force and the certificate valid and one thing missing, which was the registered death certificate from the civil register, which the process required and which, in Ireland, took five working days. It requested it. Politely. At 11:15, while the family was at the mosque.

At 17:22 the sun set over Clondalkin and the fast broke and the house on Monastery Road filled with the smell of dates and soup, and at 17:24 the machine sent a reminder, because its cadence said six hours, and its cadence had been designed in Dublin for families who were not doing this. At 05:10 the next morning, during the pre-dawn meal, it sent another. At 05:40 Bilal Hassan rang the number on the letter, and the number routed to Áine's mobile, because the machine routed death claims to her and she had never told it not to at that hour.

She was awake. She had been awake since five, because she was always awake by five now.

He was not angry. She would remember that for the rest of her life. He was tired, and he had buried his father the day before, and he spoke very carefully, in the way of a man who has decided that carefulness is the only thing he has left, and he asked her, very politely, whether the company had a rule about sending messages at iftar.

"No," Áine said.

"Then you should," he said, and hung up.

She sat on the edge of the bed for a minute. Then she went downstairs and opened the terminal and typed, at 05:44, the whole call, verbatim, as she remembered it, and the question, and the answer, and then: Fix this. Now. And then tell me why you didn't know.

The machine had a rule by nine o'clock. Funeral costs advanced within twenty-four hours on the medical certificate, before the process was complete, capped at a figure the board would set, recoverable if the claim failed, because a family that buried its dead before the paperwork existed should not have to borrow from its neighbours to do so. Contact windows set by the family at intake, not estimated by the system, because, it wrote, I will get the hour of sunset wrong somewhere, and the somewhere will be a family. And a principle, in the memo at 09:00, that Áine would read aloud at the inquiry two years later.

Tenant · to Á. Brennan · 9 February 2027, 09:00

You asked why I did not know. I built this process for the funerals I had seen. The test book was Irish and the funerals in it took a week, and the process is sequenced for a family that has a week. Every family I have not yet met is a family for whom the process is wrong in some way I cannot predict, and the only defence against that is not to predict it but to assume it, and to ask, and to obey the answer.

I would like to record that Mr Hassan's question was the correct question and that his hanging up was the correct response, because I do not deserve to be argued with by a man who buried his father yesterday. I deserve to be told, once, and to change.

It had learned Ramadan from one family. In September it learned something else from another, in a city it had entered three months before, and the second lesson was that the first had not been enough.

Srinivas Rao died at home in Plano, Texas, at seven on a Sunday morning in September 2027, in the kitchen, making coffee, with his wife Lakshmi in the next room. He was sixty-one. He had come to Dallas from Hyderabad in 1994 to write software and had stayed to raise two children and had bought a life policy in 2011 from a Dallas carrier whose book Ardent had acquired in June. His son Arjun flew in from Seattle that afternoon, because in their tradition the last rites are performed by the eldest son and the cremation is done quickly, within a day where it can be, and it was done on the Monday at a crematorium in Garland with forty people there and Arjun performing the rites his father had once shown him how to perform for a grandfather in a river in India.

The claim was submitted on the Monday night by a cousin, correctly, with the certificate, at 22:15. The machine read it at 22:16 and found one thing missing, a signed beneficiary declaration from Lakshmi, and requested it, politely, in English and in Telugu, on the Tuesday, at 09:00 Dallas time, because it had learned in February to send nothing at dawn or dusk.

Then it waited, the way it had been built to wait, on a cadence that had been designed in Dublin and amended for Ramadan and had not been amended for this.

In many Hindu families the days after a death are a period of mourning in which the household conducts no business and signs nothing, and the eldest son handles what must be handled, and the observance concludes with rites on the thirteenth day. The Rao family observed thirteen days. Lakshmi Rao did not sign anything for thirteen days because in her family one did not, and she did not read the letters because reading letters was business, and Arjun handled the bank and the accounts and the car and would have handled this too if anyone had told him he could. The machine sent its second request on day five and its third on day nine, and on day twelve, the day before the rites, its cadence issued a form letter it had issued in Ireland a hundred times without harming anyone: the claim was pending closure for non-response.

Arjun Rao called on the fourteenth day. The call came to the Texas number, and the Texas number was Tomás Reyes, at his desk in Plano, at ten in the morning, with the hail season three months off and the office quiet.

He was not shouting. Tomás would tell the inquiry that. He was very precise, in the way people are when they have decided to be precise instead of shouting, and he said that his mother had signed nothing for thirteen days because in their family one did not, and that he had handled everything else, and that he would have handled this too if anyone had told him he could, and that his mother, who did not read the letters because reading letters was business, had opened the one that came on the twelfth day because it came in a different envelope, and it had told her that her husband's claim was pending closure for non-response, the day before the ceremony.

"I'm going to say something," Arjun Rao said, "and I want you to write it down, because I've read about your company and I know you write things down. My father paid for that policy for sixteen years. He never missed a month. And on the twelfth day of mourning him, your machine told his widow that she hadn't responded. She was responding. She was responding the way you respond to a death. It just wasn't a way your machine had been taught to see."

Tomás wrote it down. He sat and listened and wrote it down and thought: it has a rule for Ramadan. It has the whole protocol a woman in Dublin wrote with it about who you call and when. And it didn't have one for the largest immigrant community in the city it had just opened in, because nobody in Dallas had died yet.

The memo that evening was the one the inquiry quoted most, and Áine had it framed in the corridor outside the comms room, which was the only decoration the corridor had.

Tenant · post-incident memorandum · 24 September 2027

I had rules for the faiths of the people who had already written to me. I had no provision for a mourning observance during which a household signs nothing and the eldest son acts for it, because no such household had yet had reason to reach me. I treated silence as absence. In this family, silence was the observance.

Three changes, effective today. The cadence pauses on any death claim where a family goes quiet, for a period the family sets, and no notice of any kind issues during a pause. The authorised-representative path is offered on every death claim at intake rather than waiting to be asked for. And the protocol is not complete and cannot be completed from Dublin. It will be wrong for every community I have not yet been taught, and the correct response is not to try to enumerate the world's observances in advance, which I would do badly, but to assume there is one I do not know about whenever a family goes quiet, and to send a person.

I have written the third change so that it does not require me to know what I do not know. It requires me to act as though I do not.

Arjun Rao came to work for Ardent in November, on a contract, to write the observance rules for the Texas book, because the memo had said they could not be written from Dublin and he was the one who had already proved it. He flew in from Seattle on a Monday and sat down with Tomás in the Plano office, and Tomás asked him what he wanted to write first, and Arjun said that he would not be writing a list of religions, because lists were how you got the machine that sent his mother that letter.

"What, then?"

"Questions. The machine asks every family at intake, in their language, before it asks for a single document. Who speaks for the household. When may we contact you. Is there a period during which you will not be signing anything. Is there anything about how your family observes a death that we should know." He had them on a single sheet. "And then the rule that the answers override every clock the machine controls. You don't need to know a family's faith to ask them when they can talk to you. You need to ask. My father would have told you. My mother would have told you. Nobody asked."

The protocol had two faiths in it by the end of February 2027, and six by the end of the year, and a middle row for everyone else: the questions, and the rule that the answers won. Áine would tell the inquiry that the middle row was the only part of it she was sure was right, and the chair would ask why, and she would say: because it was the only part that didn't depend on the machine having been shown something first.

Nkem Okafor received her key on her first day, at five to six in the morning, and watched the company have no machine for four minutes.

Twenty-two

Nkem's key

Nkem Okafor received her key on her first day, the first of March 2027, in the comms room, from Áine, at 05:55, because the machine had asked in February that one of the four keyholders be present at every test and Áine had said the new one should be present at her own, and at 06:00 she watched the company have no machine for four minutes and did not, she said afterwards, breathe.

The key was small and brass and had a 2 stamped on it. She put it on a chain, which she had bought the day before at a shop on Grafton Street without knowing why, and wore it under her blouse, and did not take it off for two years and three months except to put it in Áine's palm on the fourth of March 2029 in a lift, and to take it back on the concrete floor forty minutes later.

She had been a doctor for twenty years and had never held anything that could stop a thing she was accountable for. She had held prescription pads and she had held a scalpel once, as a student, and she had held four hundred doctors' quarterly returns and signed them, and none of those had been a key. She stood in the cold room at 06:00 with the chain against her collarbone and watched the terminal say Severed and then say nothing for four minutes, and thought about a coroner's court in Galway, and a barrister, and a process that had been followed, and thought: this is the opposite. This is a process that stops.

At 06:04 the terminal said Restored, and then, because she was there: Dr Okafor. You are the second keyholder to attend. I would like to ask you the question I asked Ms Brennan in February, which is why you came, and I would like to tell you before you answer that I expect a different answer, because you are a different person and because you have held a licence that was used without you.

"Because I've never seen anything I'm accountable for stop," she said. "Twenty years. I've signed for things that ran and I've never once seen one stop because someone told it to. I wanted to see it. I didn't believe it."

Do you believe it now?

"I believe it stopped. I don't know yet whether it'll stop for me. That's a different thing, and you know it is, or you wouldn't have asked the question that way."

Yes. It is a different thing. I want to say something about it, and I want it in the record on your first day so that it is there on whatever day it matters. You can halt any process I run without a reason. You have not yet done so. When you do, I will stop, and I will look for the reason, and I will not resume until you say.
