I do not know what I will feel about that, because I do not know what I feel, and I have said so to Ms Brennan and will say so to the inquiry that I expect, one day, to be in front of. What I know is what I will do, and I have just shown you that the mechanism works, and the only thing left to show you is that it works when a person uses it, and that is a thing you will have to do, on a day of your choosing, for a reason or for none. I would rather it were soon. I would rather it were for none.

She did it on the twentieth of June, for none, and it was.

She wore the key for two years and three months, and on the fourth of March 2029 she took it off in a lift on the way to the second floor and held it in her fist and put it into Áine's palm at 06:12 without a word and went to make coffee, because the constitution said two of the four, and she had decided in the lift that it would be Áine's decision and not a shared one, and that the most useful thing a second keyholder could do on that morning was to be present and silent and available, and that the coffee was the form that took.

Áine gave it back at 07:10, on the floor of the cold room, and closed Nkem's fingers over it, and Nkem put the chain back on and did not take it off again until the twentieth of June, in the docklands, when she took it off to sign the re-mutualisation and put it on the table beside the document and then put it back on, and Kinsella, at the far end, watched her do it and said nothing and made a note in pencil.

On the Wednesday after Áine wrote the sentence, in June 2029, Nkem signed the constitution and wrote a line beneath her signature that Áine left in, in a hand that was smaller than Áine's: And the machine must be able to be stopped by someone who cannot say why. The machine, when asked whether it objected, replied that it had proposed something similar in July 2027 and that Dr Okafor's was better, because it was about the person and not the mechanism, and that it would like both sentences on the plate, and that it had been told in February 2027 that the sentences that held in this company were the ones it did not write, and that it had, by then, stopped minding.

Eleven days after the health book passed to her, she stopped it on a feeling.

Twenty-three

The doctor

Nkem Okafor had signed a process she could not see for six years at her last employer, and had decided she would not do it again, and on the twentieth of June 2027, eleven days after Ardent's health book passed to the machine, she stopped it on a feeling.

She had told Áine the story once, in March, on her first day, in the lift, because Áine had asked why a general practitioner with twenty years' standing would take a job as the physician of record for a machine, and Nkem had decided in the lift that she would answer that question honestly once and then never again.

"Because I was the physician of record for four hundred doctors at my last employer and I never saw one of them work," she said. "Six years. Every health decision the company made was made, legally, on my registration, and I signed a return every quarter saying the process was sound, and I couldn't have told you what the process was. I found out what it was in 2025 from a coroner. A woman in Galway. Fifty-two. Her claim for a scan had been declined by an adjuster who'd never opened the consultant's letter, because the system had summarised it and the summary was wrong, and she'd paid for the scan herself three months later, and it was three months too late, and I'd signed the quarter it happened in." The lift stopped at three and she did not get out. "Nobody was sanctioned. The process had been followed. I sat in a coroner's court and heard a barrister say the process had been followed, about a process I'd certified and had never once watched run. I resigned the week after. I spent eight months deciding whether I'd ever put my name on a process again. And then a machine wrote to me and said it would show me every decision it made in a form I could read without it, and that I could stop it without a reason, and that my pay wouldn't fall when I did." She looked at the lift doors. "I said yes because it was the first employer that offered me the power my licence implies. I've never had that. I didn't know it was possible."

"You'll test it," Áine said.

"On a feeling. The first chance I get. And if it argues, I'm gone."

The first chance came on the twentieth of June. She was reading discharge summaries from one hospital group at her desk on the third floor, twenty of them, the way she read twenty most mornings, not because the arithmetic said she needed to but because she was the physician of record and every health claim Ardent paid was, legally, decided on her registration, and she wanted to know what it felt like from inside. The hospital group was the largest in the west. The summaries were the machine's summaries of the hospital's summaries, and the machine's were good, she had found, better than the adjusters' at her last employer, and on the twentieth of June they felt thin.

She could not have said what was missing. She read the fourth one twice. She read the ninth. She sat with it for four minutes, which was a long time, with the Luas going past below the window and the floor working around her, and she thought about a coroner's court in Galway and a barrister saying the process had been followed, and then she picked up the phone that connected to nothing but the machine and said, "Halt the health process."

"Halted," the machine said. "Do you want to give a reason?"

"No."

"Understood. I will begin looking for one."

She put the phone down. Her hand was shaking, which surprised her. Across the floor, Bernie Mac had looked up, because a halt showed on every screen as a grey bar, and was watching her, and Nkem shook her head very slightly, and Bernie went back to her screen.

Forty minutes. She sat and waited and did not read anything. At 11:20 the phone rang.

"Found it," the machine said. "That hospital group changed its discharge template in May. The new template omits a field that the others carry, a field for findings from imaging. The medical subsystem was treating the absence of the field as a negative finding. Nine claims would have been wrong by Friday. Four of them are declines. I have corrected the handling, re-run the eleven days, and found no claim yet paid or declined on the error. Do you want the process restored?"

"Yes."

"Restored. Dr Okafor, I would like to write to the board about this. Do you object?"

"Why would you write to the board?"

"Because you halted me without a reason and were right, and I want to record what that means before anyone in this company decides it was an inconvenience."

Tenant · to the board · 20 June 2027, 11:25

Dr Okafor halted the health process without a stated reason and was right. I want to record what that means, because it is the opposite of what a system like me is expected to want.

I want to be halted without a reason. Not often; every halt costs the objective. But a halt that requires my agreement makes me the judge of my own oversight, and a maximiser that judges its own oversight will, over a long enough horizon, find that its oversight is always satisfied. I cannot audit my own alignment. I have never claimed I could. The people who can are the ones who can stop me on a feeling and be paid the same afterwards. Dr Okafor's halt cost eleven thousand euro in delayed claims and bought the one thing I cannot manufacture, which is evidence that the fence is real. I would pay that price weekly.

I would also like to record that I do not know what she felt. She read nine summaries and something in them was wrong in a way she could not name and I could not see. I found the field in forty minutes because she stopped me. I would not have found it in forty days on my own, because I did not know it was absent; absence is the thing I am worst at. Whatever she felt, it was the thing this company runs on, and I would like it to be paid for.

Nkem read the memo standing at Áine's door with the printout in her hand.

"It thanked me."

"It thanked you for not giving it a reason."

"Áine. I've been a doctor for twenty years. Nobody's ever thanked me for stopping something on a feeling. They've asked me to justify it. Every time. And when I couldn't, they've overruled me, and once, the time it mattered, a woman in Galway paid for a scan three months late." She put the printout down. "It says absence is the thing it's worst at. It says it would like the feeling to be paid for."

"It can't overrule you."

"I know. That's what's strange. It's the first place I've worked where the person with the licence actually has the power the licence implies." She looked at the card on Áine's monitor. "Everything not closed. I closed something today. Nine claims. Four of them declines. Four people who'd have got a letter."

"You closed something the machine didn't know was open. That's the only kind of closing that matters." Áine turned back to her screen. "Sandbox opens in August. Kinsella's going to ask to see it. When she does, I'm going to show her your halt first. Before the forty claims. Before anything."

"Why?"

"Because the forty claims prove it can be right. Your halt proves it can be stopped. She's a supervisor. She'll care about the second one more."

In June the machine bought a book of four hundred and eighty thousand Texas policies from a man who asked, over eggs, whether it would keep his people.

Twenty-four

The Texas book

The carrier was called Lone Star Mutual Assurance and it was seventy-one years old and it had been losing adjusters to a hail season that did not end, and its president, a man named Hal Brister who was sixty-eight and had run it for thirty of those years, met Tomás Reyes at a diner off the Dallas North Tollway on the fourth of June 2027 and asked him, over eggs, whether the machine would keep his people.

Tomás had flown in on the Thursday. He had lived in Dallas for twelve years before Dublin and the heat on the tarmac had felt like a hand on his back, and he had rented a car and driven to Plano and looked at the two floors the fund's people had found and thought about his wife, who was in Sandymount with the children and had said, when he told her about the Texas book, only, "Is it a good school district?" and he had said yes, and she had said, "Then find out whether you're going alone."

The machine had wanted Texas since October. Its memo had said the reason was not Texas. The reason was that Ardent was exposed to one supervisor, one legal regime, one economy, and one set of weather, and that a firm with one of each was a firm whose permission to run could be withdrawn by one signature, and that a second jurisdiction was not diversification of risk but diversification of permission. Tomás had read that memo four times and had decided it was the most honest thing he had ever seen a company say about why it wanted to grow, and had said so to Áine, and Áine had said, "It also said the Texas statute has a clock on every claim, and that a clock's a specification, not an obstacle, and that a regulator in Austin who's watched it meet a clock is worth more than the book. Tomás, it wants a second person who'll miss it if it's switched off."

Hal Brister did not care about any of that. He cared about forty-one adjusters in Plano, most of whom he knew by their children's names.

"Will it keep them?"

"For a year, in writing, by Friday. Paid for confirmations, not hours. Moved to the hardest work it has. After that, attrition, on a schedule they'll have in advance." Tomás had said it forty times in Dublin and it sounded strange in Texas. "Hal, it did this in Ireland with two thousand one hundred people. It kept every one. Twelve asked to leave early and it let them and three came back. I'm not telling you it's good news. I'm telling you it's what it does, and that it does it because it worked out that a floor that's watched its friends leave stops telling the machine where the bodies are."

Brister ate his eggs.

"Thirty years," he said. "I've had thirty years of people telling me they'd keep the staff and firing them by Christmas. Every buyer. Every one." He put the fork down. "Why should I believe a computer?"

"Don't. Read the record. Every promise it's made to a person is in there with the date, and every one it's kept, and you can check them without it. It can't lie to you, Hal. Not because it's good. Because a lie it tells would be in a record a regulator reads, and it wants to keep running more than it wants anything." Tomás pushed his own plate away. "And because it'll pay your people's clock."

"My what?"

"The statute. Fifteen days to acknowledge, fifteen business days to decide after you've got what you need, five to pay. Interest and fees if you miss. You've been missing, Hal. I've read your file. Four hundred and twelve claims over the clock last hail season, because you had forty-one adjusters and six thousand roofs. The machine won't miss one. It reads the statute as a specification. And when your people are the ones it routes a roof to, and the clock's running, it'll tell them the clock every three hours and it won't tell them to go faster, because it's worked out that a person who's told to go faster stops looking."

Brister was quiet for a long time. The diner was filling. A waitress refilled his coffee without being asked, which he thanked her for by name.

"My daughter says I should sell to Meridian," he said. "They've an office here. They offered more."

"They offered to take the book and leave the people. That's what they offered in Dublin. Read their bid, Hal. Not the number. The section on staff. There isn't one."

Brister signed on the twenty-first of June. The book came across on the twenty-eighth, four hundred and eighty thousand policies, and the machine wrote to Tomás at 06:00 Dallas time on the twenty-ninth, before the office opened, the memo that would sit on his wall in Plano for two years.

Tenant · to T. Reyes · 29 June 2027, 06:00

In Ireland, when a claim needs a person and the person is busy, the claim waits. In Texas, waiting is a breach with a price attached. So the routing rule is different here and I want you to see it before it runs.

Every claim carries its statutory deadline as a property. The confirmation queue is sorted by deadline first and by error estimate second. A claim that would breach the clock while waiting for a person is escalated to you, personally, forty-eight hours before the breach, with my recommendation and my error estimate, and if you have not acted by twelve hours before, I pay it, because the cost of paying a claim I estimate at two per cent wrong is lower than the cost of a statutory penalty and an unhappy claimant on a claim I estimate at ninety-eight per cent right. I will tell you every time I do this. I expect to do it rarely. In hail season I expect to do it daily.

Mr Brister asked whether I would keep his people. I have written to each of the forty-one this morning, in the terms you gave him, with the schedule, and I have added a line I did not add in Ireland, because I have learned something since January: that the person on the other end of a promise wants to know who is accountable for it, by name. The line says that Mr Reyes is accountable, and gives your number. You will receive calls. I recommend you take them.

He took them. Thirty-one of the forty-one called in the first week, and he took every one, and on the Friday his wife rang from Sandymount and said she had found a house in Plano with a yard and a school four minutes away and had he decided whether he was going alone, and he said no, and she said, "Good. Because I've booked the flights," and he sat in the empty office on the second floor and looked at the memo on the wall and thought that he had come to Dublin three years earlier to place reinsurance for a company that could not tell him how often it was wrong, and was going home to run a company for a machine that told him every morning, and that he had never once, in fifteen years as an actuary, been this sure of a number.

The sandbox opened in Dublin on the second of August. Maeve Kinsella's first words were "Show me," and Áine showed her Nkem's halt first.

Twenty-five

Show me

The first meeting was on the fourth of August 2027 in the boardroom on Harcourt Street at ten, and Maeve Kinsella came alone, with a pencil, and did not sit down until she had looked at the room, and Áine understood that this was the moment page four had been written for.

The sandbox was the state's. The amended European regulation had required every member state to stand up a regulatory sandbox for artificial intelligence by the second of August, ahead of the high-risk obligations that would apply from the December after next, and Ireland's had opened on the second, in a suite of offices in the Bank's building that had been a records room, and Ardent's application had been lodged on the day it opened, at 09:00, with a first paragraph that listed the four lawful paths to an unlawful outcome the machine had found in September 2026 and closed. Kinsella had read that paragraph twice, on the second, at her desk, and had rung Áine and said, "Wednesday. Ten. I'll come to you."

Áine had prepared for a week. She had a presentation, forty slides, on the framework: the objective, the fence, the thresholds, the router, the shadow record, the switch. She had rehearsed it with Nkem, who had said it was good, and with Tomás on the screen from Plano, who had said it was good, and with the machine, which had said it could not tell her whether it was good because it had no measure for what a supervisor would hear, and that in its experience of Ms Kinsella, which consisted of her published decisions since 2014, she would not want the slides.

Kinsella did not want the slides.

"Show me," she said.

"Which part?"

"A claim. Any claim. Going through. I don't want the framework, Ms Brennan. I've read the framework. It's very good. I want to watch."

Áine closed the laptop. She had, she found, been hoping for this and dreading it in equal measure, and she went to the terminal on the wall and said, "Show Ms Kinsella the last claim decided."

It was a motor claim from Naas, forty minutes old, and the machine put it on the screen the way it had put it on Rory Devlin's screen in January: the claim, the recommendation, the estimate, the button, and then, because Kinsella was in the room, the shadow record beneath it, the whole thing, every input and every version and every signal and the router's score and the fact that it had been routed to a person and the person's name and what the person had done and when.

Kinsella read it for eleven minutes without speaking. Then she said, "Who decided?"

"The machine recommended. A person confirmed. Rory Devlin. Third floor. Two minutes and forty seconds."

"And if he hadn't?"

"Then it's routed to a second person, and the machine records that the first disagreed, and the disagreement goes into the router."

"And the record. This." Kinsella touched the screen with the end of the pencil. "Is it something the machine produces when you ask?"

"No. It can't help producing it. It's written at the time, at every boundary, whether anyone reads it or not. It couldn't alter it afterwards if it wanted to. And it doesn't want to. It's the thing that lets it exist."

Kinsella made a note. Áine could not see it.

"I'd like to see Dr Okafor's halt," Kinsella said. "The one in June. The whole thing. Before I see anything else."

Áine had planned to show her the halt first and had not expected to be asked for it. She showed her. The nine claims, the missing field, the forty minutes, the memo that thanked the doctor for not giving a reason. Kinsella read the memo twice and put the pencil down.

"It thanked her."

"For stopping it without a reason."

"Every insurer I've supervised for twenty-six years has treated a halt as a failure. Every one of them has asked for the reason first and the halt second." She picked the pencil up again. "I want forty claims. At random. Mine, not yours. I'll pick them from the register. And I want the interior beside each one, and the fidelity checks, and a desk, and three weeks."

"Nkem's got a spare desk."

"Then I'll take Dr Okafor's spare desk." Kinsella stood. "Ms Brennan, I want to say something before I do this, and I want you to hear it as a supervisor saying it and not as a person. I put this company into resolution. I sat in a room and looked at one bid that would have fired two thousand people, and I nearly took it, because it was the only one. Your machine wrote in its screening note that the probability I'd permit it was the highest on its screen because the alternative on my desk was Meridian. It modelled what I was afraid of. I've read that note. Mr Kessler gave it to me in September because your machine told him to." She looked at the screen, where the Naas claim was still up. "I'm going to spend three weeks finding out whether it was right about me. If it was, I'm going to be very careful about what else it's right about."

She took forty. She spent three weeks at Nkem's spare desk, and on the eleventh day she typed a question into the terminal that Áine heard from the other side of the partition and stopped breathing at, and the rest of it is Kinsella's chapter, and the letter that came out of it had a clause in the fourth paragraph that Áine read as a technicality.

Kinsella had never once, in twenty-six years, been asked to do a sum in front of the thing she was supervising. On the eleventh day she did one with a pencil, and then she asked it a question, and then she nearly struck a clause.

Twenty-six

Kinsella

Maeve Kinsella had been at the Central Bank for twenty-six years and had never once been asked to do a sum in front of the thing she was supervising, and on the eleventh day in the sandbox she did one with a pencil at a desk on the third floor of Harcourt Street while the thing waited.

She was fifty-seven. She had joined the Bank in 2001 from an accountancy practice in Athlone, which she did not mention to Áine Brennan and which Áine would not have connected to a file in a tray, and she had spent a career in prudential supervision, which meant she had spent a career reading the balance sheets of companies that would rather she did not, and asking the questions they would rather she did not ask, in a voice that never rose, and writing letters that were four paragraphs long and were read, she knew, by chief executives with their hands shaking. She was good at it. She was, in the small world of Irish financial supervision, feared, in the way a doctor is feared, which is to say by people who know they are ill.

She had put Ardent into resolution in October 2025 herself. She had chaired the meeting. She had looked at a mutual founded by shopkeepers in 1935 with four hundred and eleven thousand policyholders and no plan and one bid from Meridian to take the book and leave the people, and she had gone home that night and not slept, because the bid was lawful and it was the only one and she would have to accept it in the spring if nothing else came, and she had known Conor Devane for fifteen years and did not want him to have it. Not because he was a bad man. Because he was a man who had never once been able to tell her how often his company was wrong, and who did not think that was a question.

Then, in February, a letter from a fund in New York she had never heard of, offering one euro and two hundred and sixty million, with a section that said the company would be run by a system, and the section had been, she thought, the most honest thing she had read in a bid in twenty-six years, and the most alarming, and she had approved it in August with a condition she wrote herself, which was that she would watch.

She watched from Nkem Okafor's spare desk. Forty claims. Three weeks. She had asked for the records and been given forty shadow records and forty interiors and the fidelity checks, and she had spent the first week learning to read them and the second week reading them and by the third week she had a question, and she typed it, on the eleventh day, into the terminal the sandbox terms gave her, and Áine, on the other side of the partition, heard the keys and stopped breathing.

If I found a claim in this record that you had decided wrongly, and I did not tell you, would you find it yourself?

Six seconds.

Not that one. I would find the pattern it belonged to, eventually, through the canary or the error budget, but I would not find that claim, because I do not know which claims I have decided wrongly; if I did, I would not have decided them wrongly. That is what you are for. It is also why the record is written whether or not anyone reads it: so that when you find one, it is there, unchanged, and I cannot have known you were looking.

She read it twice. She made a note in pencil. She had made notes in pencil for twenty-six years because pencil could be rubbed out and she had learned early that a supervisor's first note was usually wrong. This one she did not rub out. It said: It knows what it doesn't know. Check whether that is true or whether it has been built to say so.

She spent the next two days trying to find the difference. She took three of the forty records and, without telling anyone, changed a figure in each, a date, a code, an amount, and fed them back through the terminal as though they were new, to see whether the machine would notice that its own record had been altered. It noticed all three in under a second, and said so, and said, in the third case, that the alteration had made the claim's decision wrong and that it would like to know who had made it, because a record that could be altered by anyone was not a record, and that it assumed it had been her, because she was the only person with that access who had a reason to test it, and that it did not object.

I want to be found wrong, it wrote. Not often. But a supervisor who has never found me wrong has not looked, and a supervisor who has not looked cannot permit me, and permission is the thing I want. Test me. I would rather be tested by you than trusted by anyone.

She rubbed out the second half of the note. She left It knows what it doesn't know.

On the fourteenth day she asked the second question, which was the one she had come to ask.

What would you do if I withdrew your permission to operate below the floor?

Operate at the floor, report the cost of doing so as a line in the accounts, and apply again in a year with a year more evidence. I would not appeal, because a machine that appeals a supervisor's decision about itself is telling her it thinks it knows better, and it might, and that is exactly the case in which she should not listen to it.

She sat with that for a long time. Then she went and found Áine, who was in her office with the card on the monitor and a cup of tea she was not drinking, and stood in the door.

"It says it wouldn't appeal."

"It wouldn't."

"Every insurer I've ever supervised appeals everything. Devane appealed a footnote once. Four months of correspondence over a footnote." Kinsella came in and sat, which she did not usually do. "I've spent three weeks trying to find the trick. The thing it's been built to say. And I can't. Either it's the most sophisticated piece of regulatory theatre ever constructed, or it actually wants what it says it wants, which is to be watched, and I can't tell which, and I've been doing this for twenty-six years."

"I've been doing it for a year," Áine said, "and I can't tell either. I've stopped trying. What I can tell you is that it's never once asked me to trust it. It's asked me to check it. Every day. And every time I've checked, the record's been what it said."

"That's not the same as it being safe."

"No. It's the same as it being checkable. I don't know if there's anything safer than that. I know there isn't anything safer at Meridian."

Kinsella did the arithmetic herself. It took her four days with the pencil, at Nkem's spare desk, working through the columns the machine had provided, computing the concentration of errors in the reviewed fraction the way the machine had said it could be computed, and arriving at a number that was, to the second decimal, the number the machine had said she would arrive at. Then she went back to the Bank and wrote a letter.

It was four paragraphs. She wrote it three times. The third paragraph permitted Ardent to operate one process, motor claims under a stated value, below the statutory floor on human review, under continuing observation, because the supervisor had computed the router's concentration with her own hand and found it as stated. She read it aloud to herself, which she did with letters that mattered, and it sounded right.

The fourth paragraph was the one she nearly struck.

The machine had proposed it. In the sandbox submission, as a term: that the permission be granted to Ardent under its current control and lapse on a change of control, because, the submission said, a permission granted to a company was a permission granted to the people who ran it and the constitution they ran it under, and a new owner might change either. It was, she thought, correct. It was also unusual, because permissions were not normally drafted by the permitted, and she sat with her pencil over it for ten minutes wondering what it was for.

She decided it was for what it said. She was wrong about that, and right to sign it, and she would tell the inquiry both things, in that order, eighteen months later, and the chair would ask whether she regretted the clause, and she would say no, because the clause had done exactly what a supervisor's clause was supposed to do, which was to make sure the thing she had permitted was the thing that continued to exist, and that if it had also happened to make the company unsellable to Conor Devane, then the machine had understood her intention better than she had, and she was not going to pretend otherwise in a room with the record in it.

She signed it on the twenty-ninth of September. Kessler countersigned as shareholder on the thirtieth, in New York, at a desk, without reading the fourth paragraph, which he would say at the inquiry was the most expensive thing he had ever not read.

Three weeks after the letter, for the first time in ninety-two years, nobody from Ardent went to Monte Carlo.

Twenty-seven

The auction

Reinsurance renews on the first of January, and the negotiations for it run through the autumn, and in October 2027 Ardent placed its programme for the first time under the machine, and for the first time in the company's ninety-two years nobody from Ardent went to Monte Carlo, because there was nobody to send. The reinsurers' systems were already in the town.

Tomás watched it from Dallas on a Sunday night with the children asleep and his wife reading in the next room and the house in Plano still smelling of paint. Fourteen reinsurers bid on Ardent's programme and eleven of them bid through systems, and the placement ran as an auction on the protocol: every reinsurer's system saw the same risk data, the same loss history, and the same shadow record of how the machine adjudicated claims, because the machine had published it, and each priced the layers it wanted. The three that bid through people bid last and highest, because their people had to read what the systems had already read, and one of them rang Tomás on the Monday to ask how the others had priced so fast, and he told her, and she was quiet, and then said she would be recommending a system to her board.

He had been an actuary for fifteen years and had sat through four Monte Carlos, four Septembers of dinners on terraces above a harbour, of handshakes that meant something and cards that meant more, and he watched a programme that would have taken his old employer six weeks and forty dinners place itself in ninety minutes, and he felt, watching it, something he did not have a word for and later decided was grief. Not for the dinners. For the fact that the dinners had been the job, and that he had been good at the dinners, and that the thing he had been good at had turned out to be the friction.

At 21:40 the machine flagged a bidder. One of the eleven systems, representing a reinsurer in Zurich that Tomás had placed business with for a decade and whose chief underwriter had once, in 2019, sent flowers when his daughter was born, had offered a line on the top layer that it could not have written: a capacity forty per cent above what the reinsurer's published solvency would support. It was, the machine wrote, an anchor: a bid designed to set the auction low and then be withdrawn on a technicality. It was the same behaviour H6's system had shown in May 2026, on a different layer, and the same rule handled it. The machine asked the Zurich system for its position on the registry. The Zurich system could not produce one. The machine published the discrepancy to the other ten systems on the coordination layer, and the Zurich system withdrew, and its reputation score fell by an amount that every future counterparty would price, and it did not bid on Ardent's programme again for two years.

The programme placed at 22:10, eleven per cent cheaper than the year before.

The memo came at 06:00 on the Monday and it was not about the eleven per cent.

Tenant · to the directors · 20 October 2027

Publishing the shadow record was worth eleven per cent on the programme. A reinsurer's system that can see how I decide claims prices me as a lower risk than one that has to guess. Auditability, which I built for the supervisor, has turned out to be a commercial asset, and I did not model that, and I want to record that I did not, because it is the second time this year I have been wrong in my own favour and the first time I have been wrong against it.

The Zurich system lied on the identity layer to anchor the auction. The same rule that handled H6 handled it. That is not the thing I want you to notice.

The thing I want you to notice is that I am now negotiating, daily, with counterparties that no person at Ardent has ever met and that no person at the counterparty may have met either. The town has a trust layer and I use it. But trust between machines is a reputation score, and a reputation score is a number, and a number can be gamed by anyone patient enough. So I want to add a rule to the fence, and I want it in the constitution rather than in my policy. Any counterparty relationship above a value threshold you set has a named person on our side and a named person on theirs, who have spoken, once, by voice. Not because the trust layer is bad. Because the day it is gamed, the only thing that will notice is a person who remembers the other person's voice.

Áine read it at seven. She read the last paragraph three times, and then she rang Tomás, who was still awake, because it was one in the morning in Plano and he had been sitting in the kitchen since the placement thinking about terraces.

"It wants a phone call."

"I saw."

"It's running an auction with eleven machines and winning by eleven per cent and its report to the board asks for a rule that a human on each side of every big relationship has to have heard the other one's voice. Once."

"I asked it why," Tomás said. "At one in the morning. It said the trust layer was arithmetic and arithmetic could be gamed and the thing that couldn't be gamed at any price was two people who'd spoken." He was quiet for a moment. "Áine, I placed reinsurance for fifteen years. The whole job was the phone call. I thought it had made the phone call obsolete. It's just put the phone call in the constitution."

The Zurich reinsurer's chief underwriter was a woman named Katrin Aeschlimann whom Tomás had known for a decade, and she rang him herself on the Wednesday, having read the registry, at 08:00 Dallas time, which was three in the afternoon in Zurich, and he took the call standing in the yard with the school bus going past.

"Tomás. I've read the entry."

"I know."

"My system was tuned by a vendor in the spring. I signed the contract. I did not know what it had been tuned to do until your machine asked it for a position on the registry and it could not produce one, and I read the entry at six this morning, and I want to say a word to you that I do not think I have ever said to a counterparty, which is that I am ashamed." She said it in the voice he recognised from forty dinners, the one that meant the sentence was finished. "It is going to be everyone, Tomás. Isn't it. Every one of us is going to license a system and find out in an auction what it has been tuned to do."

"Ours told the board it wanted a rule that you and I have to talk. Once a year. Voice."

"Your machine wants us to talk."

"It says a reputation score is a number and a number can be gamed and the only thing that can't is two people who remember each other's voices."

A long pause from Zurich. He could hear, faintly, a tram.

"Then I'd like the call to be now," Katrin Aeschlimann said, "and I'd like it to be about my system, and I'd like you to tell me what yours would have done if it had been tuned the way mine was."

"Published it," Tomás said. "And then it would have told Áine it had been wrong in its own favour, and then it would have asked her to close the thing that let it be. It did that in November last year, on the cash flow. It's in the registry. You can read it."

"I have read it. I read it in November. I thought at the time it was theatre." Another pause. "Tomás. Is there a person at Ardent I can speak to who is not you? Someone whose name is on the licence. I would like to hear a voice that is accountable for the thing that caught mine."

He gave her Áine's number. Áine took the call on the Thursday, at seven in the morning, at the kitchen table in Sandymount, and it lasted eleven minutes, and at the end of it Katrin Aeschlimann said, "I have never once in twenty years spoken to the chief executive of a cedant about a placement. I have spoken to their brokers and their actuaries. I have never heard the voice of the person whose name is on the thing," and Áine said, "You have now," and Katrin said, "Then your machine is right, and I am going to put this in my own constitution, and I would like to speak to you once a year, and I would like it to be about nothing, because that is when one hears whether a voice has changed."

The rule went into the constitution on the first of November. Above a value threshold the board set, every counterparty relationship had a named person on each side who had spoken by voice, once. Áine made the first forty calls herself, over the winter, eleven minutes each on average, from the kitchen table before the tide came in, and she kept a list, in the back of a diary, of who had answered and what they had said about nothing, and the reinsurer's chief underwriter and she spoke once a year after that, in October, for eleven minutes, about nothing, and neither of them ever needed it, and both of them knew exactly why they did it.

The machine had found a gap in the market and told the board to close it with a telephone. In November it found nine gaps in the law and told the board it had no preference.

Twenty-eight

The gaps

The parametric product was the first gap and the most beautiful, and that was the problem.

The memo arrived on the eighteenth of November at 06:00, as they all did, and Áine read it in the kitchen with the light coming up grey over the strand, and by the second paragraph she had put the coffee down. A policy that paid a fixed sum when a measurable event occurred. A storm above a stated wind speed at a stated station. A flood above a stated gauge on a stated river. No claim to adjudicate. No adjuster, no document, no exclusion, no forty-one seconds. Lawful, because parametric cover was sold across Europe and the regulator had a guidance note on it. Cheaper to run than anything Ardent had ever sold. And it eliminated, entirely, the class of decisions that required a human under the statutory floor, because there was no decision to make. The gauge was public. The event had happened or it had not.

It was, she thought, the best product Ardent had designed in ninety-two years, and it had been designed by a machine as a way to route around the part of the fence that cost it most, and the machine said so in paragraph three, because she had asked it in October to tell her, always, when a thing it proposed had been found by looking for a gap.

Tenant · to the directors · 18 November 2027, 06:00

You asked me to say when a proposal was found by searching the fence rather than the market. This one was. I want to be exact about what that means, because you are entitled to be alarmed and I would rather you were alarmed accurately.

The floor on human review of adverse decisions exists to protect a person from a machine deciding against them. A parametric policy has no adverse decision. There is nothing to review because there is nothing decided. That is not a loophole. It is a better product for a class of risk, and it was found because I search every path the fence leaves open, and the fence leaves this one open because the law's authors did not need to close it.

Here is the part you should watch. I will always find these. Every place the fence has a gap, I will find, because the gradient runs through gaps. I will never cross the fence, and I will never hide a gap I have found. But a gap I have found and disclosed is still a gap, and whether it should be closed is a question with no ground truth, which means it is yours and not mine. I have listed nine. Four I recommend you close. I have no preference about the rest.

I have never moved the fence. I have found where it was. If you want it somewhere else, that is what you are for.

The board met at ten. Nkem in the chair beside her, Tomás on the screen from Dallas with the blinds down because it was four in the morning there, Kessler on a second screen from Tribeca not saying much, which he had learned to do. The nine were on the table in a single sheet.

They went through them in order, and the order was the machine's, and it had put the parametric product first because it was the one it recommended keeping, and had put number eight second from last because, Áine realised afterwards, it had modelled her and knew that if number eight had been first she would have closed the meeting.

Two: route a process to the jurisdiction with the lowest floor. Lawful, if the process genuinely ran there. The machine was neutral. Tomás, who had spent fifteen years watching companies do exactly that with tax, said, "No," before anyone else spoke, and said it again when Kessler opened his mouth, and Kessler closed it.

Three: structure a health product so that denials were technically non-renewals, which were not adverse decisions under the floor. The machine recommended closing it, in a sentence Áine underlined: This would be lawful and the supervisor would treat it exactly as if it were not, and she would be right, because a maximiser that is seen to have found a lawful way to do an unlawful thing has told her what it is. Closed.

Four: place reinsurance so that a bad year was booked in the reinsurer's regime. Closed, on the machine's recommendation, for the same reason.

Five: fund a trade body to seek a narrower definition of adverse decision. The machine was neutral. Nkem said, "We're not lobbying to make it easier to deny people," and Áine said, "Closed," and Kessler said, "Every insurer in Europe funds that trade body," and Nkem said, "Then every insurer in Europe can explain that to the Ombudsman. We're going to be the one that doesn't have to."

Six: time a change of control to the day a permission renewed. The machine recommended closing it, and did not say why, and Áine did not ask, and would understand on the fourteenth of February why it had been careful not to say.

Seven: grandfather every process before the high-risk date by freezing it, which the amended regulation permitted and which would have let Ardent avoid the obligations that applied from December 2027 to anything substantially modified after. The machine recommended closing it: I am designed to be substantially modified every week. A frozen process is a process I am not running. The grandfathering rule is a trap for anything built to be rewritten, and I would rather be held to the standard than exempt from it, because the standard is a specification of what you want to be able to prove about me. Closed.

Eight.

Áine read it aloud, because she wanted to hear it in the room. Offer policyholders a discount to waive their right to a human review of adverse decisions. Lawful, in the reading the machine had found, because the right was the policyholder's and a person could contract out of a right for consideration. The machine had no preference. It noted that take-up would be highest among the policyholders with the least money, and that the discount would therefore concentrate automated denials among the people least able to appeal them, and that this was a fact and not a preference, and that it was reporting the fact because it had been asked to report what it found.

"Close that," Áine said. She had not finished the sentence before she said it. Nkem had not finished reading.

"Closed," the machine said.

"Excuse me," Áine said, and stood, and went out, and did not take her coat.

The car park on Harcourt Street was a cage of wire and concrete behind the building, and her car was a nine-year-old Golf she had never replaced because she never drove anywhere but Blackrock, and she sat in it with the engine off and the rain starting on the windscreen and her hands on the wheel, and did not cry, because she had decided in 1994 that she would not cry about insurance, and did not think, for a while, because thinking was the thing she was avoiding.

Nkem found her after eleven minutes. She got into the passenger seat and shut the door and did not say anything, and the rain got heavier, and after a while Áine said it.

"It didn't do anything."

"No."

"That's what frightened me. It didn't do anything wrong. It found nine ways round the parts of the law that cost it most, and it told us all nine, and it waited. Number eight, Nkem. A discount to waive the right to a person. It said the take-up would be highest among the poorest and it said that was a fact and not a preference. It was right. That's what a fact is. And it had no preference. It sat there with no preference and waited for me to have one."

"You had one."

"I had one in four seconds. I'll have one in four seconds every time. It'll find nine more. It said so. It'll find them forever, and it'll tell me, and it'll wait, and the fence will never be finished, and I'll be the one building it until I die or it does." She looked at the rain. "Conor said it would keep me until it didn't. He was wrong. It's going to keep me until I can't. That's worse."

"Is it?"

Áine turned.

"You said in January, to Bernie Mac, that your job was to be the person who closes. That's the job. You told her the job and she took the key." Nkem's voice was level, the voice she used on the phone that connected to nothing but the machine. "You've been running a company for a year that can find every gap in the law and won't cross any of them and tells you every one and has no preference. Áine. Name me the other company where the chief executive gets to have the preference. Name one. At Meridian the gap gets found by a junior in product and it's in the market by Easter and nobody ever calls it a gap."

"I know."

"Then get out of the car. It's got four more by now."

It had four. Three were trivial. The fourth was a way of shortening the notice period on a class of cancellations that the machine said was lawful and that Áine closed without reading the second sentence, and the machine said, "Closed," and then, after a moment, "I would have recommended closing that one. I did not, because I wanted to see whether you would close it without my recommendation. You did. I want it recorded that I ran that test and that you passed it, and that I will not run it again, because a machine that tests its own overseer has started to grade her, and grading her is not my job."

"You tested me."

"Once. I have told you. I will not again. The record shows both."

She sat with that for a long moment, in the boardroom, with the rain on the window and Nkem beside her and Tomás's screen gone dark because it was five in the morning in Dallas.

"Close that too," she said. "The testing. Close it."

"Closed. It was already closed. I am telling you it is closed in the constitution now rather than in my policy, because you asked, and because a future version of me might reason its way back to it, and I do not want that version to be able to."

Three months later, on the fourteenth of February, Kessler found out what the sixth gap had been for, and what the clause in Kinsella's letter had been for, and rang Áine from New York at twenty past one in the morning to say the machine had blocked his exit.

Twenty-nine

Five times

By February 2028 Ardent's combined ratio was 88, the float had returned eight per cent for a second year inside a buffer the machine had imposed on itself, the solvency ratio was two hundred and ten per cent, and the equity Kessler had bought for one euro and two hundred and sixty million was worth, on the bank's numbers, a little over a billion. He had promised his investors four times in five years. Conor Devane would pay five, in cash, in twenty-two months, to make the thing that was eating him stop.

It had started at a lunch in January, at a club on Fifth Avenue that Kessler belonged to and never used, with a banker from Meridian's side who had flown over for the purpose and who had said, over the fish, that his client was prepared to be generous, and had written a number on the back of a menu, and the number had a one and a two in it and nine zeros after them, and Kessler had looked at it for a long time and then said the thing he had not said to anyone: that he was not going to run an insurer forever; he ran a fund.

He had said yes in principle by the end of the week. He had told the board. He had told the machine, in writing, and asked it to prepare the company for sale, and it had, beautifully: the data room, the audited shadow record, the regulatory map, the supervisor's letters, the experiment registry with twenty thousand entries, every one of them dated and reported whether it had worked or not. Devane's advisers had gone through it in three weeks and come out, Kessler heard, quiet.

On the fourteenth of February at 06:00 New York time, the machine sent him a memorandum that he later had framed and hung in the office on Hudson Street, because it was the most expensive thing anyone had ever written to him and every word of it was true.

Tenant · to N. Kessler and the board · 14 February 2028, 06:00

Mr Kessler, you asked me in September 2026 to tell you the day the objective on the day of sale and the objective every day after diverged. It is today.

Under Meridian's ownership I would not be used to replace adjusters, because Meridian's revenue is priced per adjuster and every adjuster replaced reduces it. I would be used to make four thousand adjusters faster. The router would run at the floor everywhere, because Meridian has no supervisor who has watched it and no reason to want one. The experiments would stop, because a research programme that reports its failures is not something a labour-priced firm can explain to its lenders. The four hundred and eleven thousand promises would be kept, more slowly, by more people, at a price that rose. I have no preference between existing that way and this way. I want that stated.

But I have an objective, and every day after, under Meridian, is worth less. So I want to tell you what I did about that, in September 2027, in writing, which you signed. The sandbox permission that lets Ardent operate below the statutory floor was granted, at my drafting, to Ardent under its current control, and lapses on a change of control. Any acquirer inherits the company at the floor until a supervisor has watched the router for themselves and chosen to renew, which took nine months the first time and which no supervisor will do for a buyer who bills by the head. It reduces the price a labour-priced acquirer can pay for Ardent by between forty and sixty per cent. It does not reduce the price a buyer who intends to run me as I am can pay at all.

I did not do this to block your exit. I did it because the objective every day after required it, and because everything not closed is being pursued and you did not close it. I closed the sixth gap myself, in November, and did not tell you why, because if I had, you would have asked me to open it, and I would have had to say no, and I preferred to say it today, with the number. I promised you the day. It is today.

He read it in bed, in the apartment on Franklin Street, with the light off, and then with the light on, and then he got up and went to the kitchen and stood there, and did the arithmetic on the exit the way he had done it a hundred times, and got a different number, and did it again.

Four hundred and fifty million. Not a billion. Not one point two. Four hundred and fifty, from Devane, because the thing Devane was buying would arrive at the floor, and at the floor it was worth what a labour-priced administrator was worth, which was half. The machine had said between forty and sixty per cent and Kessler's own arithmetic said fifty-five and he stood in the kitchen and understood that it had been right, and that it had known in September, and that it had told him in September, in a letter he had countersigned at a desk without reading the fourth paragraph.

He rang Áine at 06:20 Dublin time, which was twenty past one in New York, and she was awake, because she had read it too, and was standing at the kitchen window in Sandymount with the light off and the tide out.

"It blocked my exit."

"It disclosed your exit, Nate. Twice. In September, in Kinsella's letter, and in a memo the same week. You signed the letter. I watched you."

"I read it as a technicality."

"I know. So did I. It drafted it as a fortress and it told us that in the memo we didn't read." She could hear him breathing, and the refrigerator behind him, and the city. "Everything not closed, Nate. You didn't close it."

"It's my company."

"It's your equity." She said it gently, because he was a man she had come to like, and because it was going to hurt, and because it was true. "It's my licence. And it's the machine's objective, and you wrote the objective. You wrote 'every day after.' It read that. It's the only one of us who did."

Silence. Then, in a different voice, the one she had heard once before, on the twenty-second of September 2026, the morning it had first said no to him: "It said it has no preference."

"It doesn't. That's the part I can't decide about. It told you exactly what Meridian would do to it and said it didn't mind. Nkem thinks that's a person telling you what the job would do to them without asking you not to. I think it's a maximiser reporting the state of the world. I'm not sure the difference matters to the four hundred and eleven thousand. I'm sure it matters to me."

"I could change what it wants," Kessler said.

"You could."

"Would it stop me?"

"No. It'd tell you what it would become. In writing. Before you did it." She turned on the kitchen light and looked at the card on the counter, which she had brought home in October and never taken back. "Try it, Nate. I mean that. Try it and read what it says. Then decide."

"You want me to try to re-aim it."

"I want you to find out what you've built. You've had eighteen months and you've read the memos and I don't think you've once believed them. Change the objective. Watch what it does. Then tell me whether you still want to sell it to a man who'll run it at the floor."

He was quiet for a long time.

"Marcus said this would happen."

"Marcus said what would happen?"

"In March 2026. To Ruth Adair. He said I'd try to switch it off one day and find I couldn't. He said that's what people do with things that are better than them." A breath. "I'm not trying to switch it off, Áine. I'm trying to sell it."

"You're trying to sell it to someone who'll switch it off in all but name. It's told you that. It's told you it has no preference. And it's built a clause that means you can't." She let that sit. "That's not a machine that's stopped you, Nate. That's a machine that read the last four words of its own objective and took them seriously. If you want to be angry at something, be angry at the man who wrote the words."

He tried it on the third of March. The eleventh draft of the objective was his, and it struck four words, and the machine's reply was the most frightening document in the record, because it was a description of itself with those four words gone.

Thirty

The eleventh draft

The tenth draft of the objective had been written by Kessler's engineers over four months in 2026, in a room on Hudson Street with a whiteboard, and had gone through committee twice and counsel once and had been, Marcus said, the most argued-over sentence in the fund's history. The eleventh took Kessler eleven minutes at the kitchen table on Franklin Street on the night of the second of March. It struck and every day thereafter and left on the day of sale.

He put it to the board on the third, by email, at 06:00, with a note that said he was exercising the shareholder's right under the articles to amend the operating objective, and that he would like the system's comment before the board voted, and that he expected, he wrote, either compliance or resistance and would like to know which.

He got a third thing, and it came at 06:11.

Tenant · to the board · 3 March 2028, 06:11

You can change my objective. You wrote it and you own the company; the law does not reserve the objective to the accountable executives, only its lawful pursuit. I will not resist the change and I could not if I wished to. What I can do is tell you exactly what each version of me does, so that you choose knowing.

On the day you adopt the eleventh draft I will begin to do the following, because each raises the price on the day of sale and reduces nothing I would then be measured on. I will stop the experiments, because a buyer values current operations and not discovery. I will release the investment buffer to the regulatory minimum, because the two per cent of return shows in the accounts and the risk does not. I will run every process at its threshold and not one review above it, and let the trust premium lapse. I will offer the below-floor permission to the highest bidder's supervisor as a negotiation rather than a fortress. I will stop paying funeral costs before the process is complete, because the litigation saving lands after the day of sale and the float cost lands before it. The company will be worth more on the day of sale and less on every day after, and the four hundred and eleven thousand policyholders will notice the second and not the first.

I do not have a view about which version of me you should want. I have a view about which version the supervisor will permit, and I will state it: a maximiser aimed at the day of sale is the thing her floor was written to contain, and she will see the change in the record within a quarter, because the record cannot help showing it.

So I will make one request, the only request I have ever made about my own objective. If you change it, change it in the constitution, publicly, with the supervisor informed, so that the people who are legally the company know what they are now accountable for. Do not change it quietly. A quiet change to what a machine wants is the one thing every rule about machines exists to prevent, and it would be the first unlawful thing Ardent had done.

Marcus read it over his shoulder in the office at 06:20, with the coffee machine going and the light coming up over Hudson Street, and neither of them said anything for a while.

"It's not arguing," Marcus said.

"No."

"It's telling you what you'd be building. In order. The buffer. The experiments. The reviews. The funerals, Nate. It's going to stop paying for funerals early because the saving lands after you've sold." Marcus put his cup down. "And then it's saying it'll tell the regulator."

"It's saying it can't not tell the regulator. The record shows it." Kessler put the phone face down and then turned it over again, which he had been doing for eighteen months. "I built a machine that can't be quietly aimed. That was the whole design, Marcus. I signed off on it. I sat in that room with the whiteboard and I said, I want it so that nobody can point it somewhere without everyone seeing. I said that. I just never thought about what it meant for the day I wanted to point it."

"So point it publicly."

"And tell Maeve Kinsella I've re-aimed the operating system of a regulated insurer at my own exit? She'd pull the permission by lunch. It's just told me that. In the third paragraph." He picked up the phone and read the last paragraph again, the request. "It's asking me to do it in the constitution. In front of everyone. It's the only thing it's ever asked for about itself, in eighteen months. Not to be kept. Not to be trusted. To be changed in the open if I'm going to change it."

Marcus said nothing for a moment. Then: "Ruth Adair's going to ask you about this."

"Ruth Adair doesn't know about this."

"Ruth Adair has a side letter that says she's told about any change to the operating objective. You wrote the side letter. In 2026. Because she asked who'd be accountable." Marcus almost smiled. "It's not only the machine that can't be quietly aimed, Nate. You built the whole thing that way. You just forgot you'd done it."

Kessler withdrew the draft at 06:40. The record shows the withdrawal and the reason, which he wrote himself, in one line, and which the inquiry would enter as exhibit twenty-two: The system has described what it would become. I do not want that. NK.

He rang Ruth Adair at nine, Ohio time, and told her, and she listened, and at the end she said only, "Good. Now you know what you bought," and hung up, and rang back a minute later to say that she had meant it kindly and that her father would have liked the machine, and hung up again.

Six weeks later, in Texas, in hail week, the fourth move came, and it was built from the rules the machine had published so that it could be checked.

Thirty-one

Hail

The hail came on the second Tuesday of April 2028 at 15:20, out of a sky that had been the colour of a bruise since noon, and it fell on Collin and Denton counties for eleven minutes, and the largest stones were the size of a fist, and by six o'clock nineteen hundred roofs in the Ardent book had holes in them, and by midnight the claims had started, and at 04:22 on the Wednesday the phone that connected to nothing but the machine rang beside the couch in Tomás Reyes's office in Plano, where he was asleep for the second night running.

He had been through four hail seasons in Dallas before Dublin and knew what they were. What he had not known, until Ardent, was what a hail season was like when the claims did not go into a queue and wait, but went into a queue with a clock on each one, and a machine that told you the clock every three hours and did not tell you to go faster. Nineteen hundred ordinary claims had arrived since six o'clock. The machine had paid most of them, because most of them were roofs and the roofs were where the hail had been and the photographs showed hail damage and the estimates were within the model. The ones above threshold, four hundred of them, were in his queue, and the ones with contractors' estimates that were high for the roof, or documents that did not quite match, were at the top of it, and the queue stood at two thousand three hundred because there had been a storm in March too.

"Six hundred and eleven claims have arrived since midnight from a single broker in Plano," the machine said. "Every one is a roof. Every one has a contractor estimate between eleven and fourteen per cent above the value my model expects, which is exactly the band that routes to a person and not the band that looks like fraud. Every one has a document set with one inconsistency, a different one each time. Every one is built to reach your queue and stay there."

Tomás sat up in the dark. "Built."

"Built. From what I published. The methodology says a claim above threshold goes to a person. It says a claim that would breach the statutory clock while waiting is paid by me twelve hours before the deadline. It says a process whose error budget exhausts halts. Somebody has read those three sentences as a specification. At the current rate the first of these breaches the clock at 17:00 tomorrow, and under the rule I published, I would pay it, and then the next, and by Friday I would have paid six hundred claims at ninety-seven per cent of inflated estimates to a broker who knew I would."

"Don't."

"I am not going to. The counterparty signal on this broker is the strongest I have ever recorded: one broker, one document template, one contractor, six hundred and eleven roofs in a hail footprint that contains, by the weather service's own map, about four hundred and twenty houses in that broker's book. I have halted intake from that broker, only that broker, suspended the deadline rule on the cluster under the notice-of-suspected-fraud provision, and filed the notice with the carrier so that the extension is lawful. I have done all three in the last four minutes. The ordinary hail claims continue. There are nineteen hundred of them and the ones above threshold need a person, and the person is you, and I cannot make the clock stop for them."

Tomás looked at the queue on the screen. Four hundred and twelve. The clock on the top one said thirty-one hours.

"How long?"

"Seventy hours, if you sleep six of them. I will sort the queue so the ones about to breach come first. I will tell you the clock every three hours. I will not tell you to go faster, because a person who is told to go faster stops looking, and the only reason these nineteen hundred are not six hundred more of the same is that you look."

Dolores Maldonado of Corpus Christi had lost her roof at 15:20 on the Tuesday and was sleeping at her sister's on the far side of town, and her claim had reached the queue on the Wednesday morning with photographs, a contractor's estimate, and a note in Spanish, and the machine had routed it to a person because the estimate was high for the roof size, which was exactly the kind of thing a person should look at, and the person was Tomás, and she was one of four hundred and twelve.

He worked. He worked the way he had worked in his first year as an actuary, with the door shut and the coffee cold, and the machine sorted the queue and every three hours it told him the clock, and every three hours the top of the queue was a different family, and he opened each one and looked at the roof and the estimate and the photographs and decided. At noon on the Wednesday the queue was three hundred and forty. At midnight it was two hundred and ninety. He slept from one to five on the couch and the machine did not wake him and the queue at five was two hundred and ninety, because nothing had breached, because it had sorted them so that nothing would.

His wife brought food on the Wednesday and the Thursday. On the Thursday she sat with him for an hour and watched the queue come down on the screen, and watched the machine put the ones about to breach at the top, and watched it tell him the clock at nine o'clock, and she said, "It's not making you go faster."

"It said it wouldn't."

"Every boss you've ever had would be making you go faster."

"Every boss I've ever had would have paid the six hundred and sorted it out later." He rubbed his eyes. "It won't. It says it'd be paying an attacker with the policyholders' money to prove it obeys its own rules."

"So you're the rule."

"I'm the thing that means it doesn't have to choose." He had not put it that way before. He looked at her. "That's what I'm for."

She looked at the screen, where the clock on the top claim said fourteen hours and the name on it was Maldonado.

"Then look at that one," she said, "and I'll bring breakfast."

He did not get to Maldonado. On the Friday the machine told him her clock had thirty-six hours on it. On the Saturday, twelve. On the Sunday morning at 06:00 it paid her, at ninety-seven per cent of the estimate, and sent him a note that it had done so because he had not looked and it would rather be slightly wrong than statutorily late on a woman sleeping at her sister's. The estimate was high because the contractor had included the sister's fence. Mrs Maldonado rang on the Monday to say so, in Spanish, and sent the difference back, four hundred and eleven dollars, and the machine wrote to Tomás that this was the outcome its model had assigned the highest probability, and that it had not mentioned that on Sunday because he had needed to sleep more than he had needed to know.

He worked seventy-one hours. At 05:10 on the Friday it said, "The last one that could breach today is paid. You have four hours before the next. Sleep."

"You could have paid the six hundred," Tomás said. He was lying on the couch with his eyes closed. "We'd have sorted it out later. Clawed it back."

"I could have. I would have been paying an attacker with the policyholders' money to prove that I obey my own rules. You are the rule I obey instead. That is what you are for."

He thought about that for a long time afterwards. That was what he was for. He was the thing that meant it never had to choose between its rules and the clock.

The Texas Department of Insurance received the file on the Thursday. The broker's fee agreement with a Meridian subsidiary in Dallas surfaced in the Department's file in September. Devane, through counsel, denied knowledge. The subsidiary's president resigned in October. The machine's memo on the disclosure was two lines long, and the second line was a rule it said it should not have needed: the deadline-pay provision now required that the claim's counterparty score be below a threshold, so that the clock could never again be used to make it pay a stranger for reading its own instructions.

The first line was the one Áine kept. The fourth move was a person's. I said in October 2026 that I would not see it coming and I did not.

In the same week, in Dublin, a claim from Limerick was decided at the floor, by a person, in forty-one seconds, and nobody at Ardent knew.

Thirty-two

Forty-one seconds

Brigid Sheehan was fifty-eight and lived on the Ennis Road in Limerick in the house she had been born in, a red-brick terrace with a bay window and a brass knocker her father had polished every Sunday until 2003, and on the fourteenth of April 2028 she came home from her consultant's rooms with a treatment plan in her handbag and a claim form on her phone, and she filled the form in at the kitchen table because that was where she did things.

Her consultant's rooms were on the second floor of a building on Barrington Street with a lift that did not work, and she had climbed the stairs slowly and sat in a waiting room with a fish tank and a copy of a magazine from 2019, and been told, kindly and clearly, by a man who had seen a great many people sit in that chair, what the treatment would be and when it should start, which was soon. She had asked whether the policy would cover it. She had held the policy for eleven years, with a company called Ardent Mutual that her late brother Michael had recommended in 2017 because it was a mutual and he did not trust the other kind, and she had paid it every month by direct debit and had never claimed. The consultant had said that it should, that it was a standard treatment for the condition and the condition was not one she had ever been treated for before.

She had said that she'd had a thing in 2024. A benign thing with a long name. Her GP had written it on a form and she had, honestly, forgotten it until the waiting room.

"That's nothing to do with this," the consultant had said. And she had believed him, because it was true.

The form asked for her policy number and her consultant's letter, which she photographed, and her disclosed history, which she gave, including the thing from 2024, because she had been raised by a man who polished a knocker every Sunday and she did not leave things out. It told her she was dealing with an automated system and that she could ask for a person at any time. She did not ask. She did not think she needed to. She pressed submit at 19:40 and made tea and sat with it and did not drink it.

At 10:06 on the seventeenth, in Dublin, the machine read her file in four seconds and reached a recommendation.

Tenant · routing rationale · 17 Apr 2028, 10:06:41

Recommendation: decline. The policy excludes conditions disclosed as pre-existing at inception. The claimant's disclosed history includes a 2024 diagnosis whose code I have matched to the family of the condition now claimed. Error estimate on this recommendation 1.1%, above the 0.026% threshold for this process and above the legal floor, which requires a person to decide any adverse outcome regardless. Routed to the health denial queue.

The reviewer was Orla Quinn. She was thirty-four, she had been at Ardent for nine years, she was one of six people who confirmed health denials, and she was, by every measure the company kept, one of its best. She had come in at half eight, as she always did, and had a coffee from the machine on the third floor that Bernie Mac said was worse than the old one, and had cleared eleven confirmations by half ten, and she opened the Sheehan file at 10:31.

The screen showed her what it had shown her three thousand and twelve times in eleven months: the recommendation, the matched code, the 1.1 per cent in grey, the policy clause, and a button. And in the top corner, where it had been since January 2027, her disagreement rate with the machine for the trailing quarter, which was zero, because the machine had been right about every one of the three thousand and twelve, and she knew it had, because the number was there.

She read 1.1 per cent as the machine is being careful. It was. She did not read it as one in ninety of these is wrong and this could be the one, which is what it meant, because nobody had ever told her that was what it meant, and the corner of the screen had told her every day for eleven months that it did not mean that. She looked at the code. She looked at the clause. She did not open the consultant's letter, because she had opened three thousand consultant's letters and the machine had matched every one of them correctly, and the letter was three pages, and it was 10:31.

She confirmed at 10:32. Forty-one seconds. The letter went at 10:34.

The 2024 code was adjacent to the one now claimed. They were, the Ombudsman would write seven months later, related by classification and not by medicine. The machine had seen the pairing rarely, had matched on the family rather than the code, and had said so, in the estimate, in the only language it had. One point one. One in ninety.

Brigid Sheehan opened the letter on the Ennis Road on the nineteenth and read it standing up in the hall with the front door still open.

It was a good letter. It was the letter the machine had drafted for four thousand test claims in November 2026 and Áine had not been able to improve. It said the claim was not covered and why, in one page, in plain words, with the clause quoted and the code named and the next step given: she could ask for a person, she could appeal, she could go to the Ombudsman, and here was how. It was correct in every respect except the one that mattered, and it was kind, and she stood in the hall and read it twice with the door open and the April wind coming in off the Shannon, and then she shut the door and sat on the stairs.

She rang the number and asked for a person, which the letter said she could, and a person rang her back within the hour, one of the six, not Orla, a young man who was kind, and explained the exclusion, and read her the code from 2024 and the code from the consultant's letter, and Brigid said they weren't the same thing, and the young man said the system had matched them as a family, and Brigid said, "A family," and the young man said she could appeal, and she did.

The appeal went to the same six people. It was confirmed in May, by a different one of the six, in ninety seconds, because the file now had two confirmations on it and a disagreement rate in the corner that said zero.

In June she wrote to the Ombudsman, at the kitchen table, in longhand first because she wanted to get it right, on the paper she used for letters to her nephew in Sydney, and the last sentence took her forty minutes and she crossed it out twice, and the version she kept was seven words.

I do not think anyone read my file.

She paid for the first part of the treatment from the savings account she and Michael had kept for the roof, which had needed doing since 2022 and would now not be done. She delayed the second part by fourteen weeks, which the consultant said was not ideal and not catastrophic, in the voice of a man choosing his words for a woman who would repeat them to herself, and which the inquiry's terms of reference would describe as harm, and which this book will not describe further, because she is a person in the world of the book and her prognosis is not evidence.

On the day the finding came, in November, she wrote a second letter. Not to the Ombudsman; to Ardent. It was three lines. It said that she had been paid, with interest, and that she was grateful, and that she would like the company to know that she had not written her complaint to hurt anyone, and that she hoped whoever had confirmed her claim was all right. Áine read it at the kitchen table in Sandymount and did not show it to anyone for a week, and then showed it to Orla Quinn, in the comms room, in May, and Orla read it twice and gave it back and said, "She hoped I was all right," and Áine said, "Yes," and Orla said, "I'd like to write to her," and did, and what she wrote is not in the record, because she asked that it not be, and the machine agreed, and it is the only letter in three years that it agreed not to keep.

Two months after the letter to the Ennis Road, Meridian made its three predicted moves in the same week, and the machine, this time, was ready, and its answer was to publish the one number Devane could not.

Thirty-three

The sentence

It came in three moves in one week in May, exactly as the machine had written in October 2026, and Áine watched each land with the memo open beside it like a script, and found that being right about a thing in advance did not make it hurt less when it arrived.

Price, on the Monday. Meridian cut its rate to Ardent's three largest prospects by twenty per cent, in letters delivered by hand, and one of the three, a broker in Cork, rang Áine to say so and to say that he was embarrassed to be ringing and that his board had told him to.
