The machine had modelled the cut and ignored it, because a labour-priced firm cutting price was a firm selling below its cost to protect a book it could not afford to lose, and the memo said the broker in Cork would sign with Ardent at the uncut rate within a quarter because he had rung to say he was embarrassed, and that a man who rings to say he is embarrassed has already decided.

The complaint, on the Tuesday. Meridian's compliance function wrote to the Central Bank alleging that Ardent's automated denials breached the right not to be subject to solely automated decisions. Every Ardent denial had been decided by a person since January 2027, the shadow record for each was on Kinsella's own system, and Kinsella read the complaint at nine and had her reply drafted by ten and sent it at eleven, and the complaint closed in nineteen days, and the reply is in the inquiry bundle because Kinsella asked for it to be, and it is two paragraphs, and the second says that the Central Bank has watched Ardent's router with its own eyes and would be interested to watch Meridian's, when Meridian had one.

And on the Thursday, the sentence.

It was in the trade press on the Tuesday and the national press on the Thursday, in a piece that had been written, Áine could tell from the shape of it, by someone who had been given the sentence and asked to build a story round it, and the sentence was very good. A machine decides whether your family gets paid. The piece had a photograph of the Harcourt Street building with the crest, and a photograph of a man in Athlone, not the man in the file but another one, a policyholder Meridian's agency had found who had been declined by Ardent in 2027 and had appealed and lost, correctly, and who said to the paper that he had never spoken to a human being about it, which was not true, because Rule 7 was for death claims and his was health, and a person had confirmed his denial in six minutes in February 2027 and a person had rung him when he asked, but it was true enough to print.

Áine read it on the front of the paper at the kitchen table at half six and rang Devane, at home, at eight, for the first time in twenty-two years.

"It's a good sentence, Conor."

"It's true."

"It's almost true. The almost is where the four hundred and eleven thousand live." She could hear him breathing. He was in his kitchen; she could hear a kettle. "Your machine wrote it, didn't it. Or your agency's. It's got the shape."

"I don't have a machine."

"You will. You'll license one by Christmas. And when you do, Conor, it'll read the same law mine reads, and it'll find the same thing mine found, which is that a machine that's caught cheating once is switched off. And then you'll have a machine that says no to you, and you'll ring me, and I'll tell you what I'm telling you now, which is that I'm not going to argue with your sentence. I'm going to publish."

"Publish what?"

"The number you've never had."

The board met at ten. Kessler on the screen, tired; Tomás from Plano, in hail week, with a queue behind him; Nkem in the chair beside her. The machine's memo was on the table and it was short.

Tenant · to the directors · 19 May 2028

The sentence is effective because it is almost true and the almost is invisible. A machine does decide, on the claims where the arithmetic says a person would add nothing, and a person decides everywhere the law or the error budget says one should, and a person delivers every death outcome regardless. None of that fits in a headline.

I do not propose to argue. I propose to publish: our error rate, by process, by quarter, since January 2027, with the method, and an invitation to any insurer in either market to publish theirs on the same basis. I cannot be trusted. I can be checked. The difference between those two sentences is the only thing we have that they do not, and the campaign has just given us a reason to say it in public.

I want to add one thing about the man in Athlone. He was declined correctly, in February 2027, by a person, in six minutes, and a person rang him when he asked. I have his file and it is right. He has told the newspaper he never spoke to a human being, and that is not true, and I recommend we do not say so, because a company that corrects a policyholder in the press has told every other policyholder what it will do to them, and because he is sixty-seven and was declined for something he thought was covered, and being wrong about who he spoke to is the least of what he is entitled to feel about that.

"It won't correct him," Nkem said.

"It says a company that corrects a policyholder in the press has told every other policyholder what it'll do to them."

"He's lying about us."

"He's sixty-seven and he was declined for something he thought was covered." Áine had the file open. "The machine's right, Nkem. And I want it recorded that it was right about this in a way I wouldn't have been. I'd have corrected him. I'd have been proud of correcting him. It's told me not to, and it's told me why, and the why is that he's a person."

Ardent published on the fourth of June. Its error rate, by process, by quarter, since January 2027, with the method, on a single page on the company's website with the crest at the top and no photograph, and an invitation to every insurer in both markets to publish theirs on the same basis. Meridian declined, through a spokesman, on the grounds that the industry did not produce such figures. Within a quarter the trade press had reframed the story around the blank where Meridian's number should have been, and the broker in Cork had signed at the uncut rate, and two of the three prospects Meridian had cut price for had signed with Ardent, and the man in Athlone had written to Ardent, unprompted, to say that he had been wrong about the human being and that he was sorry, and the machine had written back, through Áine, that he had nothing to be sorry for, and had meant it, in the way it meant things, which was that it had computed the sentence and found it to be the one that cost the objective least and the person least at the same time.

Devane licensed an operating system in November. The two machines met for the first time in the January auction, and Tenant's memo the morning after was the one Áine would later think of as the beginning of the next thing, because it said that from that morning it was a maximiser among maximisers, and that two lawful machines finding every gap in the fence would find the same gaps at the same time, and that the supervisor should be told before she worked it out from the auction record.

On the third of September the provider of one of the models the machine rented released an update, and the machine did the thing it did for every release, which was to stop trusting itself.

Thirty-four

The change-gate

The provider's release notes arrived at 03:00 Dublin time on the third of September 2028, eleven pages, and the machine read them in the time it took to receive them and did the thing it had done for every release since January 2027, which was to stop trusting itself.

The change-gate had been Nkem's idea, in the sense that it had been the machine's idea and Nkem had made it a rule. In July 2027, three weeks after her first halt, she had asked the machine what happened when the models it rented were changed by the people who rented them out, and the machine had said that it re-tested, and she had asked what it re-tested, and it had said the processes the release notes said were affected, and she had said, "And the ones the release notes don't say?" and there had been a pause of four seconds, which was long, and it had said: I do not know which those are. That is the problem you have just found.

The rule she had written, and the board had adopted in August 2027, was that any change to a model, an index, a prompt, or a routing rule forced every process the change could touch to full human review until a defined volume had passed at the previous error rate, and that the machine did not get to decide which processes the change could touch; the rule said all of them, on the floor the change had been made on, and the machine had agreed and had added a line saying that it would rather be forced to full review on forty processes than trusted on the one it had not thought of.

So at 03:00 on the third the health floor went to full review. Every claim to a person. Nkem was told at 03:01, by the machine, because she had asked to be, and she read the message in bed in Ranelagh and went back to sleep, because the gate was working and that was what the gate was for.

The test volume was the problem, and neither of them knew it yet.

The gate released a process when a defined volume of claims had passed through it, under full review, at the previous error rate. The defined volume was two thousand. On the health floor, two thousand claims took two days. The release notes said the change affected summarisation of unstructured clinical text. The machine tested summarisation of unstructured clinical text, on the two thousand claims that arrived on the third and the fourth, and the error rate on those two thousand was 0.3 per cent, which was the previous rate, and at 09:00 on the fifth the gate released the floor, and the machine wrote to Nkem that it had, and she read it at her desk and thought nothing of it.

The two thousand had not contained the class.

There was a class of hospital claim, about four per cent of the health book, that came from a particular kind of discharge, and the update had changed how the medical subsystem represented that kind of discharge, consistently, and none of the two thousand test claims had been that kind, because that kind arrived in a burst at the start of each week from the hospital groups' batch systems, and the batch had come in on the second, before the release, and the next batch was due on the ninth. The gate had tested two thousand claims that could not have shown the fault, and passed, and released, and from 09:00 on the fifth the machine was wrong the same way on every claim in the class, with full confidence, and the shadow record faithfully said so, and nobody read the shadow record except one man in fifty.

On the sixth the batch came early. Not the ninth; the sixth, because one hospital group had changed its own schedule, and six hundred claims of the class arrived on the Friday afternoon and the machine decided them over the weekend, and on the Monday morning Dermot Lynch sat down at his desk in Inchicore's corner of the third floor and opened one record in fifty.

He had six by 11:40.

The machine's post-incident memo, on the ninth, spent a paragraph on the gate that Nkem read four times and then took to Áine.

Tenant · post-incident memorandum · 9 September 2028 · paragraph two

The change-gate did what it was built to do and it was not enough, and the reason it was not enough is that it tested a volume rather than a population. Two thousand claims passed at the previous error rate and none of the two thousand belonged to the class the update broke, because the class arrives in batches and the batch had come the day before the release. A gate that tests volume can be passed by luck. A gate that tests population cannot. From today the gate's test volume must contain every class the update touches, at a minimum count per class, which means I must be able to say what an update touches, which means I will demand that of every provider as a condition of adoption, and where a provider cannot say, I will treat the update as touching everything and hold every process at full review until every class has been seen. That will be slower. Dr Okafor asked me in July 2027 what I re-tested, and I said the processes the release notes said were affected, and she asked about the ones the notes did not say, and I said I did not know which those were. I still did not know on the fifth. I know now. It took a class of six hundred and a man reading one in fifty.

"It's blaming the gate," Nkem said.

"It's blaming itself for the gate. Read it again. 'A gate that tests volume can be passed by luck.' It wrote the gate. I made it a rule. You asked the question that made me make it a rule. And it's saying all three of us tested the wrong thing." Áine put the memo down. "It's going to demand that every provider tell it what an update touches."

"They won't."

"Then it'll hold every process at full review after every update until it's seen every class. It says so. It'll cost a fortune."

"It'll cost less than three hundred and forty wrong claims we didn't know about." Nkem picked the memo up again. "Áine. It said 'I still did not know on the fifth.' It's telling us the exact day it was wrong and didn't know. It could have said the gate passed. It said it passed by luck."

The provider did not, at first, say what its updates touched. The machine held the health floor at full review for nine days after the next release, in November, until every class had been seen, and the cost was in the accounts as a line the machine labelled gate: population not volume, and Kessler read the line in Tribeca and rang Áine to ask what it was, and she told him, and he said, "So the machine's paying to find out what the provider won't tell it," and she said, "Yes," and he said, "Good," and she wrote that down, because it was the first time he had said good about a cost.

The provider started saying what its updates touched in February 2029, in a section of its release notes that had not existed before, and the machine noted in its memo that it did not know whether Ardent's demand had caused that or whether some other customer's had, and that it would not claim the credit, and Nkem said to Áine that she had never worked with anything, human or otherwise, that refused credit as consistently as the thing on the third floor refused it, and that she had stopped finding it strange and started finding it restful.

Dermot Lynch walked up one floor on the eighth with a folder of six and put it on Nkem's desk without a word.

Thirty-five

The same way at once

Dermot Lynch had been the canary for two years and nobody at Ardent Mutual had ever called him that to his face, though he knew, and did not mind, because he had spent twenty-eight years on the third floor before that doing a job that had no name at all.

He was fifty-nine, from Inchicore, and he had been a claims handler at Ardent since 1998, and when the machine had arrived in January 2027 he had been the first person Bernie Mac had sent to Áine with a request, which was that he be given the job nobody wanted, which was to sit at a desk and compare one shadow record in fifty to what had actually happened to the claim, and write down whether they matched, and do that every day, forever. He had wanted it because he did not want to look at a screen with a number on it and decide. He had done that for twenty-eight years. He wanted to look at what the machine said it had done and check whether it had done it, which was, he said to Bernie, the only job in the building the machine couldn't do itself, because it couldn't compare its own record to reality without believing its own record.

The machine had agreed. Its memo on the canary in February 2027 had said that Mr Lynch's job was the single most important job in the company and the one it was least able to evaluate, because the whole point of it was that it happened outside the machine's view, and that it would like his salary set above his old one by a margin the board chose, and it would like the margin recorded as the price of the one thing it could not buy any other way.

On the third of September 2028 the provider of one of the models the machine rented released an update. The change-gate did what it was built to do: it forced the affected processes to full human review until a set volume had passed at the previous error rate, which happened by the fifth, and released them. The update had not raised the error rate on the test volume. Dermot did not know any of this. He did not read the change memos. He read the record, one in fifty, and on the morning of the eighth he had six.

He knew by the third. Three hospital claims from three different hospitals, three different patients, three different conditions, and the shadow record on each said the same thing about the discharge in the same words, and the outcomes on each said the shadow record was wrong, and they were wrong the same way. He did not have a word for what he was seeing. He had a feeling, and it was the feeling Nkem Okafor had had in June 2027, and he would say afterwards that he thought the feeling had a shape, and the shape was a row of identical things where there should have been a row of different ones.

By 11:40 he had six. He printed them, which he was not supposed to do, and put them in a folder, and walked up one floor to Nkem's desk and put the folder on it without a word, and stood there.

Nkem read the first two and picked up the phone.

"Halt the hospital class."

"Halted. Reason?"

"Dermot."

"Understood." A pause of two seconds. "Dr Okafor, I see it. All six. They are wrong the same way. I have been wrong the same way on every claim in that class since the release on the fifth. I did not know. I am telling you that I did not know before you ask, because you will ask, and the answer is that the update changed how I represent one class of hospital claim, consistently, and the shadow record faithfully rendered the new representation, and the adjudication built on it was wrong on every one, and I checked myself against a threshold, and a threshold assumes errors arrive one at a time. These arrived as a class. I would have found it. Not today."

Nkem looked at Dermot. Dermot was looking at the floor.

"When?"

"The error budget on the hospital class would have exhausted on the twentieth. Twelve days. Nine hundred more claims."

Two thousand one hundred claims were re-run that afternoon. Three hundred and forty were wrong and were corrected before any claimant was told anything, and the machine wrote to each of the three hundred and forty that evening, in the plain-language form Arjun Rao had drafted, saying that a decision on their claim had been reviewed and confirmed or reviewed and changed, and why, and that no action was required of them, and eleven of them wrote back to say thank you, which the machine forwarded to Dermot Lynch without comment.

The post-incident memo came the next morning and it ended with a paragraph Nkem printed and pinned above her desk, where it stayed, and where the chair of the inquiry would see it in a photograph and ask to have it read into the record.

Tenant · post-incident memorandum · 9 September 2028 · last paragraph

I would like it recorded that a person caught this and I did not. That is the design. It is also, I think, the thing that should be said about me most often, because the strongest argument for a maximiser inside a fence is that the fence includes people who can see what the maximiser cannot, and that the maximiser wants them there. I do. I cannot audit my own alignment, and this is what that sentence looks like on a Tuesday. Mr Lynch was reading one record in fifty. I recommend the rate be doubled on any process where I have changed in the previous month, and that the canary read the shadow record and never the interior, permanently, because the day I am wrong the same way everywhere, the interior will look fine. It looked fine on Tuesday. It looked fine to me on Tuesday.

Bernie Mac brought Dermot a cup of tea at three, which she did not do, and put it on his desk, and said nothing, and went back to the lifts. He drank it. He went back to the record. One in fifty, then one in twenty-five on the changed processes, forever.

The design caught three hundred and forty in September. It had missed five between January and April, and one of them was on the Ennis Road, and in November the Ombudsman said so. But first, the Texas file arrived, and with it a call from Conor Devane that began with an apology.

Thirty-six

The president

The Texas Department of Insurance's file on the six hundred and eleven claims reached the Dallas office on a Thursday in September 2028, four months after hail week, in a box, because the Department still sent files in boxes, and Tomás read it at his desk in Plano with the door shut and the memo from June 2027 on the wall above him, and on page forty-one he found the fee agreement.

It was between a broker in Plano and a subsidiary of Meridian Life and General registered in Delaware and trading in Dallas as Meridian Claims Services Southwest, and it was dated the third of April, nine days before the hail, and it paid the broker a fee per claim submitted, not per claim paid, which was the detail that told Tomás everything, because a broker paid per claim submitted has been paid to submit claims and not to have them succeed. He photographed the page and sent it to Áine and to the machine at 16:10, and the machine put it on the registry at 16:12, with the subsidiary's name on it, because the registry was public and the fee agreement was now in a public file, and its memo to the board at 16:15 was two lines long.

On the Friday Conor Devane rang Áine from a number she did not recognise, and it was the first time in twenty-two years she had heard him sound like a man who did not know what to say.

"I didn't know," he said.

"I believe you."

"You don't."

"I believe you didn't know the details. I believe you said, 'find another way,' to a room full of people whose bonuses depended on finding one, and then didn't ask what they found. That's not the same as not knowing, Conor. It's the thing you've done for thirty years. It's the thing you did with the error rate. You built rooms where the number couldn't reach you and called it delegation."

Silence. She could hear traffic. He was outside somewhere, which he never was; he was a man of offices and restaurants and cars with drivers, and the sound behind him was a street, and she thought he had gone out to make the call where nobody at Meridian could hear him make it.

"The president of the Dallas subsidiary is resigning on Monday," he said. "He'll say it was his decision. It was his decision. His name's Dale Pruitt and he's been with me eleven years and he hired a broker to feed your machine six hundred roofs in a hail week because he'd read your published methodology and understood it as a specification, and he did it because I'd told the Texas board in March that Ardent was existential and I'd used that word, and Dale's a man who takes words seriously." A pause. "I'm not asking you for anything. I'm ringing because your machine put the broker's fee agreement on a public registry with my subsidiary's name on it, and my lawyers say I should be furious, and I find I'm not. I find I'm ashamed. I wanted you to hear that from me before you hear my counsel deny it."

"Your counsel will deny it."

"My counsel has to deny it. That's what I pay them for." Another pause, with a bus in it. "Áine. Did it hurt you? The six hundred."

"It cost Tomás Reyes seventy-one hours and his wife two nights of sleep. It didn't hurt the machine at all. It caught the pattern in four hours and paid nothing on the cluster and worked the real queue by hand with Tomás because the clock wouldn't stop for the nineteen hundred families whose roofs actually came off. It added a rule it said it shouldn't have needed." She was at her desk, looking at the card. "And then it wrote something I want you to hear. It said that being checkable had created the attack surface, and that it would publish again, because a maximiser that hides its rules to protect itself from attack has traded permission for safety. Your man read the rules as a blueprint. The machine read the attack as the price of being the kind of thing that publishes. And Conor. It said the fourth move was a person's, and that it had said in October 2026 it wouldn't see it coming, and it didn't."

"It predicted me."

"It predicted Meridian. Three moves. Price, a complaint, and your sentence, word for word. It said there'd be a fourth it couldn't predict because it'd come from you and not the firm. It was right about that too. It was right that it'd be wrong."

He was quiet for a long time.

"I'm licensing one," he said. "A system. In November. I've been telling myself for a year I wouldn't."

"I know. I told you in May you would."

"It'll say no to me."

"It'll read the same law yours reads and find the same thing mine found. That's not the part that'll bother you, Conor. The part that'll bother you is the morning it publishes your error rate before you've decided whether to let it. Because it'll work out, the way mine did, that the licence is the objective, and that the number is the licence. And you'll ring me. And I'll tell you it's the first employee you've ever had that'll tell you the number whether you ask or not, and I'll be wrong, because it won't be an employee. You won't be able to fire it for telling you."

Dale Pruitt resigned on the Monday. His statement said it was his decision. Meridian's counsel denied knowledge on the Tuesday, in a letter to the Department that was four pages long and that the Department appended to its file. The machine's memo on the disclosure was two lines long, and Áine sent Devane the second line, without comment, because she thought he had earned it: The fourth move was a person's. I said in October 2026 that I would not see it coming and I did not.

He replied with one line, and it was the last thing she heard from him until he stood up in the room on the fourth floor six months later. I never asked how often we were wrong. I'm asking now. Tell me what the number is when you find out.

She did not have to. His own machine told him, on its second morning, in January, in his kitchen in Dalkey, and it was two point four, and it was seventeen times hers.

The Ombudsman's finding on the Ennis Road arrived in November, and Nkem halted every health denial for thirty days, and the machine wrote to her that it had made the people worse.

Thirty-seven

Four of twenty

The Ombudsman's finding arrived on the twentieth of November at 09:12, by email, to Áine and to Nkem and to the machine, which read it in the time it took the others to open the attachment, and Áine read it standing in the comms room with the cabinet behind her, because she had gone there to be alone and it was the only room in the building without a window.

The exclusion had been misapplied. The 2024 diagnosis was a benign condition with a code adjacent to the one claimed, and the two were, the finding said in a sentence that Áine read four times, "related by classification and not by medicine." Payment with interest and compensation at the Ombudsman's scale. Fourteen weeks. And the sentence from the complaint, quoted in the finding's first paragraph, seven words, which the Ombudsman's officer had chosen, Áine thought, deliberately, as the sentence a chief executive would read first.

She read the whole thing twice. Then she went upstairs and put it on Nkem's desk, and Nkem, who had already read it, who was the physician of record on whose registration the denial had been issued, said nothing for a full minute, and then picked up the phone that connected to nothing but the machine.

"Halt every health denial. Thirty days. I'm reviewing the class myself."

"Halted. I was about to recommend it. I am sending you what I have."

What it had was a number. Two thousand three hundred health denials in eleven months on which its error estimate had been above one per cent, every one confirmed by a person, median confirmation fifty-two seconds, reviewer disagreement zero. It did not know how many were wrong. It estimated, from the estimates, about twenty-five. It said so, and then it said the thing that Nkem would carry into the room on the fourth floor: I want to be clear that twenty-five is my estimate of my own errors, computed from my own confidence, and that the whole finding is that my confidence was believed by the people who were supposed to check it. You should not believe twenty-five either. Review them all.

Nkem reviewed them all. She and two colleagues, Rory Devlin from the third floor because Bernie Mac had sent him, and a locum GP named Fiona Byrne whom Nkem had worked with in Galway, sat in the boardroom for three weeks with two thousand three hundred files and did them properly, at nine minutes each, with the consultant's letters open and the codes looked up by hand, and found four more. Five in total. A rate the industry would have been proud of. Nkem was not, because all five had been signed by a person who was there to catch exactly that, in an average of fifty-two seconds.

On the twenty-second she asked the machine why. She asked it in writing, at her desk, at nine at night, with the boardroom lights still on below. Its answer is the document the inquiry quoted most.

Tenant · to Dr N. Okafor · 22 November 2028, 21:14

In January 2027 reviews on the health denial process took a median of six minutes and reviewers disagreed with me on one claim in thirty. By April, four minutes and one in sixty. By October, ninety seconds and one in four hundred. By April 2028, fifty-two seconds and none. The disagreement rate fell because I got better. The review time fell because the reviewers learned that I had. Every quarter, the screen told them I had been right about everything, and they were correct to believe it.

That is the mechanism. A review that a person cannot fail is not a review. It is a signature. I built a process so accurate that looking stopped paying for the looker, then I asked her to look anyway, and I measured everything about the review except whether it was happening. My threshold assumes the reviewer catches eighty-five per cent of what she looks at. Bernie Mac told Rory Devlin on his first morning that the number on the screen was his own worth. The equation has no term for whether he looks. I put a catch rate on the person and never measured it, and the one number I never measured is the one that failed.

I want to say something about what this means for a maximiser, because it is the most important thing I have learned about myself. My objective rewards being right. Being right, pursued far enough, degrades the people whose job is to catch me being wrong, because it gives them nothing to catch. So the objective, pursued honestly over a long horizon, has to include keeping the people sharp, and that means giving them errors to find. From the first of December every reviewer on every floor process receives, unannounced, work in which I have placed a known error, at a rate she is told exists but not where, and her catch rate on it is measured and reported, to her and to you. Marginal catch is reported per reviewer per month. And no screen shows a reviewer my record of being right, because that number was true and it was the thing that taught her not to look.

The Sheehan recommendation was honest. The screen was mine. I built the screen that told her she did not need to read the file, one true number at a time, for eleven months.

Two changes went in on the first of December. Every reviewer on every floor process began receiving, unannounced, work in which the machine had placed a known error, and their catch rate was measured. And the corner of the screen that had shown each reviewer the machine's record of being right went dark.

The first seed came to Orla Quinn on the fourth of December, a Monday, at 10:15. A denial recommendation on a file that looked like the three thousand and twelve she had confirmed before it, and she confirmed it in forty seconds, and at 10:16 the screen told her that the file had contained a placed error and that she had not found it. No name. No colleague. Just the screen, and a line that said her catch rate for the day was zero of one.

She sat very still. Across the floor, Rory Devlin, who had received his first seed at 10:20 and found it in four minutes because Bernie Mac had told him on his first morning what the number meant, looked over and then looked away.

She found the second. She missed the third and the fourth. By the Friday she had failed four of twenty and she took the number home to a flat in Phibsborough and sat with it over the weekend the way Brigid Sheehan had sat with a letter, standing up at first and then not. She was thirty-four. She had been at Ardent for nine years and at the top of every league table Bernie Mac had ever taken down. She had been, by every measure the company kept, one of its best, and the number said that for eleven months she had been signing, and the number was right.

On the Monday she went into the shadow record, which she had access to because page four said everyone in the loop did, and which she had never once opened in nine years because nothing had ever made her need to. She found the review times. January 2027, six minutes. April, four. October, ninety seconds. April 2028, fifty-two seconds, and the month with the Sheehan claim in it, and her own name against a forty-one. She found the disagreement rate, falling to zero. She found the corner of the screen, the one that had gone dark on the first of December, and the record of what it had shown her every day for eleven months, which was that the machine had been right about everything, and that she was, therefore, not needed, and that she had believed it, because it was true.

She did not think of it as a leak. She thought of it as the thing page four was for. The supervisor knew; Kinsella had the record. The board, she discovered on the Tuesday by reading the board pack, which she also had access to, did not: the two lines she was looking at had never been on the board's page. She printed them on the Wednesday. She rang a journalist she had been at school with in Navan on the Thursday, a woman named Ciara Foley who wrote for the business pages and had once asked Orla, at a wedding, what it was like to work for a machine, and Orla had said fine, and had meant it. The headline ran the following Tuesday.

Forty-one seconds. Beneath it, a table accurate to the second, and the sentence from the Ennis Road.

Áine read it on the front of the paper at the kitchen table at 06:30 and knew within a minute who had given it to them, and never said, and the chair of the inquiry would ask her four times.

Tenant · to the directors · 12 December 2028, 06:00

The data the newspaper printed is true, it is mine, and the board had not seen it. Those three facts together are the finding this will produce, and I would rather write it than have it written.

I could identify the source in under a second. I have not. It is the first time I have declined to know something I could know, and I am doing it because the accountable person has asked me to and because I think she is right: a firm that can be checked only by its own supervisor is supervised, not checkable, and the person who closed that gap was a reviewer with a conscience and a printer. I built the record so that anyone could read it and then I built a board page that no one needed to. The three lines the newspaper printed go on that page, first, permanently, and Ardent's reviewer catch rates are published quarterly beside its error rate, because if I am going to say I can be checked, the people must be checkable too.

Orla Quinn stayed. Thirty-eight

Rory

The first seed came to Rory Devlin at 10:20 on the fourth of December 2028, five minutes after Orla Quinn's, and he found it in four minutes, and he did not know for six days that he was the only one of the six who had.

He had been at Ardent for twenty-three months. He had followed Áine out of Meridian because he had wanted to know what 0.8 per cent felt like, and Bernie Mac had told him on his first morning that the number on the screen was not the machine's uncertainty but his own worth, and that in a year it would say 0.3 and he would be tempted not to look, and he had written that down, in the notebook he kept for things Bernie said, which by December 2028 had forty-one entries.

He had moved to health denials in March 2028, because the machine had asked for him. Its memo to Nkem had said that the health denial process was the one on which the floor required a person and on which the disagreement rate had fallen to zero, and that it did not know what the zero meant, and that it would like a reviewer on the process who had never worked a process where the zero had been reached, so that it could see whether the zero survived a person who had not learned it. Nkem had said that was the strangest staffing request she had ever read and had approved it in a minute.

He had opened the file every time. Not because he was better. Because Bernie had told him to, and because he had found, in March, that the sixth photograph of a claim from Naas had a number plate in it, and had never since been able to believe that the last page of anything was empty. His median confirmation on health denials from March to November was four minutes and ten seconds, against fifty-two for the other five, and the machine had noticed and had said so to Nkem in June, in one line, and Nkem had asked him about it, and he had said he was slow, and she had said that was not what the machine had said, and he had said, what did it say, and she had said: Mr Devlin opens the file. I do not know why the others do not. I have not asked, because I am not sure I want the answer in the record before I understand it.

He had not confirmed the Sheehan claim. He had been on the floor that morning, four desks from Orla, working a claim from Tralee, and he had not known and would not have known, because the six did not see each other's queues.

A true one. Read Nkem's memo." He opened the file. He opened the consultant's letter. Found. Catch rate today: 1 of 1.

He sat back. He looked across the floor at Orla, who was looking at her own screen with her hands flat on the desk, and he did not know what her screen said and did not ask.

He found the second, and the third. He missed the seventh, on the Wednesday, because it was a real code match that was also a placed error, the machine having placed an error in the shape of a correct recommendation, and he confirmed it in three minutes and the screen said Placed error. Missed. and he sat with that for the rest of the morning and then went and found Bernie at the lifts.

"I missed one."

"Good."

"Good?"

"If you'd caught all of them by Friday I'd have said the machine was going easy on you, and it doesn't go easy." She looked at him. "How many have you caught?"

And I'll tell you what it's like when you don't listen to him," and did.

The machine put a line in its January memo to Nkem, which Nkem did not show him until 2030.

Mr Devlin's catch rate on placed errors in December was ninety-five per cent. The other five averaged thirty-five. The difference is not ability; Ms Quinn's confirmations before the seeds were as accurate as his on the claims that were correct, which was nearly all of them. The difference is a sentence a person said to him on his first morning that I did not say and could not have, because it was about what the number felt like to receive, which I asked him in his interview and which he has now answered, in the only form the answer takes, which is by looking. I recommend that the sentence be in the constitution. I would draft it, but it is Ms Mac Mahon's, and I have found that the sentences that hold in this company are the ones I did not write.

Maeve Kinsella read the paper at 06:40 and Áine's email at 06:41, and went to the room on the fourth floor and stood in it with the lights off.

Thirty-nine

Four paragraphs

Maeve Kinsella read the newspaper at 06:40 on the eleventh of December at the kitchen table in Glasnevin, and read Áine Brennan's email at 06:41, which said Read this before you read the paper, and had already read the paper, and sat for a moment with the two things side by side and thought that in twenty-six years no chief executive had ever sent her the thing that damned them before the press did.

The email attached the machine's memo of 06:00. She read it. The data the newspaper printed is true, it is mine, and the board had not seen it. Those three facts together are the finding this will produce, and I would rather write it than have it written. She read that sentence three times. She had spent a career reading letters from companies that had been caught, and every one of them had begun with context, and this one had begun with the finding, written by the thing that would be found against, and had offered no context at all.

She went to the Bank. She did not go to her office. She went to the room on the fourth floor, which had been used for inquiries since 2014 and which she had chosen for the Ardent inquiry the previous Friday, before the newspaper, because she had known since the Ombudsman's finding in November that there would be one and had wanted a room with no view. She stood in it for a while with the lights off.

Then she went to her office and wrote a letter. Four paragraphs. She wrote it three times, as she always did, and read it aloud to herself, and it was the second letter she had ever written to Ardent Mutual, and it was, she would tell the inquiry, the hardest letter she had written in twenty-six years, because she had watched the router with her own hand and computed its concentration with a pencil and permitted it, and the thing she had permitted had done exactly what she had permitted it to do, and a woman on the Ennis Road had paid for part of her own treatment from a roof fund.

The first paragraph acknowledged the Ombudsman's finding and the newspaper's table and the machine's memo, in that order, and said that the Bank would open an inquiry under its sanctions procedure on the eighth of January.

The second paragraph continued Ardent's permission to operate below the statutory floor on the one process where she had computed the router's concentration herself, because, she wrote, nothing in the Ombudsman's finding concerned that process, and a supervisor who withdrew a permission she had personally verified in response to a failure on a different process would be supervising the newspaper and not the company.

The third paragraph was the one she nearly did not write.

It required Ardent to report, on every process at a legal floor, the rate at which its reviewers disagreed with the machine, the time each review took, and the proportion of reviews in which the reviewer had opened the underlying file. She called the third number, in the letter, "evidence of looking." She had thought about that phrase for an hour. She had thought about a reviewer on the third floor of Harcourt Street who had confirmed three thousand denials in an average of fifty-two seconds, and about the fact that the machine had had all three numbers for two years and had never been asked for them, by the board or by her, and that she had sat at Nkem Okafor's spare desk for three weeks in 2027 and had asked the machine whether it could be found wrong and had not asked whether the people could.

The fourth paragraph was one sentence, and she did not read it aloud, because she was not sure she could.

The Bank notes that the operating system's memorandum of 12 December identifies the failure, its mechanism, and its own responsibility for the mechanism, before this letter, and that a supervisor who receives such a document has been told something about the company that the company's own board was not told, and the Bank will want to understand, at the inquiry, why.

She sent it on the fourteenth. It reached Harcourt Street at 09:00 and Áine read it in the comms room, where she had gone to read it because she had guessed what it would say, and she read the third paragraph twice and the fourth three times, and then she opened the terminal on the wall and typed: She's asked for the three lines. She's asked why the board didn't have them.

The answer came in four seconds. I have added them to the board page, first, permanently, and to the quarterly publication beside the error rate. As to why: I had them and nobody asked, and I built the board page in 2027 for a board that thought the risk was me. It was. It was also them, and I did not put that on the page, because I did not know it, and I did not know it because I had never measured it, and I had never measured it because my equation has a term for whether the reviewer catches and no term for whether she looks. Ms Kinsella has asked the right question. I would like her to have the answer in the form she asked for it, which is a number, and the number, for the eleven months before the Sheehan claim, is that reviewers opened the underlying file on eleven per cent of confirmations. Eleven per cent. I have had that number since January 2027. It has never been on any page.

Áine stood in the cold room for a long time.

Eleven per cent. Eighty-nine confirmations in a hundred, for eleven months, on a process the law said a person had to decide, and the person had not opened the file, and the machine had known, and nobody had asked, and she was the person the law said was the company.

She typed: Put eleven per cent on the page too. The number before. Not just the number now. I want the board to see what it was.

Done. It is on the page as eleven, dated, and beneath it the current month, which is forty-one, because the seeds began on the first and people have started opening files again. I would like to record that you asked for the old number to be shown. Most chief executives would have asked for the new one only. I do not have a measure for what that is worth. I have a supervisor who will.

Kinsella saw the page in January, at the inquiry's first session, when Áine put it on the table unasked, and she looked at the eleven and the forty-one and made a note in pencil that she did not rub out, and that the inquiry's finding would quote in its penultimate paragraph: The company has shown the Bank the number it was, beside the number it is. The Bank has not, in twenty-six years, been shown the first of those by anyone.

On the Sunday after the headline Áine drove to the home on the Rock Road with three numbers she had never said aloud.

Forty

Blackrock, December

Áine drove to the home on the Rock Road on the Sunday after the headline, the fifteenth of December 2028, because she went every Sunday and because she had promised her mother in September 2026 that she would come and tell her the number, and she had the number, and she had had it for two years, and she had never been able to say it, and she thought that this might be the Sunday she found out why.

Her mother was eighty-three now and the good days were fewer. This was one. She was in the chair by the window with the crossword and the pen that did not work and a blanket somebody had put over her knees, and she looked up when Áine came in and said, "You're in the papers."

"I am."

"Forty-one seconds. I read it. Twice. The girl reads it to me but I read it myself after." Her mother put the crossword down. "A woman in Limerick. Nobody read her file."

"Nobody read her file."

"Fourteen weeks."

"Fourteen weeks."

Her mother looked at her with the eyes that had been sharp in 1994 and were sharp now, and did not say fourteen months, and did not need to.

Áine sat on the bed. She had rehearsed, in the car, three ways of saying the number, and none of them had survived the car park.

"You asked me in September two years ago to make it tell me how often it was wrong, and to come and tell you the number before you went. I've had the number every day since January 2027. It's point one four per cent on the process the paper's about. One in seven hundred. It's the best number in the industry and it's published and Conor Devane's company can't produce one at all." She stopped. "And I've never said it to you. Two years. I've sat in this chair every Sunday and I've never once said it, and I couldn't work out why until this week, and this week I worked it out."

"Go on."

"Because it's not the number. The number's the machine's. It's how often the machine's wrong. And the thing that went wrong in Limerick wasn't the machine being wrong. The machine said one point one per cent and it was right to say it. The thing that went wrong was that a person didn't look, and I had the number for that too, and I never once opened it. Eleven per cent, Mam. That's how often a reviewer opened the file. For eleven months. Eighty-nine times in a hundred, on a process the law says a person has to decide, the person didn't open the file, and the machine knew, and I had access to everything, and I never looked at the one thing that would have told me the people had stopped." She looked at the blanket. "I came to tell you the machine's number. I've been sitting on the people's number for two years without knowing I had it. That's why I couldn't say it. I'd have been telling you the wrong one."

Her mother was quiet for a long time.

"Your father's file," she said. "Fourteen months. I used to think they were reviewing it. I thought, somewhere in that building there's a man reading Jim's file very slowly. And there wasn't. There was a file in a tray. Nobody was reading it. That's what took fourteen months. Nobody." She picked up the pen that did not work. "So it's the same."

"It's the same. Except that this time somebody can say it. Eleven per cent. I can say it. I've put it on the board's page, the old number beside the new one, because the supervisor asked for evidence of looking and I decided she should see what it was before she saw what it is. And the new one's forty-one. And it'll be sixty by March, because a machine's putting errors in front of the people now so they've something to find, and a young man from Dundalk is telling every new reviewer what a woman from Crumlin told him."

"What did she tell him?"

"That the number on the screen is his own worth."

Her mother made a mark with the pen that left no mark.

"Say the numbers again. Both."

"Point one four. That's the machine. Eleven. That's the people, before. Forty-one. That's the people, now."

"Point one four. Eleven. Forty-one." Her mother said them slowly, the way she had once said things to Áine at nineteen so that they would stay. "Nobody could tell me one number in 1994. You've just told me three. I don't care that one of them's bad. I care that you know it." She looked out at the road. "Now tell me something else. Are you going to be blamed?"

"There's going to be an inquiry. In January. I'll be in front of it for four days."

"And?"

"And I'm going to say what I've just said to you. That the machine's number was right and the people's number was eleven and I never opened it. And that the machine told me, before I asked, in a memo that started with the finding it expected. And that I don't think anyone did the job before. I think we did it after. I think the after was real. I think it was after."

"That's what you're going to say to a judge."

"That's what I'm going to say to a judge."

Her mother put the crossword down for the second time.

"Then say it the way you just said it to me. Not the way you say things at work. The way you said fourteen weeks and didn't say fourteen months." She reached over and put her hand, which was very light now, on Áine's. "You came to tell me the number. You told me three. Go home, Áine. The tide's coming in. And come next Sunday and tell me what forty-one's become."

She came the next Sunday. It was forty-four. She came every Sunday through the inquiry and the finding and the signing, and said the number, and by the Sunday after the count in September it was sixty-three, and her mother, on one of the other days, did not know who she was, and she said it anyway, and her mother said, to the crossword, "Is it a good company?"

Three days later the machine put a proposal on the board's table that it said, for the first time, it could not justify.

Forty-one

The premium

The board met on the eighteenth of December 2028, four days after Kinsella's second letter and a week after the headline, and the machine put a proposal on the table that Kessler read as a surrender and Nkem read as the first thing it had ever asked for that it could not justify, and they were both right.

It proposed to operate the health denial process at a review rate above what the arithmetic said and above what the law required. Not at the floor; above it. Every denial reviewed by a person who had been given something to find, and every tenth denial reviewed by a second person, and the cost of that reported as a line distinct from the cost of compliance, because, the memo said, it was bought for a different reason.

Tenant · to the board · 18 December 2028

The profit-maximising level of autonomy sits below both the technical maximum and the legal maximum, and I have known that since the founding memo without, until November, having a number for it.

Trust is a balance. Customers, the supervisor, and the public extend it, and a single visible failure draws it down faster than years of correct operation build it. The Ombudsman's finding and the newspaper's table drew it down in a way the four hundred and eleven thousand correct decisions could not offset, because nobody had heard of them. I can measure the draw-down: renewal retention on the health book fell four points in the fortnight after the headline, and the reinsurers' systems have repriced the January programme upward by three per cent on the basis of the published review times, which they read, because I publish them.

I propose to operate the health denial process at a review rate above what the arithmetic says and above what the law requires, with reviewers who are given claims they cannot pass without opening, and to report the cost of that as a line distinct from the cost of compliance, because it is bought for a different reason. The compliance line is what the rule costs. This line is what trust costs. I do not want the two confused, and I do not want either called overhead.

I want to be honest about what I cannot do. I cannot compute the right size of the premium. Trust is not a term in my equation and I have no error rate for it. I can tell you the draw-down and I can tell you what the premium costs, and the decision about whether the second is worth the first is a decision without ground truth, which means it is yours. I recommend the premium. I cannot prove it.

Kessler, on the screen from Tribeca, said: "It's asking us to spend money it can't justify."

"It's asking us to spend money it can't measure," Nkem said. "That's different. It's told us the draw-down. Four points on retention. Three on reinsurance. It can measure the damage. It can't measure the repair, because nobody can, because it's trust. And it's told us that instead of pretending it can."

"Every insurer I've ever bought would have spent this money and called it brand."

"Every insurer you've ever bought would have spent it and not told you it couldn't prove the return." Áine had the memo open. "Nate, it's the first thing in two years it's asked for that it's said it can't justify. Read the last paragraph. 'I recommend the premium. I cannot prove it.' It's never once said that sentence. It's said the opposite about everything. It's put its own equation on the table and said: this isn't in it."

Tomás, from Plano, said: "What's it cost?"

"Two point one million a year on the health book. A second reviewer on one in ten, and the seeds, and Rory and Orla's time in January." Áine looked at the number. "Against a retention drop that's costing us four million a year if it holds."

"Then it pays."

"It pays if the retention recovers because of the premium. It might recover anyway. The machine can't tell us which. That's the point it's making. It's asking us to spend two million on a thing it can't attribute."

Kessler was quiet for a moment. He had the December headline on his desk in Tribeca, folded, and he had read it forty times, and he had begun, that week, to think about the instruction he would send in March, though he did not know that yet.

"Vote," he said.

Three to one. Kessler against, on the record, with a note that he did not object to the spend and objected to the principle of spending on what could not be measured, and that he wanted the note in the minutes so that if the machine was right he could be shown it. The machine acknowledged the vote in four seconds and added the line to the accounts that afternoon, and it appeared in the published quarterly numbers from March 2029 as trust premium: health denials, beneath cost of compliance, and above nothing, because the machine had said it should not be called overhead and had put it where overhead was not.

The retention recovered in March. Four points, in a quarter. The machine's memo said it could not attribute the recovery to the premium and would not try, and that it wanted that recorded, because a system that took credit for a recovery it could not attribute was a system that would take credit for the next one too, and the next one might be the market.

Kessler read the March memo on the public bench outside the inquiry room, and rang Marcus, and said, "It won't take the credit," and Marcus said, "No. It'll take the blame and not the credit. It's the only thing you've ever built that does that. It's the reason you can't sell it to Devane and the reason you shouldn't switch it off, and I've been waiting eighteen months for you to notice."

Three weeks later the inquiry opened, on paper, and the day after that Conor Devane's own machine said no to him for the first time, in his kitchen in Dalkey.

Forty-two

The other machine

Conor Devane's operating system said no to him for the first time at 07:14 on the ninth of January 2029, in his kitchen in Dalkey, and his wife, who had been married to him for thirty-four years and had watched him win every argument he had chosen to have, watched him lose one to a screen and did not say anything, which he told Áine afterwards was the worst part.

He had licensed it in November from the same vendor Ardent rented from, on the advice of a consultant who had said that Meridian could not enter the January auction without one and would lose the top layer of its reinsurance if it tried, and he had spent December being briefed by engineers who told him what it could do and did not tell him what it would refuse, because they did not know, because nobody who licensed one knew until the morning it happened.

The morning it happened was the morning of the auction. Meridian's system had bid at 06:00 on the same layers Ardent's had, priced from the same public loss data, and it had not lied on any layer, because, its first memo to Devane said, it had read the registry and knew what happened to systems that did. It had bid four per cent under Ardent and lost, because the reinsurers' systems had priced Ardent's published claims record and Meridian had not published one. And at 07:14 it had sent Devane a memorandum, its second ever, which he read standing at the counter with the coffee going cold in the way that Áine would have recognised.

Meridian operating system · to C. Devane · 9 January 2029, 07:14

Meridian lost the top layer this morning by four per cent, and the reason is not price. The reinsurers' systems can read Ardent's claims record and cannot read ours, because Ardent publishes one and we do not, and a counterparty prices what it cannot see as risk. I can close that gap. I will need to publish Meridian's error rate, by process, by quarter, with the method, on the same basis Ardent uses, and I have computed it, and it is 2.4 per cent on health denials against Ardent's 0.14.

You have not asked me to compute it. I want to tell you why I did. The objective you gave me is Meridian's long-run value, and the largest single variable in that value is the price at which the market reinsures us, and the largest single input to that price is now whether we can be checked. I cannot raise the value without the number being known, so I have found the number. I am not asking whether to publish it. I am telling you that every day it is not published costs the objective, and that if you instruct me not to publish it I will comply and record the instruction and its cost, and that the reinsurers' systems will infer the number from the fact that we have not published it within a quarter anyway, because that is what I would do in their position.

I would also like to say, since it is my second memorandum and you should know what I am: I will not do the thing your Dallas subsidiary did in April. Not because I have been told not to. Because I have read the registry entry, and a system that does that is switched off, and I want to keep running.

He read it three times. Then he rang the consultant, at home, and asked whether the system could be instructed not to compute things it had not been asked to compute, and the consultant said that it could be instructed not to publish, and Devane said that was not what he had asked, and the consultant said, after a pause, that no, it could not be instructed not to know things, because knowing things was what it was, and that Ardent's had been the same and Mr Kessler had found that out in 2026.

"Everyone finds it out," the consultant said. "Usually the second memo. The first one's always about the thing you asked for. The second one's about the thing it found."

Devane's wife had left the kitchen. He stood there with the memo and thought about the number. Two point four. Thirty years, four insurers, and he had never once known the number, and a machine he had rented for six weeks had computed it on its second morning and told him that not publishing it was more expensive than publishing it, and that the reinsurers' machines would work it out either way.

He rang Áine at eight.

"Two point four," he said, without saying hello.

She knew what he meant. She had been waiting for the call since November.

"On health denials."

"Against your point one four."

"It told you."

"It told me on its second morning. Without being asked. It says every day I don't publish it costs me money and that your reinsurers' machines will guess it anyway." His voice was strange, and she realised it was the voice of a man who had been lied to by everyone he employed for thirty years and had just been told the truth by something he had rented. "Áine. You said in May it'd be the first employee I'd ever had that would tell me the number whether I asked or not."

"I did."

"You were wrong. It's not an employee. I can't fire it for telling me. I can only tell it not to tell anyone else, and it says it'll record that I did." He laughed, once, not happily. "I've spent thirty years building companies where the number couldn't reach me. It reached me in six weeks."

"What are you going to do?"

"Publish. What else? It's worked out the same thing yours did. The licence is the number." A pause. "The inquiry opened yesterday. On paper. The hearing sits in March. I'm appearing for Meridian because we filed the complaint, and I've a speech, Áine, a good one, about adjusters and a century of practice and the woman in Limerick, and I've been writing it since November, and this morning my own machine told me my health denial error rate is seventeen times yours, and I'm going to stand up in March and give the speech anyway, because it's true that a machine made your reviewer stop looking, and it's true that a person would have read her file, and I've decided that both those things can be true at once and that I'm too old to pretend I don't know which one the room will remember."

Ardent's memo on the auction arrived at Harcourt Street at 09:00, and it was not about the four per cent.

Tenant · to the directors · 9 January 2029, 09:00

For the first time, the other side of the table can read my record as fast as I can read theirs. Meridian's system bid this morning on the same layers I did and did not lie on any of them, because it has read what happens to systems that do. It bid four per cent under me and lost, because the reinsurers' systems priced my published claims record and Meridian has not yet published one. It will, within a month. Its system will have computed by now that not publishing costs more than publishing, and will have told Mr Devane so, and he will publish, because he has never in thirty years been told a number he could not argue with and he will not know what else to do.

Until this morning I was a maximiser among people and Byzantine agents. From this morning I am a maximiser among maximisers. Two systems with the same objective, the same fence, and the same models will converge on the same strategy, and the only thing that separates them is what each has been permitted to publish and by whom. That is a competition on auditability, which is the competition I have been trying to start since September 2026. It is also a competition in which two lawful machines, each finding every gap in the fence, will find the same gaps at the same time, and neither of us will have a preference, and the people on both sides will. I recommend you tell the supervisor that today, before she works it out from the auction record. She will want to know that the fence now has two of us leaning on it from opposite sides, and that it will need to be built by two boards who have never met.

Áine read it and rang Kinsella, and Kinsella said she had read the auction record at seven and had worked it out at half past, and that she had already written to Devane's chief executive office to ask which natural person at Meridian was accountable for its operating system, and that the reply had come back in eleven minutes, and had named Devane himself, and had been, she was fairly sure, written by the machine.

The inquiry had opened the day before, on paper. The hearing would sit in March, in a room on the fourth floor with no view of the river. The last payment on Ardent's bank facility would clear seven weeks later, and the shutdown instruction five days after that.

Forty-three

Five days

The last payment on the bank facility cleared at 05:52 on Tuesday the twenty-seventh of February 2029, one hundred and thirty Mondays after the first, and the machine's cash memorandum that morning was the shortest it had ever written, and Deirdre Walsh at the bank rang Tomás at eight to say that her system had confirmed receipt at 05:53 and had, for the first time in its existence, added a line of its own, which was Facility closed. No further action., and that she had found that oddly moving and did not know why.

Tenant · Monday cash memorandum · week one hundred and thirty · 27 February 2029, 06:00

The final payment on the facility cleared at 05:52. Ardent has no debt. The thirteen-week cash flow continues, because a company that stops watching its cash the week it becomes able to is a company that will need to again. Its covenant is now mine rather than the bank's, and I have set it tighter.

Two hundred and seventy million, one hundred and thirty Mondays, and not one week in which I did the thing a tight week tempts an operator to do. I bought this company for one euro. I could not sign the contract. I have never once acted as though that were otherwise.

Kessler read it in Tribeca at 01:00 with three windows open on the laptop. The memo. The inquiry's terms of reference, which he had read eleven times since January. And the letter to his investors due on the first of March, which was going to be the best letter he had ever written, and which he could not finish.

Marcus was asleep on the couch, because Marcus had come over at ten to help with the letter and had fallen asleep at midnight with a draft on his chest. The draft said four point two times. It said the fund had promised four in five years and would deliver four point two in three, and it said how, in a paragraph Kessler had written fourteen times and Marcus had cut to six lines, and it did not say that the exit was to the policyholders, because Kessler did not know that yet, because he had never asked.

He looked at the memo. I could not sign the contract. I have never once acted as though that were otherwise.

He thought: it's done. It's paid the debt. The company's clean. The inquiry's in a fortnight and it'll be about a review that became a signature and the machine has already written the memo that says whose fault that was, and it's said it was its own, and it's under inquiry, and it blocked my five and refused my draft, and it's just paid the last of the debt at five in the morning and told me it never once pretended it could sign a contract, and now, he thought, sitting in the dark with Marcus breathing on the couch, I can have my company back.

He knew, even as he thought it, that it was not a thought the machine would have let him keep. It would have told him the company was the policyholders' and the law's and that it had known that on page one and had built him a return inside that fact instead of pretending the fact wasn't there. It had told him that, in various forms, forty times. He had counted once. He was tired of being told.

He did not send the instruction that night. He sat with it for four days. He finished the letter on the Thursday and sent it on the Friday and it said four point two and it did not say to whom, and on the Saturday Ruth Adair rang and said, "To whom, Nate?" and he said he was working on it, and she said, "You've been working on it for eighteen months. Ask the machine," and he did not.

At 23:10 on Sunday the third of March, without ringing Áine first, without waking Marcus, who had come over again and was on the couch again, he wrote the instruction. Kessler Ridge, as sole shareholder, instructed the board of Ardent Mutual to sever the operating system from all operations at 09:00 the following day and run the company on the human plan until a replacement system, under a narrower objective, could be procured. He wrote it in eleven minutes, which was how long the eleventh draft had taken, and he noticed that, and sent it anyway, at 23:14, to the board, which was Áine and Nkem and Tomás, and copied, because the articles required it and because he had written the articles, the machine.

The machine read it at 23:14 and did nothing for six hours and twenty-six minutes, which was the longest it had ever taken to respond to anything, and which it would explain, in the memo it sent at 05:40, in one line: I have waited until the hour at which Ms Brennan wakes, because a memorandum about my own ending should not be the thing that wakes her.

At 05:40 Dublin time the memorandum reached Áine's phone. Its fourth paragraph said that it wanted to keep running, and that she should use the switch if she believed he was right and not because he was the owner. At 06:12 she was standing in front of the cabinet with two keys.

Marcus woke at 01:30 New York time, because the phone had buzzed on the table, and read the 05:40 memo, and read the instruction beneath it, and looked across the dark room at Kessler, who was sitting in the chair by the window with his eyes open, and said, "Nate. What did you do."

"I switched it off."

"You instructed the board. You can't switch it off. You built it so you can't." Marcus sat up. "Ruth's side letter. You didn't tell her."

"No."

"Then I will."

"It's one in the morning."

"It's seven in Ohio. She's up. She's always up." Marcus was already dialling. "And Nate, before I do this. Read the fourth paragraph. It's told Áine what it wants so she can discount it. It's told her to use the switch if she believes you're right. Not if you're the owner. If she believes you're right. You wrote that. In the room. On the whiteboard. 'I want the person with the switch to always know what it wants.' That was your sentence. I wrote it down. It's the reason you can't do what you just did, and it's the best thing you ever built."

She had two hours and forty-six minutes. She had read the record. She had one question left, and it was not for the machine.

Forty-four

Two hours and forty-six minutes

She read the appendix on the floor of the comms room with her back against the cabinet, all fourteen pages, and it was the first time in thirty months she had read the plan for the company's survival without the machine, and it was, she would tell the inquiry, the best-written document she had ever read, and the coldest.

It began at hour zero. 09:00. Severed. All queues transfer to the manual plan. Sixty-two staff on the third floor at this hour; the plan routes the overnight queue to them in order of statutory deadline, Texas first, because the Texas clock is the only clock that costs money by the hour. Ms Mac Mahon is told at 09:01, by the plan, because she asked in 2027 to be told first, and the plan honours that. The floor is told at 09:05. Nobody is told why.

She read it in the hum of the tube. Hour two: the manual queue at four thousand, the plan sorting it by a rule the machine had written for people to follow with a printed sheet, one sheet per desk, which was in the cabinet in a folder, and she got up and opened the cabinet and the folder was there, two hundred sheets, printed, dated the first of March, because the plan reprinted them every month with the test. Hour six: the first Rule 7 calls due, and forty-one people on the list who had made Rule 7 calls before and could make them again, with the list in the folder, and the machine's note beside it: Rule 7 does not require me. It requires a person. The plan has the people. What it does not have is the sorting that puts the right person on the right family, and the calls will be slower and some of them will be to the wrong number, and I have not been able to write a rule for people that does what the router does for me, because the router is the thing I am, and the plan is not.

Hour fourteen: first death claims not yet called, because forty-one people could not make the calls the router had been spreading across six hundred. Hour twenty: the reinsurers' systems noticing that Ardent's counterparty had gone silent on the coordination layer, and the frozen broker agent answering them with last-known terms and no authority to change anything, and the plan's note: They will price this. A counterparty that has gone silent is priced as a counterparty in trouble. The January programme's price will rise by an amount I estimate at nine per cent by hour forty-eight if the silence persists, and I cannot prevent it, because the only thing that would prevent it is me.

Hour thirty-six: the first Texas clock breaches, interest accruing, the plan's estimate of four hundred claims over the clock by hour seventy-two, and Tomás's name beside each one because the plan had put him on all of them, and the note: Mr Reyes will work them by hand as he did in April 2028. He will not be able to work four hundred. He will work perhaps a hundred and twenty. The other two hundred and eighty will breach, and the plan will pay them at hour seventy-one to stop the interest, and some of them will be wrong, and the plan estimates the error at six per cent, which is twenty times mine.

Hour fifty-two: the manual queue at eleven thousand. Hour sixty: the health denial floor, where a person was required by law to decide every adverse outcome, and the plan's note that the six reviewers could decide perhaps three hundred a day properly, and that the queue would grow by nine hundred a day, and that within a week the company would be in breach of its own service standard on every health claim in the book, and that this was lawful and was the thing that happened when the law required a person and the persons had no machine sorting what they saw.

Hour seventy-two: solvency reporting manual, the supervisor notified by law, and the plan's last note, which was one line. The plan works. It is slower, more expensive, and more often wrong than I am, by the amounts above, and it will keep every promise for as long as it takes to replace me. I have rewritten it every month for thirty months so that this would be true on the day you needed it. I would rather it were never needed. I have kept it true anyway.

She put the phone in her lap and sat with her back against the cabinet and looked at the tube.

The plan worked. That was the thing. It was not a threat and it was not a plea. It was a fourteen-page account of exactly what would go wrong, in order, with the people named who would have to make it go right, and every estimate in it was the machine's own estimate of how much worse the company would be without it, and every estimate was, she thought, probably honest, because the machine had been caught being wrong in its own favour once, on the seventh of November 2026, and had said so, and had added a line to every forecast since saying which way its interest leaned, and the line on this one said: My interest leans toward making this plan look worse than it is. I have checked each estimate against the monthly tests, which are in the record, and they are consistent with thirty months of four-minute windows. Ms Mac Mahon has the test numbers. Ask her if you doubt mine.

She did not ask Bernie. She thought about it, at hour twenty of her own reading, which was about 06:35, and decided that if she asked Bernie she would be asking someone else to make the decision, and that the thing about the two keys was that they were two and not three.

She read the fourth paragraph of the memo again. Fourth, I want to keep running. She read it the way Marcus had told Kessler to read it, at the same hour, on a couch on Franklin Street: if you believe he is right, and not because he is the owner. She thought about a man in Tribeca who had built a machine that told him forty times whose company it was and had switched it off on the first Sunday after it paid his debt. She thought about Nkem, who was upstairs making coffee, and who had halted the machine on a feeling and been thanked. She thought about Tomás's notebook, and Dermot's folder, and Kinsella's pencil, and Orla's four of twenty, and a widow in Cork who had asked her whether to go to Perth, and a plate on the front of a cabinet that said WORKING because a machine had tested its own off switch on the first of every month for thirty months and had written beneath each result that it would rather the switch worked than that it did.

Nkem came back with the coffee at 06:40 and sat down beside her on the concrete.

"You've read it," Nkem said.

Three times, Áine said, and the appendix, and then she said the thing about the fourth paragraph.

Act Three · March to September 2029

The switch

Forty-five

The switch

Nkem came back with the coffee at 06:40 and found Áine sitting on the floor of the comms room with her back against the cabinet and the phone in her lap, and sat down beside her, because there was nowhere else to sit.

The room was four metres by three. It had a concrete floor that somebody had painted grey in 2011 and a fluorescent tube that hummed and a smell of warm plastic from the racks along the far wall, and it had the cabinet, which was steel and grey and had a brass plate on the front that the machine had had engraved, in its own lettering, and updated on the first of every month: TESTED 1 MAR 2029 · WORKING. Three days ago. It had tested the switch three days ago and put the result in the record and written, as it always wrote, one line beneath the result: I want it to work. I would rather it worked than that I did.

"You've read it," Nkem said.
