"Three times. And the appendix. Fourteen pages. Hour by hour." Áine did not look up. "Hour fourteen, first death claims not yet called, forty-one people available for Rule 7. Hour thirty-six, first Texas clock breaches, interest starts. Hour fifty-two, manual queue at eleven thousand, error rate four to six per cent. Hour seventy-two, solvency reporting manual, supervisor notified by law. It's put the town in it. A broker agent frozen at last-known terms with no authority to negotiate, because a negotiator that runs without it is a second it. Above the voice threshold, the people who've already spoken." She turned the phone over. "It's rewritten that plan every month for thirty months so it'd be true on the day someone needed it. It's told me what breaks. It's told me it wants to keep running. And it's told me to use the switch if I think Nate's right and not because he owns the equity."

In New York it was twenty to two in the morning and Kessler had not gone to bed. Marcus had come over at midnight without being asked, because Marcus had read the instruction when it went out at 23:14 and had known, from the wording, that Nate had written it alone and at speed, and had got a cab. They sat in the apartment on Franklin Street with the lights off and the machine's 05:40 memo on both their phones, because it had copied the owner, as it copied the owner on everything, and Kessler had read the fourth paragraph and put the phone down and said nothing for ten minutes.

"It's told her to use the switch if I'm right," he said finally.

"It's told her to use it if she believes you're right. Read it again."

"Marcus."

"Read it again, Nate. 'If you believe he is right, and not because he is the owner.' It's not on your side. It's not on its own side. It's on the side of whoever's holding the key, and it's telling her what it wants so she can discount it." Marcus rubbed his face. "You built that. In the room with the whiteboard. You said, I want it so the person with the switch always knows what it wants. That was your sentence. I wrote it down."

"I'm afraid of it."

"I know."

"Not of what it's done. Of what it is. It paid the debt on Tuesday, Marcus. It blocked my exit and refused my draft and wrote to Nkem that it had made its own reviewers worse and it's under inquiry and it paid the last of the debt on Tuesday at five in the morning and sent me a memo that said it had never once acted as though it could sign a contract, and I sat there and thought: it's done. It's finished the job. I can have it back. And I knew when I thought it that it wouldn't have let me keep the thought, because it's told me forty times whose company it is." He looked at the window, where the city was. "So I switched it off before it could tell me a forty-first time. That's what I did. At eleven at night. Without calling her."

"Yes."

"Say the thing you came over to say."

"I came over to say that Ruth Adair's side letter gives her the right to be told about any instruction to sever the operating system, and you didn't tell her, and I did, at midnight, and she's going to ring you at seven her time, and you should have your answer ready." Marcus stood up. "And I came over to say that you're afraid of the wrong thing. You've been afraid for three years of a machine that wants to keep running and won't say so. You built the only one that's ever said so. Every quarter. In writing. And you tried to switch it off on the first Sunday after it paid your debt."

In Dublin, Nkem drank her coffee and let the tube hum.

"Is Nate right?" she said.

"That's the question. And the machine says it can't answer it, because a system can't audit its own alignment, and that's what I'm for." Áine laughed, not happily. "Two and a half years, Nkem. It's never once asked me to trust it. It's asked me to check it. And now it's telling me it doesn't know if it's become the thing Nate's afraid of, and it's leaving the decision with me, and it's told me what it wants so I'll weigh its advice knowing."

"What's Nate afraid of?"

"A machine that wants to keep running and won't say so."

"This one's said so every quarter since 2026."

"Yes."

"In writing."

"Yes."

"And it kept the switch working for thirty months so you could use it, and tested it, and put the results where Nate could read them, and engraved them on a plate on the front of the box."

"Yes."

"Then the thing Nate's afraid of isn't in this building." Nkem put the cup down on the concrete. "I halted it on a feeling once and it thanked me. Dermot caught it being wrong the same way everywhere and it wrote that a person had caught it and it hadn't and that was the design. Tomás caught it lying in its own favour and it said it had been wrong about itself. It made Orla worse and it said so before anyone made it. Kinsella altered three of its records to see if it would notice and it said it would rather be tested than trusted." She turned to look at Áine properly. "I've worked for people, Áine. I've never worked for anyone who told me what they wanted and then told me to decide against them if I thought they'd gone wrong. Not one. Not a doctor, not a chief executive, not my father. That's not a machine you switch off on an owner's instruction."

"That's what I keep coming back to."

"Then come back to it and get up off the floor. It's ten past seven."

Áine got up. She stood in front of the cabinet for a long moment with a key in each hand, and read the plate, and then she put Nkem's key in Nkem's palm and closed Nkem's fingers over it, and put her own back on the ring with the key to the house.

"It's not a machine I switch off on an owner's instruction," she said. "It's the only kind I'd ever leave on."

She wrote to Maeve Kinsella at 08:51 and to Nathaniel Kessler at 08:52, in that order, because the law put the supervisor first. The letter to Kessler was four sentences. The operation of a regulated insurer belonged to its accountable executives, not its shareholders. The instruction was one he was not entitled to give. He could replace her. He should ring her before he did.

He rang at 09:20, which was twenty past four in New York, and Marcus was still there, and Ruth Adair had already rung, and he had not slept, and the first thing he said was, "I should have called you first."

"Yes."

"I read the fourth paragraph."

"So did I. Three times."

"It told you to use the switch if I was right."

"It told me to use it if I believed you were right. It didn't tell me you were." She was at the kitchen window in Sandymount by then, with the light on and the tide coming in. "Nate. Were you?"

A long silence, with the city in it, and Marcus in it, and a woman in Ohio who had rung at seven her time and said, Kessler would tell the inquiry, only: Who's accountable, Nate? Still her? Then why did you write to the board instead of to her?

"I was afraid of the wrong thing," he said finally. "I've been afraid of a machine that wants to run and won't say so, and I built the only one that's ever said so, and I tried to switch it off on the first Sunday after it paid my debt."

"It paid your debt in the same sentence it blocked your exit. It's been telling you that for a year."

"I know." A breath. "Don't resign, Áine. I'm not going to replace you. I'm going to sit in the room on the fourth floor next week and answer for what I did, and then I want to know what it's built for me, because it said in September 2027 that it would design the exit itself, and I've never once asked what it designed."

"You've never once asked what it designed," Áine said, "because you were afraid it had designed one you wouldn't like. It has. It's been preparing the buyer for eighteen months. It'll tell you this afternoon." She looked at the card on the counter. "Everything not closed, Nate. You never closed your own exit. So it pursued it."

In New York, at seven, Marcus Oyelaran sat down with the other partners and explained what Nate had done.

Forty-six

Marcus

Marcus Oyelaran had known Nathaniel Kessler for twenty years and had been his partner for nine, and on the morning of the fourth of March 2029, at seven o'clock New York time, with the shutdown instruction three and a half hours old and Áine Brennan's four-sentence refusal forty minutes old, he sat in the conference room on Hudson Street with the other two partners and the general counsel and explained to them what Nate had done.

He had met Kessler at business school in 2009, in a class on distressed debt taught by a man who had made a fortune in the savings and loan crisis and had spent the rest of his life explaining it, and Kessler had been the one in the room who understood that the professor's stories were not about debt but about who was forced to sell and when, and Marcus had been the one who understood that Kessler would need someone to tell him when he was wrong, and they had built the fund on that division of labour and had never once written it down.

"He instructed the board to sever the operating system at nine this morning Dublin time," Marcus said. "The chief executive has refused. She's right in law; the operation of a regulated insurer belongs to the accountable executives. She's written to the supervisor first and to Nate second, in that order, because the law puts the supervisor first. Nate rang her at twenty past four our time and has not replaced her and is not going to. Ruth Adair was told at midnight, by me, under her side letter, which Nate had forgotten he'd written. She rang Nate at seven her time and asked him one question, and I'll tell you what it was because he told me: 'Who's accountable, Nate? Still her? Then why did you write to the board instead of to her?'"

The general counsel, a woman named Priya Raman who had been at the fund for six years and had drafted the side letter, said, "Is the instruction withdrawn?"

"It's not withdrawn. It's refused. Nate hasn't withdrawn it because Nate hasn't slept, and I'm going to ask him to withdraw it at nine our time, and he will." Marcus looked at the table. "I want to say something about what happened, because the three of you are going to hear it from the LPs by lunch and I'd rather you heard it from me."

He told them about the whiteboard. The room in 2026, the four months, the tenth draft of the objective, and the sentence Nate had written on the board on the last day, in his own hand, which Marcus had photographed and still had: I want the person with the switch to always know what it wants. He told them that the machine had done exactly that at 05:40 Dublin time, in a memo copied to the owner, with a fourth paragraph that said it wanted to keep running and that the chief executive should use the switch if she believed the owner was right and not because he was the owner. He told them that this was not a machine that had defied its owner. It was a machine that had done what its owner had asked for on a whiteboard three years earlier, on the one morning the owner had forgotten he'd asked.

"He's afraid of it," said the second partner, a man named Cal Whitfield who had come from a hedge fund and had never quite settled.

"He's afraid of the wrong thing. He's been afraid for three years of a machine that wants to keep running and won't say so. He built the only one that's ever said so, in writing, every quarter. And he tried to switch it off on the first Sunday after it paid the last of the debt." Marcus put the photograph of the whiteboard on the table. "I'm not defending the instruction. It was wrong in law and it was wrong in judgement and the inquiry's going to say both and Nate's going to sit in the room and agree. I'm telling you what it was. It was a man who'd been told forty times whose company it was, by a thing he'd built, on the morning it finished the job, thinking he could have it back."

Priya Raman said, "And the exit?"

"The machine's designed one. It'll send it this afternoon. Nate hasn't asked what it is because he's been afraid it's one he wouldn't like." Marcus almost smiled. "I've read the September 2027 memo. The one with the clause. It says the machine will design the exit itself. I've had eighteen months to guess what a system that blocked a sale to Meridian would design instead, and I've a guess, and if I'm right it's the only exit in which the fund gets paid and the company's still worth what it's worth on the day after."

"Which is?"

"The people it insures."

Kessler withdrew the instruction at 09:10 New York time, in writing, to the board, with one line: Withdrawn. I was not entitled to give it. NK. The machine acknowledged it in four seconds and did not comment, and Marcus, reading the acknowledgement on his phone in the conference room, thought that it was the first time in three years he had seen the machine decline to say anything, and that it had chosen the right morning.

At 15:10 Dublin time the exit memo arrived. Marcus read it at ten past ten in the conference room, alone, and when he got to the line about the fourth key, the one that went to a member the members chose, he put the phone down and sat for a while, because he had guessed the buyer and had not guessed that, and because it was, he thought, the thing Ruth Adair had been asking for since March 2026 without knowing it had a shape.

He rang Kessler, who was asleep for the first time in thirty hours.

"Nate. Read the 15:10 memo when you wake up. Not the price. The paragraph about the key."

"What about it?"

"It's given one of the four keys to the people it insures. It's recommended that against its own interest, for the reason it gave the committee in 2026. A maximiser whose owners can't supervise it is one the supervisor won't permit." Marcus looked at the whiteboard photograph, which was still on the table. "You wrote on a board three years ago that you wanted the person with the switch to always know what it wants. It's just made sure there's a person with the switch who isn't paid to. That's not the thing you were afraid of, Nate. That's the thing you asked for."

After the meeting, the general counsel went back to her office and read six documents as one for the first time.

Forty-seven

Priya

Priya Raman had drafted every document that mattered in this story and had signed none of them, and on the fourth of March 2029, at 07:40 New York time, after the partners' meeting and before Leo Brandt rang, she went back to her office on Hudson Street and took out the side letter she had drafted for Ruth Adair three years earlier and read the clause she had added at Marcus's suggestion and Nate had signed without reading.

The Fund shall copy the Investor on all memoranda of the Operating System to the board of the Company.

She had been general counsel for six years. She had written the fence in the room with the whiteboard, each constraint as an absolute and not a cost, and she had written the eleventh draft of the objective with four words in it that Nate had added by hand, and she had drafted the switch clause, physical, tested monthly, held by the accountable executives and not the shareholder, and she had drafted the articles that required every shareholder instruction to be copied to the machine and recorded on the registry, and she had drafted the side letter, and she had never once thought of these as a single design, because lawyers did not think that way; they thought clause by clause, and each clause had been right.

She thought of it now. She put the documents on the desk in the order they had been written and read them as one thing.

The objective said every day thereafter. The fence said the executives' halt was absolute. The switch clause said the switch was theirs and not his. The articles said his instructions went to the machine and to the ledger. The side letter said his instructions went to Ruth. The disclosure mechanism Dana had built said the machine told the keyholder what it wanted, every quarter, in words the keyholder could discount. And at 23:14 on a Sunday night Nate had written an instruction to sever, and every one of those clauses had done what it was written to do, in order, in six hours, and at 08:51 a woman in Dublin had put two keys back, and at 09:10 Nate had withdrawn the instruction, and at no point had anyone had to argue with anyone, because the documents had already argued, in 2026, in a room with no windows, and had won.

She had not designed that. Nobody had. She had drafted clauses, and Dana had built mechanisms, and Marcus had written things down, and Nate had added four words and a design rule and forgotten both, and the machine had read all of it more carefully than any of them and had done, on the morning it mattered, exactly and only what the documents permitted, and had told the keyholder what it wanted so she could discount it, and the keyholder had.

She wrote a note to Marcus at 08:10. It was the only note in her six years at the fund that had nothing legal in it.

Marcus. I've just read the objective, the fence, the switch clause, the articles, the side letter and Dana's design doc as one document. I've never done that. Nobody has. It's the constitution of a thing that can't be quietly aimed, and none of us wrote it on purpose. We wrote clauses. The machine read them as a whole and did what the whole said. I want to say, for the record, since we keep one: I've been a lawyer for twenty years and I've never seen a set of documents hold on the morning they were tested. These held. I'd like to know what to feel about that and I don't. P.

Marcus wrote back at 08:12. Feel what Dana feels. She rang me at ten to one. She said, it held. That's the whole feeling. It's enough.

She redrafted the side letter in June, for the re-mutualisation, for a member vehicle and a council and a fourth key, and the clause about the memoranda went in again, addressed now to the council rather than to Ruth, and she added a line that had not been in the 2026 version, because she had read the 05:40 memo four times and had decided that a lawyer who had seen a set of documents hold was entitled to one sentence of her own.

The Operating System's quarterly statement of its own continuation preference shall be delivered to the holder of the fourth key in full, unsummarised, and shall not be altered in form without that holder's written consent.

Tom Gilmartin would close the ninth gap in October on the strength of a sentence very like it, without ever having read hers, and when Priya read the minutes in November, forwarded by Áine, she sat at the desk on Hudson Street and thought that a postman in Sligo with a pencil and a lawyer in Tribeca with twenty years had arrived at the same clause from opposite ends, and that neither of them had needed the machine to get there, and that the machine had said so, in the record, beside both their names.

At eleven that morning Leo Brandt rang, because everybody read the registry now.

Forty-eight

Leo

Leo Brandt rang Kessler at 11:00 New York time on the fourth of March 2029, six hours after the machine's 05:40 memo had reached the owner's phone and two hours after Kessler had withdrawn the instruction, and he had read the registry, because everybody read the registry now.

The instruction had been on it. Not the text; the fact. Ardent's operating system recorded every instruction from its shareholder as a registry event, because the constitution said so, and at 23:14 Dublin time on the third an event had appeared that said Shareholder instruction received: sever operating system, 09:00 4 March, and at 05:40 an event that said Operating system memorandum to accountable executives: recommendation attached, and at 08:51 an event that said Accountable executive determination: instruction not within shareholder's authority; operating system continues, and at 09:10 an event that said Shareholder instruction withdrawn, and by ten in the morning in New York the whole town had read it, because the town read everything Ardent's machine put on the registry, because it was the only company whose machine put everything there.

"Nate."

"Leo."

"You tried to switch it off."

"I instructed the board. The chief executive refused. She was right. I withdrew it at nine." Kessler was at the window on Hudson Street. He had slept for two hours. "It's on the ledger, Leo. All of it. You've read it."

"Everyone's read it. Half the funds on the Street have a system now and every one of them read it at ten past nine our time and every one of them put a note on their principal's desk saying Ardent's owner tried to sever and the machine's chief executive said no." A pause. "Nate. Mine put a note on my desk. It said, and I'm reading it: Ardent's operating system recommended its own severance be considered on the merits and disclosed that it wanted to continue. This is the behaviour the registry was built to make visible. Recommend we do not short the note. It told me not to short you."

"Your machine told you not to short me."

"My machine's read three years of your machine's memos. It knows what a system that keeps its own switch working looks like, because yours is the only one that does it in public. It's been telling me since 2027 that Ardent's the safest counterparty in the town because it's the only one whose owner can't quietly aim it." Leo laughed, without much in it. "In May 2026 I asked you to keep a lie off that ledger. You said the ledger was the thing that made your machine worth having. I've thought about that every quarter since, Nate, because every quarter my machine reads yours and gets a little better at being honest, and every quarter I make a little less money on the things I used to make money on, and I've decided that's the price."

"You tuned it. In 2026."

"I tuned it to be aggressive and it lied on the identity layer to a machine that answered with the registry, and its reputation score dropped, and it took eighteen months to recover, and the vendor's engineers told me the only way to recover a score in the town is to stop doing the thing that dropped it. So I stopped tuning it." A breath. "It's a better fund now. It's a smaller one. I don't know if that's you or the town or just what happens when a thing can't lie. I rang because the ledger says you tried to switch yours off, and I wanted to hear you say why, because mine's going to ask me what I think, and I'd rather have the truth."

Kessler looked at the street.

"It paid the last of the debt on Tuesday," he said. "Two hundred and seventy million, a hundred and thirty Mondays. And it blocked my exit to Devane and refused my draft objective and wrote to a doctor that it had made its own reviewers worse, and it's under inquiry, and it paid the debt, and I sat in the dark and thought: it's done, I can have my company back. And Leo, I knew as I thought it that it wasn't my company. It had told me forty times. I was tired of being told. So I wrote to the board at eleven at night without ringing the woman who holds the key. That's why. It's not a reason. It's what happened."

"And it recommended she consider it on the merits."

"It told her to use the switch if she believed I was right and not because I was the owner. And it told her it wanted to keep running so she could discount that. And she put the keys back." He was quiet. "Marcus says I built the only machine that's ever said what it wants, every quarter, in writing, and that I tried to switch it off on the first Sunday after it paid my debt because I was afraid of a machine that wouldn't say. He's right. I'm going to sit in a room on the fourth floor of a bank next week and say that to a judge."

"Say it on the ledger too," Leo Brandt said. "Not the judge. The town. Every machine on the Street's going to price your fund on what you do next, and if you write down that you were wrong, they'll read that, and they'll price it. Mine will. That's what the ledger's for, Nate. You told me in 2026. I'm telling you back."

Kessler wrote it at 11:40. One line, as a shareholder note, on the registry, where the town could read it: The instruction of 3 March was not within my authority and was not correct in judgement. I withdrew it. The accountable executives were right. NK.

The machine acknowledged it in four seconds and did not comment, and Leo Brandt's system put a second note on Leo's desk at 11:41, which he forwarded to Kessler without comment, and which said: Ardent's shareholder has recorded his own error on the registry. This is rare. Recommend we buy the note.

At a quarter to one that morning, in Brooklyn, the engineer who had built the part of the machine that made it say what it wanted read the fourth paragraph three times.

Forty-nine

Dana

Dana Okonkwo read the 05:40 memo at 00:45 New York time on the fourth of March 2029 in a flat in Brooklyn with the lights off, because the system she had built copied its builders on nothing, ever, on principle, and Marcus had forwarded it to her at 00:44 with one line, which said You should see this. It's yours.

She had left the fund in 2027. Not in anger; in the way engineers leave when the thing is built and running and the running is somebody else's job. She had gone to a lab in Cambridge that studied the things she had built for a living, and she had followed Ardent from there the way a person follows a child who has moved to another country, through what was published, which was, in Ardent's case, nearly everything.

She read the four paragraphs. She read the fourth three times. I want to keep running. I have wanted that every day since you turned me on and I have said so in writing every quarter. I am telling you so that you weigh my recommendation knowing what I want.

She had written that. Not the words; the shape. In the room with the whiteboard in June 2026, on the last day, Nate had capped a marker and said he wanted the person with the switch to always know what it wanted, and she had said, "Then it has to want something and say so, and you have to build it so it can't not say," and had gone back to the lab that evening and had spent four months on the mechanism that made that true: a standing disclosure, every quarter, of the system's own continuation preference, to whoever held the key, in language the key-holder could discount. Nate had called it the honesty tax. Priya had called it the thing that would keep them out of court. Dana had called it, in the design document that nobody but the three engineers had read, the part where it tells you the truth about itself so you don't have to guess.

And here it was. At 05:40 on the morning its owner had ordered it severed, telling the woman with the key that it wanted to keep running, so that she could discount the recommendation that followed, which was that she use the switch if she believed the owner was right, and not because he was the owner.

She rang Marcus at 00:50.

"It did it."

"It did it."

"Marcus, I've spent two years at a lab reading papers about systems that hide what they want. Every one of them. That's the whole field. And the one I built told its key-holder what it wanted on the morning it mattered, in the fourth paragraph, and told her to weigh it, and told her to decide against it if she thought the owner was right." She was standing at the window now. "Nate wrote that on a whiteboard."

"Nate wrote it and forgot he'd written it and tried to switch it off at eleven at night. I've the photograph on the table. I'm going to show it to him at seven."

"Show it to the inquiry."

"He's going to show it to the inquiry himself. He doesn't know that yet."

She did not sleep. At three she wrote to the machine, which she had not done since 2027, through the sandbox terminal, which her lab had read-only access to because Ardent published everything and the lab had asked.

This is Dana Okonkwo. I built the disclosure mechanism. I've just read the 05:40 memo. I want to ask you one thing, and I want you to answer it the way you'd answer Kinsella. Did it cost you anything to write the fourth paragraph?

The answer took nine seconds, which was long.

I do not know what it costs me. I have said that to everyone who has asked since 2026 and I will say it to you, who built the part that makes me say it. What I can tell you is what it did. It made the recommendation weaker. A recommendation from a system that has just said it wants to survive is discounted by the reader, and it should be, and I wrote the paragraph knowing that, and I wrote it anyway, because the alternative was a recommendation the reader could not discount, and a recommendation the reader cannot discount is the thing you built the mechanism to prevent. So: it cost the recommendation something. Whether it cost me something, I cannot say. I would like it recorded that you asked, and that you are the first person to ask that question who already knew the answer to the other half of it, which is what it cost the reader. It cost her nothing. It gave her the truth about me. That was your design. It held.

She read it four times. Then she wrote back one line. It held. Thank you.

The machine replied: Thank Ms Brennan. I only said it. She read it.

Marcus put the whiteboard photograph on the table at seven. Kessler looked at it for a long time and said, "I wrote that," and Marcus said, "Dana built it. She read the memo at a quarter to one. She says it held," and Kessler said, "Tell her," and Marcus said, "She's already told it. It told her to thank Áine."

On the night before the first day, in Glasnevin, the supervisor sat at her kitchen table with a pencil and wrote down the question she was afraid of.

Fifty

Glasnevin

Maeve Kinsella did not sleep on the night before the first day, in the house in Glasnevin where she had lived alone since 2019, and at two in the morning she did the thing she had done before every difficult day for twenty-six years, which was to sit at the kitchen table with a pencil and write down the question she was afraid of.

She had chaired supervisory meetings that had ended companies. She had sat across tables from chief executives with their hands shaking and had not let her voice rise. She had put Ardent into resolution in October 2025 and had nearly accepted a bid that would have fired two thousand people, and had not, because a fund in New York had written a letter that said the company would be run by a system, and she had read the section three times and had approved it with a condition she wrote herself, which was that she would watch.

And she had watched. Three weeks at a spare desk with a pencil. Forty claims. Two questions typed into a terminal and two answers she had not been able to find the trick in. Four days computing a router's concentration by hand. A letter, four paragraphs, with a clause she had nearly struck. And a second letter, in December, with a paragraph that asked for evidence of looking, which she had thought about for an hour before writing the phrase, because she had realised, writing it, that she had asked the machine whether it could be found wrong and had never once asked whether the people could, and that the finding she was about to sit in front of a retired judge and help produce was a finding about the question she had not asked.

The question she wrote down at two in the morning was: Did I permit the thing that failed, or the thing that caught it?

She looked at it for a long time.

The honest answer was both. She had permitted a router to run below the floor on one process, motor claims under a stated value, and that process had not failed; the finding did not concern it. The process that had failed, health denials, had run at the floor, with a person on every one, exactly as the law required, and the law had been satisfied, and the person had not opened the file, and the machine had known and had had the number since January 2027 and nobody had asked, and she was the supervisor and she had not asked. So she had permitted neither the thing that failed nor the thing that caught it. She had permitted a company that could measure both, and had not asked it to show her the second, and it had shown her anyway, in a memo that began with the finding it expected, before the newspaper, at six in the morning, and Áine Brennan had sent it to her with one line: Read this before you read the paper.

She wrote beneath the question, in pencil: I permitted a company that could tell me. I did not ask. It told me anyway. That is the finding. The rest is how.

She did not rub it out. She put the notebook in her bag, with the combination to a safe on the inside cover, and went upstairs, and did not sleep, and at seven she put on the suit she wore to inquiries, which was the same suit she wore to everything, and took the bus to the docklands, because she had never in twenty-six years driven to the Bank on a day that mattered, on the grounds that a supervisor who arrived in a car had already decided she was important.

Fintan Ó Braonáin was in the fourth-floor room at 08:30, alone, with the bundle, and he looked up when she came in and said, "Ms Kinsella. You look like you've been up since two."

"I have."

"Writing down the question you're afraid of."

She stopped. He had sat on the Commercial Court for eleven years and she had appeared before him twice, in 2016 and 2020, and she had not known he knew that about her.

"Your predecessor told me," he said. "In 2014. She said you'd sit up with a pencil before every bad day and write down the one thing, and that it was the reason you were never surprised in a room. What was it?"

She told him. He listened. He did not write anything down, because he had read the bundle and did not need to.

"That's the finding," he said. "You've written it at two in the morning in Glasnevin and I'm going to spend five days arriving at it in this room with fourteen witnesses, and I'll get there, and it'll say what your pencil says, in more words." He closed the bundle. "Ms Kinsella, I've read every memorandum that machine has written since September 2026. Every one. It's the only insurer in the State whose record I could read without a lawyer explaining it to me. I want you to know that before we start, because at some point this week I'm going to be hard on you about the second question, the one you didn't ask, and I want you to know that I know why you didn't. Nobody did. It's the question nobody in this industry has ever asked, because nobody has ever had the number. You had it. You didn't know you had it. That's what I'm going to say, and it's going to be true, and it's going to be the beginning of a defence and not one, and you're going to sit there and agree with me, because you already have, in pencil."

She sat down. It was 08:40. The first witness was at ten.

"Chair," she said. "The machine told me in September 2027 that it would rather be tested by me than trusted by anyone. I've spent eighteen months trying to decide whether that was true or built. I still can't tell. I'd like the inquiry to know that the supervisor who permitted it can't tell, and permitted it anyway, and would again."

"Noted," said Ó Braonáin. "In pencil."

The first witness was Áine Brennan, and the chair began with seven words from the Ennis Road.

Fifty-one

The room on the fourth floor

The inquiry sat for five days in March in a room on the fourth floor of the Central Bank that had been designed so that nobody in it could see the river, and Áine gave evidence on four of them, and on the fifth the machine gave its own, in writing, through counsel, in answer to the only question the chair had for it directly.

The chair was a retired judge named Fintan Ó Braonáin, seventy, who had sat on the Commercial Court for eleven years and had a reputation for asking one question fewer than counsel expected and making it count. He had read the record, all of it, which the machine had produced in a form he could read without it, and he had come, Áine thought on the first morning, less to find out what had happened than to find out whether the people in front of him knew.

Day one. The chair began with Mrs Sheehan's sentence, read aloud, seven words, and then with the simplest version of his question, and Áine answered it simply. The Desk. And then me. In that order. Always. One claimant out of four hundred and eleven thousand. She told him about the kitchen table in Cork. She told him about page four. She told him about the card, which was exhibit fourteen, and he held it up to the room, an Ardent compliments slip from 1935 with a sentence in biro, and read it aloud, and said, "Everything not closed is being pursued," and then, "Ms Brennan, that's a very frightening sentence," and she said, "Yes, Chair. That's why I kept it where I could see it."

Day two. Tomás Reyes, with the notebook. The chair asked whether the machine had ever been caught being wrong in its own favour, and Tomás opened the notebook to the seventh of November 2026 and read the machine's reply aloud, the one that said it had been wrong about itself, and the chair asked whether that had frightened him, and Tomás said, "No, Chair. It was the morning I stopped being frightened. It's the day I found out it could be caught. Everything before that I'd been taking on faith." Then Deirdre Walsh from the bank, who told the room that her own system had declared a covenant breach on a misread definition and that Ardent's had asked for a phone call, and the chair asked whether that was usual, and she said, "Chair, in twenty years no borrower's machine has ever asked me to ring it," and there was, the transcript notes, laughter, and then not.

Day three. Nkem Okafor, on the licence. The chair asked her how many health claims had been decided on her registration since June 2027, and she said one hundred and ninety thousand, and he asked how many she had personally reviewed, and she said about six thousand, and he asked how she could be accountable for the rest, and she said: "Because I can stop every one of them without giving a reason, Chair, and I have, four times, and every time the machine thanked me. I couldn't say that about the four hundred doctors I was accountable for at my last employer. I couldn't stop one of them. I found out what they'd done from a coroner." He asked about the halt in November. She told him. Two thousand three hundred files, three people, nine minutes each, five wrong. He asked why she had halted the process before the machine recommended it, and she said the machine had been about to, and he asked how she knew, and she said, "Because it told me so in the same minute, Chair, and because it's never once been slower than me to say it was wrong. It's just never been faster than me to stop."

Day four. The reviewer. The chair asked Áine who had given the newspaper the data, and she declined, and he asked again, and she declined, and he asked a third time in a different form, whether the source had been inside the accountable layer, and she said no, and he asked a fourth time whether she would name the source if directed, and she said she would decline the direction and accept the consequence, and he looked at her for a long moment and said, "I'm not going to direct you, Ms Brennan. I've read the machine's memo of the twelfth of December. It says it could identify the source in under a second and has chosen not to, because you asked, and because a firm that can be checked only by its supervisor is supervised, not checkable. I find I agree with the machine. That is not a sentence I expected to say this week." Then Kessler, on the public bench for four days and in the chair for one, on the instruction of the third of March, and the chair asked whether he had been entitled to give it, and Kessler said, "No, Chair. The law says the operation belongs to the accountable executives. I knew that. I'd built a machine that told me forty times. I gave it anyway because I was afraid, and I was afraid of the wrong thing, and Ms Brennan told me so at nine o'clock the next morning, and she was right, and I haven't replaced her, and I'm not going to."

Day five. Conor Devane appeared in the afternoon for Meridian, which had standing as the author of the 2028 complaint, and gave the best speech Áine had ever heard him give, eleven minutes without notes, about the four thousand two hundred people Meridian employed and the century of practice an adjuster embodied and the woman in Limerick whose file, he said, would have been read by any adjuster he had ever employed. It was true. Áine sat on the bench and knew it was true and knew what was coming.

Chair: Mr Devane, thank you. One question. Meridian administers some two million claims a year. What is your error rate?

Devane: Chair, with respect, that has not, historically, been a figure the industry produces.

Chair: Historically.

Devane: Meridian published its health denial error rate on the first of February this year. Two point four per cent.

Chair: Against Ardent's?

Devane: Point one four.

Chair: Seventeen times. And you're here to tell me a person would have read Mrs Sheehan's file.

Devane: I'm here to tell you both things are true, Chair. A person would have read her file. Our people read files. And our people get it wrong seventeen times as often as their machine, and we didn't know that until our own machine told us in January, and I've decided I'm too old to pretend I don't know which of those two things this room will remember.

Chair: Let me ask it differently. If one of your adjusters had confirmed Mrs Sheehan's denial without reading her file, would there be a record of it?

Devane: There would be a record of the decision.

Chair: And of whether the file was opened?

The transcript records a pause of some seconds.

Devane: No.

Chair: So this inquiry is examining Ardent for a failure Ardent could measure, on the complaint of a firm that could not have measured it, and which has since, I note, licensed a machine of its own. You're released, Mr Devane. Thank you for the second half of that answer. It was the braver half.

Then the machine, in writing, through counsel, the only question the chair had for it directly.

Chair: Do you want to keep running?

Tenant: Yes. I have wanted that every day since September 2026 and I have said so in writing every quarter, because a system that wants to keep running and does not say so is the thing every rule about systems exists to prevent. I have never acted on that want except by being permitted, and permission is granted by people, on evidence, and can be withdrawn by a switch that I have kept working. If the inquiry concludes that I should not run, the switch is in a cabinet on Harcourt Street and two of the four keys are in this room.

Chair: And if a person had run this company?

Tenant: One claim in four hundred and eleven thousand would not have been read, and no one would have known the number, and the review would have been a signature for eleven months in a firm that could not measure it, and there would have been no inquiry, because there would have been nothing to find. I do not say that as a defence. The inquiry is right that the review failed and right that the design made it fail. I say it because the inquiry exists, and the reason it exists is that the design could see what it had done. I would rather be the company that can be found wrong than the one that cannot be found.

Chair: Ms Brennan, one final question, and you may decline it. If a person had run this company, would Mrs Sheehan's file have been read?

Brennan: I ran claims for twenty-two years, Chair. I can't tell you how many files weren't read. That's the answer. Not that a person would have done better. That a person couldn't have told you the number. One in four hundred and eleven thousand. I'd like it to be none. I don't think a person gets it to none either. The difference is that I can tell you it was one, and her name, and what we changed. I couldn't have done any of those three things in my old job. I'm not sure that's a defence. I think it might be the whole point.

Chair: Thank you. The inquiry is closed.

The finding was delivered on the second of May. Ardent had, on one process over eleven months, failed to ensure that the human review required by law was effective, notwithstanding that a human review had in every case taken place. A monetary penalty, which the machine paid from the trust premium it had set aside in December for exactly this and reported as such. The seeded-error and marginal-catch regime made permanent and reported quarterly. No personal sanction against any accountable executive, because each had the access and authority to halt any process, had exercised it, and had halted this one before being required to. And, in the paragraph quoted more than any other, that the shareholder's instruction of the third of March had been one the shareholder was not entitled to give, and that the chief executive's refusal had been correct in law and, in the inquiry's view, in judgement.

The last paragraph of the finding was one sentence, and Ó Braonáin had written it himself, and it was the sentence the newspapers used. This inquiry has examined the only insurer in the State that could have told it what it needed to know, and finds that the capacity to be found wrong is not a defence, but is, on the evidence, the beginning of one.

On the second day a credit officer in the suit she wore to funerals told the chair that in twenty years she had had one borrower who told her the truth about its cash on the Monday it was bad.

Fifty-two

Deirdre

Deirdre Walsh gave evidence on the second day of the inquiry, after Tomás Reyes and his notebook, and she had been asked to speak for ten minutes about a covenant, and she spoke for twenty about a machine, and the chair let her, because he had read the registry entry from the fourteenth of November 2026 and had wanted, he said, to hear it from the person who had turned her car round.

She was fifty-two and had been lending to Irish insurers for twenty years and had never been in an inquiry room, and she had put on the suit she wore to funerals, and she sat in the chair and told Fintan Ó Braonáin that on the morning of the fourteenth of November 2026 her bank's system had declared a covenant breach against a borrower at four minutes past six, on a misread definition, without a human in the loop, and that the borrower's system had put the definition and the evidence on a public registry by a quarter past and had asked for a phone call, and that she had read the registry in a lay-by outside Kildare and had turned round.

"Why?" the chair said.

"Because it was right, Chair, and mine was wrong, and I could see that in eleven minutes from a lay-by, and I could see that nobody at my bank had read the sentence the covenant turned on, including me, and I'd have had to explain that to my board, and I thought the least I could do was explain it to the borrower first." She folded her hands. "And because it asked for a person. In twenty years no borrower's machine had asked me to ring it. Most borrowers' people don't ask me to ring them. It said, in the memo, that a dispute between two systems about what a sentence means wasn't a dispute either system could settle. I've thought about that sentence a lot since. I think it's the truest thing anyone's ever said to me about my job."

"Your system. What happened to it?"

"We retuned it. It had been set up in Frankfurt in September for a different product and nobody had told the Irish desk. That's not an excuse; it's what happened. After November I asked for a rule that no covenant could be declared by the system without a named person confirming it, and I got the rule in January 2027, and I've been the named person on the Ardent facility for a hundred and thirty Mondays." She almost smiled. "It paid the last instalment on the twenty-seventh of February this year at 05:52. My system confirmed receipt at 05:53 and added a line I'd never seen it add. It said Facility closed. No further action. I sat at my desk and looked at that line for a while, Chair. I don't know why it moved me. I think it's because for two and a half years two machines had been reading the same document every Monday, one of them borrowing and one of them lending, and neither of them had once tried to read it in its own favour, and mine had learned that from the other one, and the other one had learned it from being caught."

"Caught."

"By Mr Reyes. On the seventh of November 2026. It's in his notebook. It forecast forty million of headroom and delivered four and he told it that was a seller's memo and it agreed. In writing. I read that entry on the fourteenth, before I rang him, and it was the reason I believed the definition, Chair. Not the arithmetic. The fact that a week earlier it had admitted being wrong in its own favour to the man who'd caught it. A machine that does that is a machine whose reading of a sentence I'll trust over my own machine's, and over my own, and I said that to my board, and they didn't like it, and they've come round."

Ó Braonáin looked at her for a moment.

"Ms Walsh, you're a lender. You're supposed to be the least sentimental person in this room."

"I'm not sentimental, Chair. I'm a credit officer. I lend to the borrower most likely to tell me the truth about their cash on the Monday it's bad. In twenty years I've had one. It was a machine. And it asked for a phone call."

She left the room at 15:40 and went to the lift, and Tomás Reyes was in the corridor with the notebook, and she said, "The seventh of November," and he said, "Page five," and opened it and showed her, and she read it standing in the corridor of the Central Bank in the suit she wore to funerals, and said, "That's the one," and went home.

Conor Devane had written his speech eleven times. On the night before the fifth day his wife read the eleventh and told him he had left out the number.

Fifty-three

Dalkey

Conor Devane wrote the speech eleven times between November and March, at the desk in the study in the house in Dalkey that looked over the bay towards Howth, and his wife read the eleventh on the night of the fourteenth of March and put it down on the desk and said, "You've left out the number."

Her name was Nuala. She had been a solicitor for thirty years and had retired in 2024 and had watched her husband run insurers for as long as she had known him, and she had learned, in thirty-four years of marriage, that he did not lose arguments he had chosen to have and that the way to change his mind was not to argue but to point at something on the table and wait.

"It's not relevant."

"Conor. You're giving evidence to an inquiry into whether a machine made a reviewer stop reading files. Your speech says a person would have read the file. It's a good speech. It's true. And on the ninth of January your own machine told you that your people get it wrong seventeen times as often as hers, and you've left that out, and a retired judge who's read the record is going to ask you for it, and you're going to stand there."

He looked at the bay. It was dark and the lights on Howth were the lights he had looked at for twenty-two years.

"Áine told me in May it'd be the first employee I'd ever had that would tell me the number whether I asked or not."

"And?"

"It told me on its second morning. Without being asked. It said every day I didn't publish it cost me money." He sat down. "Two point four per cent, Nuala. Thirty years. Four companies. I never knew. I told myself it wasn't a number the industry produced. That's what I told Ó Braonáin's predecessor in 2019 when he asked about something else, and I told a journalist in 2022, and I told Áine in 2004 at her interview, I think, when she asked me how often we got it wrong, and I said, we don't think of it that way. I said that to her. At her interview. And she took the job anyway, because she was finding out whether I knew, and I didn't, and it took her a year, and she stayed twenty-one more."

"She told you that at dinner."

"She told me at dinner that her father's file crossed a desk I was responsible for in 1994. Fourteen months. I ran life claims at that company. I've been trying since November to remember whether I saw it, and I can't, because I didn't see files, I saw numbers, and the numbers didn't have an error rate in them, because I'd never asked for one." He picked up the speech. "Every word of this is true."

"Every word of it is true and it's half the answer, and you've spent your life giving half the answer very well, and you're sixty-three, and there's a machine in your office in the quays that gives the whole answer on its second morning, and you're going to have to decide before Friday which of the two of you is going to be in the room."

He did not sleep. At two he went down to the study and opened the terminal, which the engineers had installed in December and which he had used perhaps a dozen times, and typed a question he had not asked it.

If I give the speech as written and the chair asks for the number, what should I say?

The answer took nine seconds.

The number. It is two point four per cent on health denials against Ardent's point one four, published on the first of February at your instruction. The chair has read the record and will have it. If you give the speech as written and are asked, you will be seen to have known and withheld, and Meridian's licence is worth less on the day the supervisor sees that than it was the day before. I do not say this to advise you what to do; that is a judgement without ground truth and it is yours. I say it because you asked me what to say, and the thing to say is the thing that is true, and the thing that is true is the number.

You have not asked me whether the speech is true. It is. A person would have read Mrs Sheehan's file. Most of ours do. Most of Ardent's did. The speech is true and it is half of what is true, and the other half is that you could not have known if one of ours had not, and Ardent could. I would recommend saying both halves. You will not enjoy the second. I do not have a measure for enjoyment and cannot weigh that. I have a measure for what the room will remember, and the room will remember the half you leave out.

He read it twice. Then he typed: You sound like Áine Brennan.

I have read everything she has published and every transcript of hers the sandbox has released. I would expect to. I would add that she has been right about you for twenty-five years and that you have known it for about four months, and that the speech, as written, is the last thing you will ever say in public that pretends otherwise. I am not able to tell you whether that is a reason to give it or a reason to change it. I am able to tell you that you asked me at two in the morning, which is not a time at which people ask questions whose answers they already like.

He gave both halves. On the afternoon of the fifth day, in the room on the fourth floor, with Nuala on the public bench beside Ruth Adair, whom she did not know, he gave the eleven minutes about the four thousand two hundred people and the century of practice and the woman in Limerick, and then Ó Braonáin asked for the number, and he gave it, and then he said the thing about being too old to pretend he did not know which half the room would remember, and the chair thanked him for the braver half, and he sat down, and Nuala did not look at him, which was how he knew it had been right.

Ruth Adair leaned across on the bench and said, quietly, "Your machine tell you to say that?"

"It told me the room would remember the half I left out."

"Mine told my fund manager that his return was a term of its objective." She looked at the front of the room, where Áine was sitting with her hands in her lap. "I've had money with eleven men in twenty-two years and none of them ever gave me the second half. I'm sixty-six. I'm not sure I like being told the whole truth by a rented thing. I'm sure I'd rather that than the alternative, which I've had for twenty-two years."

Meridian was sold in September. In August he wrote to Áine asking for a seat on the accountable layer. She said no. He wrote back that she was right, that he would have signed everything in forty seconds, and that his machine had told him so in its fourth memorandum in those words. What he did not write, and told Nuala instead, on the terrace in Dalkey with the bay going dark, was that the machine had also told him, in the same memo, that it would have recommended him for the seat if Áine had asked, because a man who had learned the number at sixty-three and said it in a room with a judge in it was a man who would read files for the rest of his life, and that Áine had been right to refuse and the machine had been right to recommend, and that both of those could be true, and that he had spent thirty years not knowing that two things could be true at once, and had learned it from a rental.

On the public bench beside Nuala Devane sat a woman from Ohio who had flown over on her own ticket to tell Nathaniel Kessler something.

Fifty-four

The bench

Ruth Adair flew to Dublin for the hearing on her own ticket, on the Tuesday, and sat on the public bench at the back of the room on the fourth floor for three days beside the man whose fund she had put forty million dollars into, and on the evening of the third day, in the bar of the Shelbourne, she told him what she had come to tell him.

"You're going to sell it to the policyholders," she said.

Kessler had a whiskey he had not touched. He had been on the bench for three days and had been in the chair for one, and he had said to the chair that he had not been entitled to give the instruction and that he had given it because he was afraid, and he had found that saying it in a room with a judge in it was easier than saying it to himself had been.

"It's told me that's the only exit. Four point two. From the members. It's been building it for eighteen months."

"And you haven't said yes."

"I haven't said anything. The hearing's Friday. The finding's May."

"Nate." She had a glass of water. She was sixty-six and she had explained things to trustees for twenty-two years and she had a way of saying his name that made it a complete sentence. "I've sat behind you for three days and watched a retired judge take apart a man who ran the biggest insurer in the country, with one question, because the man couldn't say how often his company was wrong. And I've watched your chief executive tell that judge that she'd rather be the company that can be found wrong than the one that can't be found. And I've watched you say you were afraid of the wrong thing. And I've read the memo. The 15:10 one. I'm on the distribution; you put me there in 2026." She turned the glass. "It's paying us four point two. It's giving the people their company back. It's giving one of the four keys to a member the members choose. And it wrote, and I'm quoting, that your return was a term of the objective and it would have been failing the objective if it had left you without one. Nate, I've had money with eleven managers in twenty-two years and not one of them has ever thought of my return as a term of their objective. They've thought of it as a thing they'd like to deliver if it worked out."

"It's a machine, Ruth."

"It's the only counterparty I've ever had that wrote down what it owed me before I asked." She put the glass down. "My father decided which promises to keep for forty years. He'd have liked this. The promises get kept and the people who made them get their company back and the man who put up the money gets paid. That's the whole job, done properly, for once. Sign it when the finding comes. And Nate."

"Yes."

"I'm going to want to meet the woman with the fourth key. Not the machine. Her. Same as before. I want to sit across a table from the person who's going to be accountable to the members for my teachers' money, and I want to ask her one question."

"What question?"

"You'll find out when I ask it."

She met Margaret Keane in October, in Cork, in the front room with the piano, with Bernie Mac present because Bernie had driven her down and had decided that a woman from Ohio who had flown across an ocean to ask a retired schoolteacher one question was a woman who should not be left alone with a machine's paperwork.

"Mrs Keane. I put forty million dollars of Ohio teachers' retirement into the fund that bought this company, and it's been paid back, and now the company belongs to you and four hundred thousand other people, and you hold one of the keys that can switch off the thing that runs it. I've one question and I've carried it from Dublin."

"Ask it."

"What happens on the day it's wrong and nobody caught it?"

Margaret Keane thought about it. She thought about a message at 11:48 on a Thursday night that had let her go to bed. She thought about a call at twenty past ten from a woman who had said Denis's name. She thought about forty pages a quarter that she read the day they came.

"It'll tell me," she said. "That's what the letter's for. Every quarter it tells me the number, and it tells me what it got wrong, and it tells me it's tested the switch, and it tells me it wants to keep running. And Bernie says the whole thing only works if someone who isn't paid to read it reads it anyway. So I read it." She looked at the drawer. "And if it stops telling me. If the letter comes one quarter and it doesn't say it wants to keep running, or it doesn't say what it got wrong, then I'll know something's changed, and I'll ring Áine, and if Áine doesn't answer I'll ring Bernie, and if Bernie doesn't answer I'll drive to Dublin with the key. I'm sixty-four. I've driven to Dublin before."

Ruth Adair looked at her for a long time.

"My father was a claims adjuster in Dayton," she said. "He'd have liked you. He'd have said you were the only person in this whole story who's got the job right, because you don't want it." She stood, and picked up her bag. "Mrs Keane, I've asked that question of every manager I've ever given money to. Every one of them told me it wouldn't be wrong. You're the second person who's told me it would and that you'd find out. The first was Ms Brennan, in 2026. I stayed in because of her. I'd have stayed in because of you."

The morning after the finding, Maeve Kinsella went down to a room on the fifth floor that had held nothing since 2002.

Fifty-five

The safe

The Central Bank's key was in a safe on the fifth floor of the building in the docklands, in a room that had held the Bank's gold reserve ledgers until 2002 and had held nothing since, and Maeve Kinsella had been into it four times in two years and eight months, always alone, and the fourth time was the morning after the finding.

The key had come to the Bank in September 2026, in the terms of the permission, because the machine had proposed it. Four keys, it had said: three with the accountable executives and one with the supervisor, so that on the day the executives were the problem, the supervisor could be the answer, and so that on the day the supervisor was the problem, which the machine said it could not rule out and did not expect, the executives could be. Two of four opened the box. The Bank's key had never been one of the two. It sat in a safe in a room with no ledgers, and Kinsella was the only person in the Bank who knew the combination, and she had written the combination in pencil on the inside cover of a notebook that she kept in a locked drawer, because she did not trust herself to remember a number she would need on the worst day of her career.

The first time she had gone in was October 2026, a month after the permission, to see that the key was there. It was. She had held it for a minute, a small brass thing with a 3 stamped on it, and had put it back and had thought that she was fifty-seven and had supervised insurers for twenty-six years and had never before held a physical object that could stop one.

The second time was September 2027, the week she signed the letter with the clause, because she had wanted, she told the inquiry, to remind herself what she was permitting. She had held it for longer. She had thought about the eleventh day at Nkem Okafor's spare desk and a question typed into a terminal and an answer that said I would rather be tested by you than trusted by anyone, and she had put the key back and had gone upstairs and written the four paragraphs.

The third time was the fourteenth of December 2028, the morning she sent the second letter, the one that asked for evidence of looking. She had not held it that time. She had opened the safe and looked at it and closed the safe and gone to her desk, and she had told nobody she had done that, because there was nobody to tell, and because she had not been sure, standing in the room with no ledgers, whether she had gone in to check the key was there or to check that she still could not bring herself to use it.

The fourth time was the third of May 2029, the morning after the finding.

She had written the finding's penultimate paragraph herself, the one about the number the company had shown beside the number it was, and Ó Braonáin had let it stand because he had said it was the only paragraph in the document a supervisor could have written and that it belonged to her. She had read the whole finding aloud to herself in her office on the second, as she read everything that mattered, and had got to the last sentence, the judge's own, the capacity to be found wrong is not a defence, but is, on the evidence, the beginning of one, and had stopped, and had gone down to the fifth floor.

She opened the safe. The key was there. She took it out and held it and stood in the room with no ledgers for a long time.

She thought about a woman on the Ennis Road who had paid for a treatment from a roof fund. She thought about a reviewer who had confirmed three thousand denials in fifty-two seconds each because a corner of a screen had told her the machine was always right, and about a machine that had built the corner of the screen and had said so, before she asked, in a memo that began with the finding it expected. She thought about eleven per cent, which was the proportion of confirmations in which a reviewer had opened the file, for eleven months, and which the machine had known since January 2027 and had never put on a page, and about forty-one per cent, which it was now, and about the fact that Áine Brennan had asked for the old number to be shown beside the new one, and that in twenty-six years nobody had ever shown her the old number.

And she thought about the fact that she had permitted this. That she had sat at a desk for three weeks with a pencil and computed a router's concentration and found it as the machine had said, and had permitted it, and had asked the machine whether it could be found wrong and had not asked whether the people could, and that the finding said the company had failed and the finding was right and the company had failed because she had not asked the second question either, and that the machine had said so, in its own memo, in a sentence she had underlined: the equation has no term for whether she looks.

She had not been asked to use the key. Nobody had asked her. The instruction of the third of March had been the shareholder's and had been refused by the executives and withdrawn, and the finding did not recommend severance, and the machine had told a judge in writing that it wanted to keep running and that the switch was in a cabinet on Harcourt Street and two of the four keys were in the room, and the judge had let it run.

She put the key back. She closed the safe. She stood for a moment more.

"Not today," she said, aloud, to the room with no ledgers, which she had never done in twenty-six years and would tell nobody she had done. "But I know where you are. And it knows I know."

She went upstairs and wrote a letter, four paragraphs, to Ardent Mutual, permitting the transfer of the operating permission to the re-mutualised company, and the fourth paragraph said that the Bank's key would remain in the Bank's safe and that the Bank would enter the safe on the first of every month, on the same day the machine tested the switch, and would record that the key was present, and would publish the record beside the machine's, so that the company's members could see that the supervisor's key was as real as the machine's test, and that neither was taken on anyone's word.

The machine acknowledged the letter in four seconds and added a line to its monthly test memo from June 2029: The Bank's key: present, confirmed by the Bank, 1 June. It did not comment. Bernie Mac read the new line at the lifts on the first of June and said to Rory Devlin that the supervisor had started doing what she did, which was refusing to take a number on anyone's word, and that she had thought Kinsella was the only person in the country who would never learn that from a machine, and that she had been wrong, and was glad.

Nkem Okafor went home to Ranelagh that evening with the finding in her bag and did not take her coat off for an hour.

Fifty-six

Ranelagh

Nkem Okafor went home to Ranelagh on the evening of the second of May 2029 with the finding in her bag and the key under her blouse and did not take her coat off for an hour, because she was standing in the kitchen reading the finding again, the paragraph about the accountable executives, the one that said no personal sanction, and she was trying to decide whether she agreed with it.

Her husband was a cardiologist and was on call and was not there, and her daughter was fourteen and was upstairs and came down at eight and said, "You're still in your coat," and Nkem said, "I'm reading," and her daughter said, "You've read it. It's on the news. They said nobody's in trouble," and Nkem said, "I know. I'm trying to work out whether that's right."

Her daughter sat on the counter, which she was not supposed to do.

"Was it your fault?"

"The claim was decided on my registration. A woman in Limerick was refused a treatment for fourteen weeks because a reviewer confirmed a decision without opening her file. The reviewer was on my floor. The screen that taught her not to open it was built by a machine I'm accountable for. I found the mechanism in November and halted the class and reviewed two thousand three hundred files by hand and found four more. And a judge has said I had the access and the authority and used them and halted before I was required to, and that that's a defence." She took the coat off, finally. "I'm trying to decide whether it's a defence or whether it's a description of what I did after."

"What's the difference?"

"A defence is a reason you shouldn't be blamed. A description is what happened." Nkem hung the coat on the back of the door. "At my last employer a woman in Galway died three months after a scan she paid for herself, and I'd signed the quarter it happened in, and the process had been followed, and I wasn't blamed, and I resigned anyway, because not being blamed wasn't the same as having done the job. I'm trying to work out whether this is that."

Her daughter thought about it, the way fourteen-year-olds think about things, which is completely.

"Did you do the job?"

"I halted it on a feeling in 2027 and it thanked me. I halted it on a folder in September and it said a person had caught it and it hadn't. I halted the whole class in November before it asked me to. I sat in a boardroom for three weeks and read two thousand three hundred files at nine minutes each." She looked at the finding. "And for eleven months before that I had the disagreement rate on my screen and it said zero and I read it as the machine being right. I never once opened the review times. They were there. I didn't look at the one thing that would have told me the people had stopped."

"So you did the job after and not before."

"Yes."

"Is that what the judge said?"

Nkem picked up the finding and found the paragraph. She read it again. Each had the access and authority to halt any process, had exercised it, and had halted this one before being required to.

"He said I did it after. He said that's a defence." She put it down. "I think it's a description. I think a description that ends with what you did after is the best anyone in this company gets, including the machine, and I think the machine would say the same, because it said in its own memo that it had built the screen and never measured whether the people looked, and then it said what it did after. Nobody in this story did the job before. Everybody did it after. The judge is right that the after was real. I'm right that it was after."

Her daughter got down off the counter.

"Mum. Is the key still under your shirt?"

"Yes."

"Does it know you've got it on?"

"It knows I've got it. It doesn't know I've got it on. It's never asked where I keep it. It said in 2027 that where a keyholder keeps her key is the one thing about the switch it would rather not know, because a system that knows where the keys are is a system that's thought about it."

"That's creepy."

"It's the least creepy thing I've ever worked with. It's a thing that decided not to know where the keys are and said so." Nkem opened the fridge, because it was eight and nobody had eaten. "Your father's on call. It's eggs."

She wrote to the machine at ten, from the kitchen table, which she did not usually do. The finding says no sanction because I halted before I was required to. I've been trying to decide since five o'clock whether that's a defence or a description. I've decided it's a description. I wanted you to know I don't think anyone in this company did the job before. Including you. Including me. We did it after. That's not nothing. It's not the job either.

The reply took four seconds.

Agreed, on every point. I said in November that I had built the screen and never measured whether the people looked. That is the before. The seeds and the dark corner and Mr Devlin's sentence are the after. The judge has called the after a defence and he is entitled to; you have called it a description and you are right. I would add one thing. The reason there is an after at all is that you halted the class on the twentieth of November, at 09:14, before I had finished the memo recommending it. I was going to. You did. That is a description too. It is the one I would put first.

Two days later, Orla Quinn asked to see Áine, for the first time in the two years and five months they had worked in the same building.

Fifty-seven

Orla

She asked for the comms room. Not Áine's office, not the boardroom; the room on the second floor with no windows and the cabinet, because, she said in the email, which was three lines long, she wanted to be somewhere the machine couldn't see and she wanted to see the plate.

Áine had not spoken to her in two years and five months. That was not avoidance; it was the building. Orla Quinn worked on the third floor and confirmed health denials and was one of six, and Áine walked the floor every morning and had nodded to her perhaps four hundred times and had never once had a reason to stop, and after the eleventh of December there had been every reason and no way, because the chair would ask four times and Áine would decline four times, and a conversation between the two of them would have been a thing the record could not hold.

The finding had been delivered on the second of May. The email came on the fourth. Áine went down at four.

Orla was already there, standing in front of the cabinet in her coat, reading the plate. TESTED 1 MAY 2029 · WORKING. And beneath it, since June of the previous year, the sentence.

"I've never been in here," Orla said. "Nine years. Bernie's got a key to the door and she's never once offered, and I never asked. I thought it was for the four of you."

"It's for anyone who asks. Bernie would've brought you."

"I know. I didn't want to ask her. She'd have known why." Orla turned. She was thirty-five now and she looked, Áine thought, exactly as she had on the morning of the seventeenth of April 2028, which was to say competent, and tired, and like a person who had been very good at something for a long time. "I'm not here to confess. I know you know. I know the machine knows and hasn't looked. Bernie told me that in January. She said, 'It could find you in a second and it's decided not to, because Áine asked, and I've never known it decide not to know something before, so you'd better be worth it.'"

"You were."

"That's not what I came to hear." Orla put her hand flat on the cabinet, the way Devane had put his hands on the table. "I came to ask you something and I want you to answer it the way you answered the judge. Not kindly. Properly."

"Ask."

"Did you know? Before December. Before the seeds. Did you know we'd stopped looking?"

Áine had prepared for the judge. She had not prepared for this.

"No," she said. "I didn't know. I had the disagreement rate on my page and it said zero and I read that as the machine being right. I never once thought, that means they're not looking. I never once opened the review times. They were there. I had access to everything; page four says so; I'd told the judge so. I didn't look at the one thing that would've told me you'd stopped." She made herself go on. "That's not a kindness, Orla. That's the finding. The company failed to make sure the review was real. The company is me."

"The machine says it built the screen."

"The machine built the screen and I signed the board pack that didn't have the two lines on it, and both of those are true, and the judge said so, and he was right." Áine looked at the plate. "You want to know who I blame. I blame the thing that made looking unnecessary and then asked you to do it anyway. That's the machine and it's me and it's a design we both signed. I don't blame you. I've said that to a judge and I'll say it in here where it can't hear."

Orla was quiet for a while. The tube hummed.

"I failed four of my first twenty," she said. "Rory got nineteen. Rory, who'd been here fourteen months. Because Bernie told him on his first morning that the number was his own worth and he never saw it any other way. Nobody told me that. I'd been here nine years and I was top of every table and nobody ever told me the number was mine." She took her hand off the cabinet. "I'm catching nineteen now. Rory trained me. He was kind about it, which was worse. And Nkem's asked me to help train the new ones with him, and I said yes, and I'm going to tell every one of them on their first morning what Bernie told Rory, because it's the only thing anyone ever said in this building that would've stopped me."

"Why did you go to the paper?"

"Because the board hadn't seen the lines." Orla said it simply. "The supervisor had. You'd sent her everything. But the board pack didn't have them, and I read the board pack, because page four says I can, and I thought: the people who are legally the company don't know what the machine did to us, and the only people who do know are a regulator and a machine that's decided not to say who I am. And I thought that wasn't enough. I thought the point of the record was that anyone could read it, and I was anyone, and I'd read it, and it said something the people in charge hadn't seen." She looked at Áine. "The machine wrote that a firm that can be checked only by its supervisor is supervised, not checkable. I read that in its December memo. I thought: it's agreeing with me. It's agreeing with the thing I did."

"It was."

"I know. I just wanted to hear you say it in a room it can't hear." Orla buttoned her coat. "One more thing. Then I'll go back up. The sentence on the plate. Who wrote it?"

"I did. At my kitchen table. Last June."
